Mod ℓ \ell multiplicities in certain U ⁡ ( 4 ) U(4) Shimura varieties
Jeffrey Manning

Abstract
. We use the Taylor–Wiles–Kisin patching method to investigate the multiplicities with which Hecke eigensystems appear in the mod- ℓ \ell cohomology of unitary Shimura sets, associated to central simple algebras of the form B = M 2 ​ ( D ) B=M_{2}(D) , for D D a nonsplit quaternion algebra over a C ​ M CM field. We follow a similar strategy to the one used in our prior work for Shimura curves, exploiting the natural self-duality in this setting. Our method requires a careful analysis of certain irreducible components of local deformation rings. We introduce and analyze a new local model for local deformation rings specific to the case of a banal prime, which is significantly better behaved than the standard local models for local deformation rings. Our main result is a “multiplicity 2 a 2^{a} result”, in the case where quaternion algebra D D ramifies only at banal primes, where a a is the number of places in the discriminant of D D which satisfy a certain Galois theoretic condition. We also prove an additional statement about the endomorphism ring of the cohomology of the Shimura set, which has an application to the study of congruence modules.



1.
Introduction

In their proof of Fermat’s Last Theorem, Taylor and Wiles
[
Wil95
,
TW95
]
introduced what is now known as the
Taylor–Wiles–Kisin patching method
. This method has since become one of the most powerful tools in the Langlands program.

In the modern formulation of this method (due to Kisin
[
Kis09
]
and others) one considers a ring
R_{\infty}=\widehat{\bigotimes}_{v}R^{\square}_{v}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{g}]]
,
where
v
runs over a finite set of primes and each
R_{v}^{\square}
is a local (framed) Galois deformation ring at
v
, and constructs a nicely behaved module
M_{\infty}
over
R_{\infty}
, called a
patched module
.

The module
M_{\infty}
is constructed by gluing together various cohomology groups on certain Shimura varieties, and hence its properties are closely related to those of the cohomology groups used in its construction. Determining the structure of
M_{\infty}
as an
R_{\infty}
-module thus has many applications to the study of automorphic forms and Shimura varieties, beyond simply proving automorphy lifting results.

Of particular interest to this paper, the dimension of the special fiber
M_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{\infty}}M_{\infty}
equals the multiplicity of a certain Hecke eigensystem appearing in the mod
\ell
cohomology of a Shimura variety. Determining this multiplicity has applications towards the mod
\ell
analogue of the classical Langlands correspondence (see for example
[
BDJ10
, Section 4]
).

While the ring
R_{\infty}
depends only on local Galois theoretic information at a finite set of primes,
M_{\infty}
is explicitly constructed as global object. From the construction, it is not at all obvious that the
R_{\infty}
-module structure of
M_{\infty}
does not depend global information, or even that it is independent of the (infinitely many) non-canonical choices made in its construction.

The surprising property of
M_{\infty}
, which is the key to many applications of the patching method is that, despite its construction
M_{\infty}
often behaves as if it is a purely local object, depending only on local Galois theoretic information at the same finite set of primes defining
R_{\infty}
. The recently formulated
categorical Langlands program

[
EGH22
]
turns this into a precise conjecture, predicting that
M_{\infty}
can be explicitly described in terms of certain local objects appearing in the categorical local Langlands correspondence.

In the special case when
R_{\infty}
is regular, Diamond
[
Dia97
]
and Fujiwara
[
Fuj06
]
proved this conjecture (prior to the formulation of the general conjecture) by using the Auslander–Buchsbaum formula to prove that
M_{\infty}
is free over
R_{\infty}
. However in many more general situations the categorical Langlands conjectures predict that
M_{\infty}
is
not
free.

In the case when
M_{\infty}
is associated to the cohomology of a Shimura curve or Shimura set over a totally real number field, prior work of the author in
[
Man21
]
presented a new method for determining the structure of
M_{\infty}
which can work in situations when
M_{\infty}
is not free. The method exploits the natural self duality of
M_{\infty}
, which comes from the existence of a Hecke equivariant perfect pairing on the cohomology groups, as well as a computation of the Weil class group
\Cl(R_{\infty})
of
R_{\infty}
.

The patching method has been extended to the case of Shimura sets associated to definite unitary groups of rank
n>2
in
[
CHT08
]
and
[
Tay08
]
. The method of
[
Man21
]
does not immediately generalize to this setting, as the “natural” perfect pairing on the relevant cohomology groups is no longer Hecke equivariant. Roughly, the reason for this is that any matrix
A\in\GL_{2}(\Bbbk)
is conjugate to its inverse transpose
{}^{t}A^{-1}
, up to a scalar, but the same is not true for matrices in
\GL_{n}(\Bbbk)
for
n>2
.

However the work of
[
CHT08
, Section 5.2]
shows that in certain cases, one can produce a Hecke equivariant perfect pairing in this context. Specifically if
G
is a definite unitary group associated to a central simple algebra
B
of rank
n
over a CM field
F
, and
X_{K}
(for
K\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
a sufficiently small compact open subgroup) is an associated Shimura set, then there will exist a Hecke equivariant perfect pairing on
H^{0}(X_{k},\mathbb{Z}_{\ell})
provided that
B^{\op}\cong B
, or equivalently that either
B\cong M_{n}(F)
or
B\cong M_{n/2}(D)
for
D
a quaternion algebra over
F
. In such cases it is thus possible to generalize the results of
[
Man21
]
to such settings, provided that one can compute
\Cl(R_{\infty})
.

The primary purpose of this paper is to compute
\Cl(R_{\infty})
, and hence generalize the main result of
[
Man21
]
, in what is arguably the simplest nontrivial case with
n>2
: the case where
B=M_{2}(D)
for a quaternion algebra over
F
, such that all primes at which
D
ramifies are
banal
. We believe that our techniques (particularly the methods of computing the class groups used in Section
10
and
11
) can be used to prove similar results much greater generally. We will pursue this in future work.

Our main result (stated using notation and terminology defined later) is:

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
F^{+}
be a totally real number field with
F/F^{+}
a quadratic CM extension. Let
\ell\geq 5
be a prime which does not ramify in
F
and such that all primes of
F^{+}
lying above
\ell
split in
F
. Let
E/\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
be a finite extension with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
and residue field
\mathbb{F}
.

Let
D
be a nonsplit quaternion algebra over
F
such that:

•

If
w
is a finite prime of
F
at which
D
ramifies, then
w
is split over
F^{+}
(that is, there is a prime
v
of
F^{+}
which splits in
F
as
v=ww^{c}
) and
D
also ramifies at
w^{c}
;

•

If
w
is a finite prime of
F
lying over
\ell
, then
D
splits at
\ell
.

Let
\Delta
be the set of finite places of
F^{+}
lying below a prime of
F
at which
D
does not split.

Let
G/F^{+}
be a unitary group associated to an involution of the second kind on
B:=M_{2}(D)
(see Section
6
for a precise definition) which is compact at all infinite places of
F^{+}
and quasisplit at all finite places
v\not\in\Delta
of
F^{+}
which split in
F
.

Let
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
be a compact open subgroup such that
K_{v}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
G(F^{+}_{v})
for
all
places
v
, and is hyperspecial whenever
v\not\in\Delta
.

Let
X_{K}
be the Shimura set associated to
K
, and let
M(K)=H^{0}(X_{K},\mathcal{O})
. Let
\mathbb{T}(K)
be the Hecke algebra acting on
M(K)
and let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}(K)
be a non-Eisenstein maximal ideal with
\mathbb{T}(K)/\mathfrak{m}=\mathbb{F}
. Let
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{F^{+}}\to\mathcal{G}_{4}(\mathbb{F})
be the associated (absolutely irreducible) Galois representation, and assume that

(1)

\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(G_{F(\zeta_{\ell})})\subseteq\GL_{4}(\mathbb{F})
is adequate;

(2)

The extension
F/F^{+}
is unramified at all finite places;

(3)

For each
v\in\Delta
,
v
is
banal
, in the sense that
v\nmid\ell
and
q_{v}^{i}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
for all
i=1,2,3,4
, where
q_{v}:=\Nm_{F^{+}/\mathbb{Q}}(v)

Then
\dim_{\mathbb{F}}M(K)/\mathfrak{m}M(K)=2^{a}
, where
a
is the number of places
v=ww^{c}\in\Delta
such that
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}}
is unramified with
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Frob_{w})\sim\overline{\lambda}\diag(q_{v}^{2},q_{v},q_{v},1)
for some
\overline{\lambda}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
. Moreover the map
R\to\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
is an isomorphism, where
R(=R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu})
is the global Galois deformation ring defined in Section
7
.

As explained above, the strategy for proving Theorem
1.1
is similar to that of
[
Man21
]
. Namely, we consider the patched module
M_{\infty}
associated to
M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
and show that this is a self dual
R_{\infty}^{B}:=R_{\Sigma}^{\loc,B}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{g}]]
-module (where
R_{\Sigma}^{\loc,B}
is a completed tensor product of certain local Galois deformation rings, as defined in Section
7
) of generic rank
1
, and hence must correspond to an element of
\Cl(R_{\infty}^{B})
satisfying
2M_{\infty}=\omega_{R_{\infty}^{B}}
.

At this point, if
\Cl(R_{\infty}^{B})
is
2
-torsion free, this is enough to determine
M_{\infty}
up to isomorphism, and in particular shows that
M_{\infty}
depends only on the ring
R_{\infty}^{B}
, and hence only on the local Galois theoretic information determining
R_{\infty}^{B}
, confirming the predictions of the categorical Langlands program. Moreover if one can explicitly compute the unique
M\in\Cl(R_{\infty}^{B})
satisfying
2M=\omega_{R_{\infty}^{B}}
, then one can explicitly determine the structure of
M_{\infty}
, at which point one can easily deduce results like Theorem
1.1
by simply computing
M_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{\infty}^{B}}M_{\infty}
.

For convenience in this paper (similarly to as in
[
Man21
]
) we instead work with
\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}:=R_{\infty}^{B}/(\varpi)
and thus only determine
\overline{M}_{\infty}:=M_{\infty}/\varpi M_{\infty}
up to isomorphism (see Theorem
8.1
), which is still sufficient for our main results. This is mainly to avoid having to prove a mixed characteristic version of Proposition
8.3
, and so it is likely that a slightly more careful analysis would be enough to determine
M_{\infty}
itself.

The conditions in Theorem
1.1
were mainly imposed in order facilitate this method, and to allow for the computation of
\Cl(R_{\infty}^{B})
. Condition (1) is the standard Taylor–Wiles condition from
[
Tho12
]
, and is needed to apply the patching method. Condition (2) is needed in order to apply the automorphic multiplicity one results of
[
Lab11
]
, in order to show that
M_{\infty}
has generic rank
1
.

The conditions that
K_{v}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
G(F_{v}^{+})
for all
v\not\in\Delta
, as well as the conditions on
\ell
, ensure that the relevant local deformation rings are formally smooth at all primes
v\not\in\Delta
and so

R_{\infty}^{B}=\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Delta}}R_{v}\right)[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]

for some
s\geq 0
and some rings
R_{v}
depending only on
D_{w}
,
K_{v}
and
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}}
(for
v=ww^{c}
in
F
). Moreover for all
v\in\Delta
, the condition that
K_{v}
is a maximal compact subgroup of
G(F^{+}_{v})
implies that
R_{v}
is the fixed type local deformation ring
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
from Section
3.2
. Thus it suffices to study the rings
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
, and in particular to compute their class groups.

As shown in
[
Sho16
,
Sho18
]
, the scheme
\mathcal{M}(4,q_{v})
parameterizing pairs of matrices
\Phi,\Sigma\subset\GL_{4}
satisfying
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q_{v}}
forms a local model for the unrestricted framed deformation ring
R^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}})
, and a certain irreducible component of
\mathcal{M}(4,q_{v})
forms a local model for
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
.

Unfortunately this local model can often be quite difficult to work with explicitly, as the equation
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q_{v}}
involves
32
variables (given by the entries of the two matrices) with
16
relations, and involves an arbitrary power of a
4\times 4
matrix. Moreover it can be difficult to precisely determine the additional equations needed to cut out the component modeling
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
.

The purpose of condition (3), namely that we only consider such rings at
banal
primes, is to restrict our attention to a special case where
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
is far more manageable. In Section
4
, we present a new local model,
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
for the ring
R^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}})
, which is specific to the banal (and unipotent) case, which is much simpler to work with. In particular, this local model involves significantly fewer than 32 variables, and involves only quadratic relations between these variables.

This model turns out to be simple enough to allow us to completely analyze all of the local deformation rings arising in the context of Theorem
1.1
. Specifically we show that
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
is formally smooth in all but two cases: when
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}}
is unramified and either
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Frob_{w})\sim\overline{\lambda}\diag(q_{v},q_{v},1,1)
or
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Frob_{w})\sim\overline{\lambda}\diag(q_{v}^{2},q_{v},q_{v},1)
for some
\overline{\lambda}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
. Hence if
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
is not formally smooth, then it is a power series ring over one of two specific rings corresponding to these two cases, called
\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
respectively in the main body of the paper.

Hence the proof of Theorem
1.1
reduces to a careful analysis of these two rings. We show that both of these rings are Cohen–Macaulay (which implies the final statement of Theorem
1.1
) and compute the class groups of the mod
\ell
reductions of these rings,
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
. We compute that
\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is Gorenstein with
\Cl(\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2}))=0
and
\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is non-Gorenstein, with
\Cl(\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2}))\cong\mathbb{Z}
. From this we deduce that
\Cl(\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{a}
(where
a
is as in the statement of Theorem
1.1
), which is particular is
2
-torsion free. This allows us to explicitly determine the structure of
\overline{M}_{\infty}
, and hence to prove Theorem
1.1
.

Additionally, our computations imply that the natural duality map
\overline{M}_{\infty}\otimes_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}\overline{M}_{\infty}\to\omega_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}
is surjective. As in
[
Man21
, Theorem 1.2]
, this implies the following additional result:

Theorem 1.2
.

In the setting of Theorem
1.1
, the map
\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\End_{\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}}(M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})
is an isomorphism.

As shown in
[
BKM21
, Theorem 3.12]
, this implies an important statement about the
congruence module
of
M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
, namely that the congruence modules of
M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
are equal.

While the main results of this paper are specific to the
\GL_{4}
case, and to the case where
B=M_{2}(D)
for
D
a quaternion algebra, for the purposes of future applications much of the paper is written in far greater generality. In particular the local models in the banal case are constructed for
\GL_{n}
for general
n
. We suspect that many parts of our argument can be generalized significantly. In particular, in the banal case, it should be possible to use our local model to compute
\Cl(\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})
in much greater generality. We shall return to this idea in future work.

Comparing Theorem
1.1
with other standard results in the literature (and with
[
Man21
, Theorem 1.1]
) one might expect the same result to hold with the condition that
K_{v}
is maximal for all
v\not\in\Delta
split in
F
replaced by a
minimal ramification
condition at all primes
v\not\in\Delta
which split in
F
. Indeed by
[
CHT08
, Lemma 2.4.19]
this would still imply that the relevant local deformation rings are formally smooth. We have primarily avoided doing this to avoid having to give a detailed treatment of types, and in particular the way in which they interact with self-duality (or with the generic rank
1
result from Proposition
6.2
), as this would take us too far afield for this current paper.

As a final note, the categorical Langlands conjectures predict that the main result of Theorem
1.1
should hold more generally. Specifically in Theorem
1.1
, rather than taking
B=M_{2}(D)
, instead let
B
be a division algebra over
F
of rank
4
(and still assume all of the other conditions, in particular that
K_{v}
is maximal compact for all
v
). In this case the patched module
M_{\infty}
will be a module over the ring:

R_{\infty}^{B}=\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Delta}}R_{v}\right)[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]

where
\Delta
is the set of all places where
B
does not split, and we have
R_{v}=R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{4})
if
B_{w}
is a division algebra and
R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{2,2})
if
B_{w}=M_{2}(D_{w})
for a quaternion algebra
D_{w}
. In this situation,
[
CHT08
, Section 5.2]
no longer implies that
M_{\infty}
is self-dual. However the categorical Langlands conjectures now predict that
M_{\infty}
can be decomposed as

M_{\infty}=\left(\boxtimes_{v\in\Delta}M_{v}\right)[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]

where each
M_{v}
is a module over
R_{v}
depending only on
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}}
and on
B_{w}
. The proof of Theorem
1.1
determines
M_{v}
(or rather
\overline{M}_{v}:=M_{v}/\varpi M_{v}
) for all
v
with
B_{w}=M_{2}(D_{w})
, and so to determine
M_{\infty}
one only needs to determines
M_{v}
for the
v\in\Delta
for which
B_{w}
is a division algebra.

In particular, if one restricts attention to the case when
R_{v}=R_{v}^{\square}(\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{w}}},\st_{4})
is formally smooth whenever
B_{w}
is a division algebra (which by Proposition
4.18
happens, in particular, whenever
v
is banal) one has
M_{v}=R_{v}
for all such
v
, and so
M_{\infty}
has the same form as in the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Hence the categorical Langlands conjectures imply that the results of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
should hold in this more general context, and moreover they suggest that
M_{\infty}
should indeed be self-dual in this case, even though this is not directly implied by the work of
[
CHT08
, Section 5.2]
.

However in order to prove these results by the method of this paper, we would need an unconditional proof that
M_{\infty}
is still self-dual in this case. We intend to return to this issue in a future joint paper with David Helm.

Outline of the paper

This paper is divided into three parts. Part
I
is concerned with the local aspects of the argument. Section
2
summarizes the relevant results from the local Langlands correspondence, while Section
3
summarizes the theory of local (framed) Galois deformation rings, and reviews the main properties of the local model
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
. Most of the results in these sections are fairly standard. Most of the new theoretical results are contained in Section
4
, which introduces and studies the new local models in the banal case.

Part
II
is concerned with the global aspects of the argument, and in particular the results needed for the patching method. Sections
5
,
6
and
7
cover, respectively, Global deformation rings, automorphic forms on unitary groups, and the main patching argument. All of the results in these sections are again standard. Section
8
then uses the method of
[
Man21
]
to compute
\overline{M}_{\infty}
and uses this to prove Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

Lastly, Part
III
is concerned with the explicit computations involving the rings
\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
. Section
9
recalls the main commutative algebra results needed for these computations, and the rings
\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
are studied in Sections
10
and
11
, respectively. These computations are somewhat involved, so for the convenience of the reader all of the results from these sections that are needed for our main results are summarized in Theorems
4.21
and
4.22
, and so the entirety of Part
III
may be skipped on a first reading.

Part I
Local Theory

2.
Types and the local Langlands Correspondence

In this section we will the properties of the local Langlands correspondence that we will need for the remainder of the paper. For the sake of simplicity we will not work in full generality, instead we will focus mainly on the case of unipotent representations, as this is all that will be needed for our results.

For this section, we will fix a prime
p
and let
L
be a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with residue field of order
q
. We will let
G_{L}=\Gal({\overline{L}}/L)
be the absolute Galois group of
L
,
I_{L}\unlhd G_{L}
be the inertia subgroup and
P_{L}\unlhd I_{L}
be the wild inertia subgroup. Let
W_{L}
be the Weil group of
L
.

Let
\varphi\in W_{L}
be a lift of arithmetic Frobenius and let
\sigma\in I_{L}/P_{L}
be a topological generator, so that
\varphi\sigma\varphi^{-1}=\sigma^{q}
in
W_{L}/P_{L}
, and moreover
W_{L}/P_{L}
is identified with the pro-finite completion of

\left\langle\varphi,\sigma\middle|\varphi\sigma\varphi^{-1}=\sigma^{q}\right\rangle=\mathbb{Z}\ltimes\mathbb{Z}\left[\frac{1}{p}\right].

2.1.
Inertial Types

Definition 2.1
.

We say that a
Weil–Deligne
representation of
L
(of dimension
n
) is a pair
(r,N)
where:

•

r:W_{L}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is a continuous representation;

•

N\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is a nilpotent matrix;

•

Nr(g)=r(g)N
for all
g\in I_{L}
;

•

r(\varphi)N=qNr(\varphi)
.

and we say that
(r,N)
is
Frobenius semisimple
if
r
is semisimple.

We say that an
inertial type
is an equivalence class (up to conjugation by elements of
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
) of pairs
\tau=(r_{\tau},N_{\tau})
where
r:I_{L}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is a continuous representation and
N_{\tau}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is nilpotent which extends to a Weil–Deligne representation
(r,N_{\tau})
.

We say a Weil–Deligne representation
(r,N)
has type
\tau=(r_{\tau},N_{\tau})
if
(r|_{I_{L}},N)
is
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{C})
-conjugate to
\tau
.

If
\rho:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}({\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell})
is a continuous Galois representation, we will use
\WD(\rho)
to denote the associated Weil–Deligne representation, and we will say that
\rho
has type
\tau
if
\WD(\rho)
does.

We will now construct the specific Weil–Deligne representations and inertial types we will work with in this paper. First define a character
\|\ \|:W_{L}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
by
\|g\|=1
for
g\in I_{L}
and
\|\varphi\|=q
. Let
n
be any positive integer and let
\chi:W_{L}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
be a character, define the
n
-dimensional Weil–Deligne representation
\Sp_{n}(\chi)=(\operatorname{sp}_{n,\chi},N_{n})
by

\displaystyle\operatorname{sp}_{n,\chi}(g)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\|g\|^{n-1}\chi(g)&&&\\
&\|g\|^{n-2}\chi(g)&&\\
&&\ddots&\\
&&&\chi(g)\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle N_{n}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&&&\\
&0&1&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&0&1\\
&&&&0\end{pmatrix}

which is clearly a Frobenius semisimple Weil–Deligne representation.

For any
k
-tuple of positive integers
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
and any characters
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}:W_{L}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
we define

\Sp_{\underline{n}}(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k})=\Sp_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k})=\Sp_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\Sp_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k}).

We say that the representation
\Sp_{\underline{n}}(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k})
is
generic
if there is no pair
(i,j)
with
\chi_{j}=\|\ \|^{m}\chi_{i}
for some
m
with
0\leq m\leq n_{i}
and
m+n_{j}>n_{i}
. One can also define generic representations more generally, but we will omit this as we will not need to consider any Weil–Deligne representations not in the form
\Sp_{\underline{n}}(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k})
.

We will say that a Weil–Deligne representation
(r,N)
is
unipotent
if it is Frobenius semisimple and
r|_{I_{L}}
is trivial (i.e.
r
is unramified), and note that the unipotent Weil–Deligne representations are exactly
\Sp_{\underline{n}}(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k})
for
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}
unramified.

For any
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
, consider the nilpotent matrix
N_{\underline{n}}=N_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}=\begin{pmatrix}N_{n_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&N_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}
where
N_{n_{i}}
is as in the definition of
\Sp_{n}(\chi)
above. Let
\tau_{\underline{n}}=\tau_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
denote the inertial type
(\mathbbm{1},N_{\underline{n}})
. Note that up to conjugation,
\tau_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
is independent of the ordering of the
k
-tuple
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
, and so we will often treat
\underline{n}
as an
unordered
partition of
n
.

By definition, if
\Sp_{\underline{n}}(\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k})
is a unipotent Weil–Deligne representation it is of type
\tau_{\underline{n}}
, and moreover it is easy to see that any Frobenius semisimple Weil–Deligne representation of type
\tau_{\underline{n}}
is unipotent. We thus call the types
\tau_{\underline{n}}

unipotent
types.

2.2.
The local Langlands correspondence

For any positive integer
n
, let
B_{n}\subseteq\GL_{n}(L)
denote the Borel subgroup of upper triangular matrices. For any
k
-tuple
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
with
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}
let
P_{\underline{n}}=P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
denote the parabolic subgroup

P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}=\begin{pmatrix}\GL_{n_{1}}(L)&*&\cdots&*\\
0&\GL_{n_{2}}(L)&\cdots&*\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\GL_{n_{k}}(L)\end{pmatrix}\subseteq\GL_{n}(L)

Let
M_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}=\GL_{n_{1}}\times\cdots\times\GL_{n_{k}}\subseteq P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
be the corresponding Levi subgroup, which we will treat as a quotient of
P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
.

For any admissible representations
\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{k}
of
\GL_{n_{1}}(L),\ldots,\GL_{n_{k}}(L)
, define an admissible representation

\pi_{1}\times\pi_{2}\times\cdots\times\pi_{k}=\nInd_{P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}}^{\GL_{n}(L)}(\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi_{k})

of
\GL_{n}(L)
, where
\pi_{1}\otimes\pi_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes\pi_{k}
denotes the representation of
P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
inflated from
M_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
, and
\nInd_{P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}}^{\GL_{n}(L)}
denotes the normalized induction (as defined in
[
HT01
]
).

For any character
\chi:L^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
and any integer
m
, we will let
\chi(m)
be the character
\|\ \|_{L}^{m}\otimes\chi
. Now we recall the following standard results (cf
[
Rod82
]
):

Proposition 2.2
.

For any character
\chi
and and integer
n
, the representation

\chi\times\chi(1)\times\cdots\times\chi(n-1)

of
\GL_{n}(L)
has a unique irreducible quotient, which we will denote
\St_{n}(\chi)
.

For any character
\chi
and any integer
n
, let
\Delta(\chi,n)
denote the set
\{\chi,\chi(1),\ldots,\chi(n-1)\}
. We say that
\Delta(\chi_{1},n_{1})
and
\Delta(\chi_{2},n_{2})
are
linked
if
\Delta(\chi_{1},n_{1})\not\subseteq\Delta(\chi_{2},n_{2})
,
\Delta(\chi_{2},n_{2})\not\subseteq\Delta(\chi_{1},n_{1})
and
\Delta(\chi_{1},n_{1})\cup\Delta(\chi_{2},n_{2})=\Delta(\chi^{\prime},n^{\prime})
for some
\chi^{\prime}
and
n^{\prime}
. We say that
\Delta(\chi_{1},n_{1})

precedes

\Delta(\chi_{2},n_{2})
if
\Delta(\chi_{1},n_{1})
and
\Delta(\chi_{2},n_{2})
are linked and
\Delta(\chi_{1},n_{1})\cup\Delta(\chi_{2},n_{2})=\Delta(\chi^{\prime},n^{\prime})
(i.e. if
\chi_{2}=\chi_{1}(m)
for some
0\leq m\leq n_{1}
).

Then we have

Proposition 2.3
.

For any
k
-tuple of integers
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
with
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=n
and any characters
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}
. If the
n_{i}
’s and
\chi_{i}
’s are ordered so that
\Delta(\chi_{i},n_{i})
does not precede
\Delta(\chi_{j},n_{j})
for any
i<j
then the representation

\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\St_{n_{2}}(\chi_{2})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k})

of
\GL_{n}(L)
has a unique irreducible quotient, which we will denote
Q(\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\St_{n_{2}}(\chi_{2})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k}))
. Moreover, if no two
\Delta(\chi_{i},n_{i})
’s are linked then the representation
\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\St_{n_{2}}(\chi_{2})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k})
is already irreducible, and so
Q(\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\St_{n_{2}}(\chi_{2})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k}))=\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\St_{n_{2}}(\chi_{2})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k})
.

Let
\rec_{L}
denote the local Langlands correspondence, constructed in
[
HT01
, Theorem A]
, normalized so that
\rec_{L}(\chi)=\chi\circ\Art_{L}^{-1}:W_{L}^{\ab}\to L^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for any character
\chi:L^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
. Then we have:

Proposition 2.4
.

For any
k
-tuple of integers
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
with
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=n
and any characters
\chi_{1},\ldots,\chi_{k}
. If the
n_{i}
’s and
\chi_{i}
’s are ordered so that
\Delta(\chi_{i},n_{i})
does not precede
\Delta(\chi_{j},n_{j})
for any
i<j
then

\rec_{L}(Q(\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k})))=\Sp_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}(\rec_{L}(\chi_{1}),\ldots,\rec_{L}(\chi_{k})).

We will say that
Q(\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\St_{n_{2}}(\chi_{2})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k}))
is
generic
if two
\Delta(\chi_{i},n_{i})
’s are linked (in which case
Q(\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k}))=\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k})
), and note that Proposition
2.4
implies that
\rec_{L}(Q(\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k})))
is generic if and only if
Q(\St_{n_{1}}(\chi_{1})\times\cdots\times\St_{n_{k}}(\chi_{k}))
is.

To avoid giving a detailed discussion of types we will simply say that an irreducible admissible representation
\pi
of
\GL_{n}(L)
has type
\st_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
if
\rec_{L}(\pi)
has inertial type
\tau_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
.

2.3.
The local Jacquet–Langlands correspondence

Now fix an integer
d|n
and let
D/L
be a division algebra of dimension
d^{2}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{D}
. Let
r=n/d
and consider the algebraic group
\GL_{r}(D)
over
L
, and note that this is an inner twist of
\GL_{n}(L)
.

Let
\Irr_{L}
and
\Irr_{D}
denote the set of isomorphism classes of irreducible unitary representations of
\GL_{n}(L)
and
\GL_{r}(D)
, respectively.

Badulescu
[
Bad07
]
constructs a map
\mathbf{LJ}:\Irr_{L}\to\Irr_{D}\cup\{0\}
(which is in general neither invective nor surjective). We will not need to use the general construction of this map. Rather we will simply recall the following special case:

Proposition 2.5
.

Take any
\Pi\in\Irr_{L}
and let
\pi=\mathbf{LJ}(\Pi)
, and assume that
\pi\in\Irr_{D}
(so that
\pi\neq 0
). Assume that
\Pi
is generic and that
\pi^{\GL_{r}(\mathcal{O}_{D})}\neq 0
. Then
\Pi
has type
\st_{d,d,\ldots,d}
.

Proof.

As
\Pi
is generic, by the Bernstein–Zelevinsky classification (cf.
[
Rod82
]
) there exists an ordered partition
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
of
n
corresponding to a standard parabolic
P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}\subseteq\GL_{n}(L)
with Levi
M_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}=\GL_{n_{1}}(L)\times\cdots\times\GL_{n_{k}}(L)
, and supercuspidal representations
\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{k}
of
\GL_{n_{1}}(L),\ldots,\GL_{n_{k}}(L)
such that
\Pi=\nInd_{P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}}^{GL_{n}(L)}(\sigma_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma_{k})
(and in particular, this representation is irreducible).

Now by the definition of
\mathbf{LJ}
(cf.
[
Bad08
, Section 2.7]
) we have
\mathbf{LJ}(\Pi)=0
unless
d|n_{i}
for all
i
. If
d|n_{i}
for all
i
, then
P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
corresponds to a standard parabolic
P^{\prime}_{n_{1}/d,\ldots,n_{k}/d}\subseteq\GL_{r}(D)
(defined analogously to
P_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
) with Levi
M^{\prime}_{n_{1}/d,\ldots,n_{k}/d}=\GL_{n_{1}/d}(D)\times\cdots\times\GL_{n_{k}/d}(D)
and one has

\pi=\mathbf{LJ}(\Pi)=\nInd^{\GL_{r}(D)}_{P^{\prime}_{n_{1}/d,\ldots,n_{k}/d}}(\sigma^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma^{\prime}_{k})

where each
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}
is a square integrable representation of
\GL_{n_{i}/d}(D)
for which
\mathbf{JL}(\sigma_{i}^{\prime})=\sigma_{i}
, where
\mathbf{JL}
is the usual Jacquet–Langlands map for square integrable representations.

But now the condition that
\pi^{\GL_{r}(\mathcal{O}_{D})}\neq 0
implies that
P^{\prime}_{n_{1}/d,\ldots,n_{k}/d}=P^{\prime}_{1,\ldots,1}
is the group of upper triangular matrices in
\GL_{r}(D)
(so
k=r
and
n_{i}=d
for all
i
) and that each for each
i
,
\sigma_{i}^{\prime}=\chi_{i}\circ\nu:D^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
for some unramified character
\chi_{i}:L^{\times}\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}
, where
\nu:D^{\times}\to L^{\times}
is the reduced norm.

But now one has

\sigma_{i}=\mathbf{JL}(\sigma_{i}^{\prime})=\mathbf{JL}(\chi_{i}\circ\nu)=\St_{n_{i}}(\chi_{i})=\St_{d}(\chi_{i})

for each
i
, and so (as
\Pi
is generic)

\Pi=\nInd_{P_{d,\ldots,d}}^{GL_{n}(L)}(\sigma_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\sigma_{k})=\St_{d}(\chi_{1})\times\cdots\times\St_{d}(\chi_{r})=Q(\St_{d}(\chi_{1})\times\cdots\times\St_{d}(\chi_{r})),

which implies that
\Pi
indeed has type
\st_{d,\ldots,d}
by Proposition
2.4
.
∎

3.
Local Galois Deformation Rings

In this section we will define the various local Galois deformation rings which we will consider in the rest of the paper, and review their relevant properties.

For the rest of the paper, we will fix a finite extension
E
of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, uniformizer
\varpi\in\mathcal{O}
and residue field
\mathcal{O}/\varpi=\mathbb{F}
.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
(resp.
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}^{\wedge}
) be the category of Artinian (resp. complete Noetherian) local
\mathcal{O}
-algebras with residue field
\mathbb{F}
. Consider the (framed) deformation functor
\mathcal{D}^{\square}(\overline{r}):\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}\to{\bf Set}
defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\square}(\overline{r})(A)

\displaystyle=\{r:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(A),\text{ continouos lift of }\overline{r}\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\big(M,r,(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})\big)\middle|\parbox{303.53377pt}{$M$ is a free rank $n$ $A$-module with a basis $(e_{1},\ldots,e_{n})$ and $r:G_{L}\to\Aut_{A}(M)$ such that the induced map $G\to\Aut_{A}(M)=\GL_{n}(A)\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F})$ is $\overline{r}$}\right\}_{/\sim}

It is well-known that this functor is
pro-representable
by some
R^{\square}(\overline{r})\in\mathcal{C}^{\wedge}_{\mathcal{O}}
, in the sense that
\mathcal{D}^{\square}(\overline{r})\equiv\Hom_{\mathcal{O}}(R^{\square}(\overline{r}),-)
. Furthermore,
\overline{r}
admits a universal lift
r^{\square}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(R^{\square}(\overline{r}))
.

For any continuous homomorphism,
x:R^{\square}(\overline{r})\to{\overline{E}}
, we obtain a Galois representation
r_{x}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}({\overline{E}})
lifting
\overline{r}
, from the composition
G_{L}\xrightarrow{r^{\square}}\GL_{L}(R^{\square}(\overline{r}))\xrightarrow{x}\GL_{L}({\overline{E}})
.

We call
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
the
framed deformation ring
of
\overline{r}
.

Most of our results will specifically be concerned with the local deformation rings occurring when
v\nmid\ell
(the “
\ell\neq p
” case).

In the case when
v|\ell
, we will only need to consider local deformation rings in the
Fontaine–Laffaille
range. Specifically, for each embedding
\tau:L\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
, fix an
n
-tuple of integers
\lambda_{\tau}=(\lambda_{\tau,1},\ldots,\lambda_{\tau,n})
for which
\lambda_{\tau,1}\geq\lambda_{\tau,2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{\tau,n}
. Let
R^{\square,\crys,\{\lambda_{\tau}\}}(\overline{r})
be the Zariski closure of the set of points
x\in(\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r}))({\overline{E}})
for which
r_{x}
is crystalline with Hodge–Tate weights
\HT_{\tau}(r_{x})=\{\lambda_{\tau,1},\lambda_{\tau,2},\ldots,\lambda_{\tau,n}\}
for each
\tau
. We will refrain from giving a precise definition of the terms involved here, as it is not relevant to our discussion. We will refer the reader to
[
CHT08
]
or
[
BLGGT14
]
for more details, and use only the following result from
[
CHT08
, Section 2.4]
:

Proposition 3.1
.

If
L/\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
is unramified and

\lambda_{\tau,1}>\lambda_{\tau,2}>\cdots>\lambda_{\tau,n}\geq\lambda_{\tau,1}-(\ell-2)

for all
\tau
, then
R^{\square,\crys,\{\lambda_{\tau}\}}(\overline{r})\cong\mathcal{O}[[X_{1},\ldots,X_{n^{2}+[F_{v}:\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}]n(n-1)/2}]]
.

We will say that
\lambda_{\tau}=(\lambda_{\tau,1},\ldots,\lambda_{\tau,n})
is
in the Fontaine–Laffaille range
if it satisfies the inequality from this proposition.

3.1.
Local Deformation Rings for
v\nmid\ell

From now on assume that
v\nmid\ell
. By enlarging
\mathcal{O}
if necessary, we will assume that
\mathbb{F}
contains all
q^{n}-1
st roots of unity.

Let
I_{L}\to I_{L}/\widetilde{P}_{L}
be the maximal pro-
\ell
quotient of
I_{L}
. We will restrict to the case when
\overline{r}(\widetilde{P}_{L})=1
(it is well known that the general case of computations of local deformation rings in the
\ell\neq p
case may be reduced to this case, see for instance
[
Sho18
, Section 2]
).

Now for any
A\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}^{\wedge}
,
\ker(\GL_{n}(A)\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}))
is pro-
\ell
, and so any lift
r:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(A)
also satisfies
r(\widetilde{P}_{L})=1
. As
G_{L}/\widetilde{P}_{L}
is topologically generated by
\varphi
and
\sigma
, it follows that any lift
r:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(A)
is determined by
r(\varphi),r(\sigma)\in\GL_{n}(A)
.

Now as in
[
Sho18
, Section 2]
for any ring
R
, let
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{R}
denote the moduli space of pairs
(\Phi,\Sigma)\in\GL_{n,R}\times_{\Spec R}\GL_{n,R}
satisfying
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q}
. Write
\mathcal{M}(n,q)=\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}

We will let
x_{\overline{r}}=(\overline{r}(\varphi),\overline{r}(\sigma))
denote the
\mathbb{F}
-point of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
corresponding to
\overline{r}
. Then
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
is canonically isomorphic to the complete local ring of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
at the point
x_{\overline{r}}
and the universal lift
r^{\square}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(R^{\square}(\overline{r}))
is given by
r^{\square}(\varphi)=\Phi
and
r^{\square}(\sigma)=\Sigma
.

Now
[
Sho18
, Theorem 2.5]
gives:

Proposition 3.2
.

\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
is a complete intersection, reduced and flat over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
n^{2}
, and hence
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
is as well.

Moreover the proof of
[
Sho18
, Theorem 2.5]
gives:

Lemma 3.3
.

The characteristic polynomial
\chi_{\Sigma}(t)
of
\Sigma
is constant on each connected component of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{E}
.

Proof.

As
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
, and hence
\mathcal{M}(N,q)_{E}
, is reduced it is enough to check this on
{\overline{E}}
points. As explained in
[
Sho18
, Theorem 2.5]
, for any
x=(\Phi_{x},\Sigma_{x})\in\mathcal{M}(n,q)({\overline{E}})
the relation
\Phi_{x}\Sigma_{x}\Phi_{x}^{-1}=\Sigma_{x}^{q}
implies that all of the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{x}
are
q^{n}-1
st roots of unity. Hence there are only finitely many possible polynomials
\chi_{\Sigma_{x}}(t)\in{\overline{E}}[t]
for
x\in\mathcal{M}(n,q)({\overline{E}})
. If these polynomials are denoted
f_{1},\ldots,f_{N}
, then
\{V(\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=f_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
is a finite cover of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)({\overline{E}})
, and hence of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{E}
, by
disjoint
closed subsets, and so each
V(\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=f_{i})\subseteq\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{E}
must be a union of connected components of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{E}
.
∎

3.2.
Fixed type deformation rings

In order to prove our main results, we will also need to consider various deformation rings with
fixed type
. We will again restrict to the case when
v\nmid\ell
.

Let
\tau
be any inertial type defined over
E
. For any
x\in(\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r}))({\overline{E}})
, we will say that
x
has type
\tau
if
r_{x}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}({\overline{E}})
does. We will let
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau)\subseteq\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r})
be the Zariski closure of the set of
{\overline{E}}
points with type
\tau
. Explicitly this means that
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau)
is the minimal reduced
\ell
-torsion free quotient of
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
with the property that any
{\overline{E}}
point
x:R^{\square}(\overline{r})\to{\overline{E}}
with type
\tau
factors through
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau)
.

For any unipotent inertial type
\tau_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}}
, we will also denote
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}})=R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau_{n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}})
.

We say that an
{\overline{E}}
point
x\in(\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r}))({\overline{E}})
is
non-degenerate
if
\WD(r_{x})
is generic. Now
[
Sho18
, Proposition 4.6]
gives

Proposition 3.4
.

For any any inertial type
\tau
defined over
E
:

(1)

\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau)
is a union of irreducible components of
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r})
, and hence is flat and equidimensional of dimension
n^{2}
over
\mathcal{O}
;

(2)

If
x
is a non-degenerate
{\overline{E}}
point of
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r})
, then
x
lies on a unique irreducible component of
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r})
and
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r})[1/\ell]
is formally smooth at
x
;

(3)

The non degenerate
{\overline{E}}
points are Zariski dense in
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r})
;

(4)

If
x
is a non-degenerate
{\overline{E}}
point of
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau)
then
x
has type
\tau
.

3.3.
Unipotent deformation rings

The exponent of
q
in the relation
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q}
can make explicit computations somewhat difficult, since it involves arbitrary powers of an
n\times n
matrix, so we will need to find a simpler description of
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
in order to carry out the computations in this paper.

Given any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
A
and any
n\times n
matrix
X\in M_{n}(A)
, we will let
\chi_{X}(t)=\det(tI-X)
denote the characteristic polynomial of
X
. We say that a matrix
X\in\GL_{n}(A)
is
strongly unipotent
if the characteristic polynomial of
X
is
(t-1)^{n}
, and we say that
X
is
unipotent
if
(X-I)^{n}=0
. Similarly we will say that a matrix
N\in M_{n}(A)
is
strongly nilpotent
if its characteristic polynomial is
t^{n}
, and
nilpotent
if
N^{n}=0
. Note that strongly unipotent matrices are unipotent and strongly nilpotent matrices are nilpotent, and in both cases the definitions are equivalent if
A
is a field.

Now for any unipotent
X\in\GL_{n}(A)
and any nilpotent
N\in M_{n}(A)
we will define

\displaystyle\log X

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(-1)^{k-1}\frac{(X-I)^{k}}{k}=(X-I)-\frac{(X-I)^{2}}{2}+\cdots\pm\frac{(X-I)^{n}}{n}\in M_{n}(A)

\displaystyle\exp N

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{n}\frac{N^{k}}{k!}=I+N+\frac{N^{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{N^{n}}{n!}\in\GL_{n}(A)

and note that these are well defined, as we have assumed that
\ell>n
, and so
k
and
k!
are units in any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
A
for all
k=1,\ldots,n
. One can easily check that
\log X
is strongly nilpotent, that
\exp N
is unipotent, and that
\log
and
\exp
are inverses, and moreover that Moreover, that
\log X^{m}=m\log X
for all
m\geq 0
.

We say that a Galois representation
r:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(A)
is
unipotent
if
r(g)
is unipotent for all
g\in I_{L}
. If
r(\widetilde{P}_{L})=1
, this is equivalent to simply requiring that
r(\sigma)
is unipotent. From now on assume that
\overline{r}
is unipotent.

Let
R^{\unip}(\overline{r})
be the minimal, reduced
\ell
-torsion free quotient of
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
with the property that for any
x\in(\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r}))({\overline{E}})
, if
r_{x}
is unipotent, then
x
factors through
R^{\unip}(\overline{r})
. Notice that for any
x
, that
r_{x}
is unipotent if and only if
\WD(r_{x})
has a unipotent inertial type. It follows from Proposition
3.4
that
\Spec R^{\unip}(\overline{r})
is the scheme theoretic union of all of the
\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau)
, as
\tau
ranges over all unipotent inertial types. In particular, it is flat and equidimensional of relative dimension
n^{2}
over
\mathcal{O}
.

Let
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)=\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
be the Zariski closure of the set of points
(\Phi,\Sigma)\in\mathcal{M}(n,q)({\overline{E}})
for which
\Sigma
is strongly unipotent, and let
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{A}=\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)\times_{\mathcal{O}}\Spec A
for any
A
. Then Lemma
3.3
implies that
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{E}
is a union of connected components of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{E}
, and so (as
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
is the closure of
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{E}
in
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
)
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
is a union of irreducible components of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
. In particular,
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
is flat and equidimensional over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
n^{2}
.

From our definitions, it follows that
R^{\unip}(\overline{r})
is the complete local ring of
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point
(\overline{r}(\varphi),\overline{r}(\sigma))
.

Our goal is to find a simpler description of the space
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
. Note that if
\Sigma
is unipotent, then the relation
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q}
is equivalent to
\Phi(\log\Sigma)\Phi^{-1}=q(\log\Sigma)
. So now for any ring
R
, define
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{R}
to be the moduli space of all pairs of matrices
(\Phi,N)\in\GL_{n,R}\times_{\Spec R}M_{n,R}
satisfying
\Phi N\Phi^{-1}=qN
, and write
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Notice that in general,
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)
is not flat over
\mathcal{O}
. Indeed consider the case where
q\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
(but
q>1
). Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
has dimension
n^{2}
by the work below, but
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathbb{F}}
is the variety parameterizing pairs
(\Phi,N)\in\GL_{n,\mathbb{F}}\times_{\mathbb{F}}M_{n,\mathbb{F}}
satisfying
\Phi N=N\Phi
, which is known to have dimension
n^{2}+n
.

For any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
A
, define closed subschemes
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{A}^{\circ}=V(\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=(t-1)^{n})\subseteq\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{A}
and
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{A}^{\circ}=V(\chi_{N}(t)=t^{n})\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{A}
. That is,
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{A}^{\circ}
(resp.
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{A}^{\circ}
) is the maximal subscheme on which
\Sigma
is strongly unipotent (resp.
N
is strongly nilpotent). Then we have an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{A}:\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{A}^{\circ}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{A}^{\circ}
defined by
\mathcal{L}_{A}(\Phi,\Sigma)=(\Phi,\log\Sigma)
, with inverse given by
(\Phi,N)\mapsto(\Phi,\exp N)
.

Define
\mathcal{N}(n,q)=\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
to be the maximal reduced
\mathcal{O}
-flat subscheme and let
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{A}=\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}\times_{\mathcal{O}}\Spec A
for any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
A
. Note that
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
is the Zariski closure of the set of
{\overline{E}}
-points of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
, and hence
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
is the underlying reduced subscheme of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
.

We claim the following:

Proposition 3.5
.

There is an isomorphism
\mathcal{L}:\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}(n,q)
given by
(\Phi,\Sigma)\mapsto(\Phi,\log\Sigma)
. In particular,
R^{\unip}(\overline{r})
is isomorphic to the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point
(\overline{r}(\varphi),\log\overline{r}(\sigma))
, and the universal lift
r^{\unip}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(R^{\unip}(\overline{r}))
is given by
r^{\unip}(\varphi)=\Phi
and
r^{\unip}(\sigma)=\exp N
.

By definition,
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
is the Zariski closure of the set of
{\overline{E}}
points of
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}
, and so is the maximal reduced
\mathcal{O}
-flat subscheme of
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}
. The proof of Proposition
3.5
will thus hinge on the observation that
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{E}=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
.

In fact, we shall prove something slightly stronger than this, which will be useful for our discussion of the banal case below.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field. We will say that
q
is
banal
in
\Bbbk
if
q\neq 0
and
q^{i}\neq 1
in
\Bbbk
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. Since
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, this depends only on
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk
, and as
q>1
,
q
is automatically banal in
\Bbbk
if
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk=0
(so in particular,
q
is banal in
E
).

Lemma 3.6
.

If
\Bbbk
is a field which is an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, and
q
is banal in
\Bbbk
, then
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\Bbbk}=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
.

Proof.

By definition, we must prove that
\chi_{N}(t)=t^{n}
, where we view
N
as an
n\times n
matrix in
M_{n}(\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}))
. Let
\chi_{N}(t)=t^{n}+\gamma_{n-1}t^{n-1}+\cdots+\gamma_{1}t+\gamma_{0}
for
\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{n-1}\in\Gamma(\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\Bbbk})
. Since
\Phi N\Phi^{-1}=qN
on
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{E}
we get

\displaystyle\chi_{N}(t)

\displaystyle=\det(tI-N)=\det(tI-\Phi N\Phi^{-1})=\det(tI-qN)

\displaystyle=t^{n}+q\gamma_{n-1}t^{n-1}+\cdots+q^{n-1}\gamma_{1}+q^{n}\gamma_{0}

and so
\gamma_{k}=q^{n-k}\gamma_{k}
for all
k=0,\ldots,n-1
. Now as
\Gamma(\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\Bbbk})
is an
\Bbbk
-algebra, and
q
is banal in
\Bbbk
, it follows
\gamma_{k}=0
for all
k=0,\ldots,n-1
, and so
\chi_{N}(t)=t^{n}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

We have
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{E}=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
. Now as
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{E}=\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}\times_{\mathcal{O}}\Spec E
, it follows that
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}({\overline{E}})=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}({\overline{E}})
, and so the Zariski closure of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}({\overline{E}})
in
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}
is equal to the Zariski closure of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}({\overline{E}})
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
, which is
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
by definition. Hence
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
is the maximal reduced
\mathcal{O}
-flat subscheme of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}
. The isomorphism
\mathcal{L}_{\mathcal{O}}:\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}^{\circ}
thus restricts to the desired isomorphism
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{N}(n,q)
. The rest of Proposition
3.5
follows automatically from this.
∎

We now claim the following:

Proposition 3.7
.

If
\Bbbk
is a field which is an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, and
q
is banal in
\Bbbk
, then
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is a reduced complete intersection of dimension
n^{2}
over
\Bbbk
.

In particular,
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}=\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
.

Proof.

Note that as
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
is the underlying reduced subscheme of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
, the last claim follows from the fact that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
is reduced (as
q
is banal in
E
).

Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is the open subscheme
\GL_{n,\Bbbk}\times_{\Bbbk}M_{n,\Bbbk}\subseteq\mathbb{A}^{2n^{2}}_{\Bbbk}
by
n^{2}
relations (given by the matrix equation
\Phi N=qN\Phi
), to prove that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is a complete intersection of dimension
n^{2}
, it is enough to prove that
\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}\leq n^{2}
.

Now define a map
\pi:\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}\to M_{n,\Bbbk}
by
(\Phi,N)\mapsto N
. By Lemma
3.6
, for any
(\Phi,N)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
,
N\in M_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
is nilpotent. Now note that
\pi
is clearly equivariant for the natural conjugation action of
\GL_{n,\Bbbk}
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
and
M_{n,\Bbbk}
.

Now the set of conjugacy classes of nilpotent matrices in
M_{n,\Bbbk}
is in bijection is the set of unordered partitions of
n
(by sending a nilpotent matrix to the collection of sizes of its Jordan blocks). For any unordered partition
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
, let
C_{\underline{n}}\subseteq M_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
be the conjugacy class of nilpotent matrices corresponding to
\underline{n}
, and let
\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}=\pi^{-1}(C_{\underline{n}})\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
. Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})=\bigsqcup_{\underline{n}}\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}
.

Now for any integer
d\geq 1
, consider the matrices

\displaystyle N_{d}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&&&\\
&0&1&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&0&1\\
&&&&0\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{d}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}q^{d-1}&&&&\\
&q^{d-2}&&&\\
&&\ddots&&\\
&&&q&\\
&&&&1\end{pmatrix}

in
M_{d}(\Bbbk)
, and note that
N_{d}
is nilpotent and
\Phi_{d}N_{d}=qN_{d}\Phi_{d}
. For any partition
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
of
n
, define the matrices

\displaystyle N_{\underline{n}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}N_{n_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&N_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle\Phi_{\underline{n}}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi_{n_{1}}&&\\
&\ddots&\\
&&\Phi_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}

in
M_{n}(\Bbbk)
. Then we again have
\Phi_{\underline{n}}N_{\underline{n}}=qN_{\underline{n}}\Phi_{\underline{n}}
so
(\Phi_{\underline{n}},N_{\underline{n}})\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}(\Bbbk)
. Moreover by definition we have
N_{\underline{n}}\in C_{\underline{n}}
and
(\Phi_{\underline{n}},N_{\underline{n}})\in\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}
for all
\underline{n}
. In particular, each
\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}
is nonempty.

In fact, we have the following:

Lemma 3.8
.

For each partition
\underline{n}
of
n
,
\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
is irreducible.

Proof.

Consider the fiber
\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}
. By the above,
(\Phi_{\underline{n}},N_{\underline{n}})\in\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
, so
\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
is nonempty. Now by the definition of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
,
\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
is the variety of
\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
cut out by the equation
\Phi N_{\underline{n}}=qN_{\underline{n}}\Phi
. As this equation is linear in the entries of
\Phi
, and
\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
is a Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{n^{2}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
, it follows that
\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
is isomorphic to a (nonempty) Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{d_{\underline{n}}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
for some integer
d_{\underline{n}}\leq n^{2}
. In particular,
\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
is irreducible.

Now under the conjugation actions of
\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
on
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
and
M_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
,
C_{\underline{n}}
is generated by
N_{\underline{n}}
, and hence
\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}=\pi^{-1}(C_{n})
is generated by
\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
, so there is a Zariski continuous surjection
\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\times\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}
. Since
\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
and
\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
are irreducible
{\overline{\Bbbk}}
-varieties, so is
\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\times\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
, and hence
\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}
is indeed irreducible.
∎

It follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})=\bigcup_{\underline{n}}\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{n}}
, and each
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{n}}
is an irreducible closed subvariety of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
. Hence for any irreducible component
\mathcal{Z}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
we have
\mathcal{Z}({\overline{\Bbbk}})=\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{n}}
for some
\underline{n}
. In particular, each irreducible component of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
contains the
{\overline{\Bbbk}}
-point
(\Phi_{\underline{n}},N_{\underline{n}})
for some
\underline{n}
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}\subseteq\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk})
be the maximal ideal of
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk})
corresponding to the point
\Bbbk
-point
(\Phi_{\underline{n}},N_{\underline{n}})
. We claim the following:

Lemma 3.9
.

For each
\underline{n}
,
\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}/\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}^{2}=n^{2}
.

Proof.

Write
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
. Let
X=(x_{ij}):=\Phi-\Phi_{\underline{n}}
and
A=(a_{ij}):=N-N_{\underline{n}}
. Then by definition the local ring
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk})_{\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}}
is isomorphic to the polynomial ring

\displaystyle\frac{\Bbbk[\{x_{ij},a_{ij}\}]_{(x_{ij},a_{ij})}}{(\Phi N-qN\Phi)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Bbbk[\{x_{ij},a_{ij}\}]_{(x_{ij},a_{ij})}}{\left((\Phi_{\underline{n}}+X)(N_{\underline{n}}+A)-q(N_{\underline{n}}+A)(\Phi_{\underline{n}}+X)\right)}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Bbbk[\{x_{ij},a_{ij}\}]_{(x_{ij},a_{ij})}}{\left((\Phi_{\underline{n}}A-qA\Phi_{\underline{n}})+(XN_{\underline{n}}-qN_{\underline{n}}X)+(XA-qAX)\right)}.

As
XA-qAX\equiv 0\pmod{\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}^{2}}
it follows that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}/\mathfrak{p}^{2}_{\underline{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(x_{ij},a_{ij})_{ij}/(x_{ij},a_{ij})_{ij}^{2}}{\left((\Phi_{\underline{n}}A-qA\Phi_{\underline{n}})+(XN_{\underline{n}}-qN_{\underline{n}}X)\right)}=\frac{\bigoplus_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Bbbk x_{ij}\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq i,j\leq n}\Bbbk a_{ij}}{\left((\Phi_{\underline{n}}A-qA\Phi_{\underline{n}})+(XN_{\underline{n}}-qN_{\underline{n}}X)\right)}.

Now note the following:

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\Bbbk
be any field and assume that
q
is banal in
\Bbbk
. Take integers
a,b\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and consider a
\Bbbk
-vector space
W
of dimension
2ab
with basis denoted by
\{y_{ij},z_{ij}\}_{1\leq i\leq a,1\leq j\leq b}
. Consider the two
a\times b
matrices
Y=(y_{ij})
and
Z=(z_{ij})
with entries in
W
, and define

C=(c_{ij}):=(\Phi_{a}Y-qY\Phi_{b})+(ZN_{b}-qN_{a}Z),

interpreted as an
a\times b
matrix with entries in
W
. Define
\Span(C):=\Span\{c_{ij}\}_{ij}\subseteq W
to be the
\Bbbk
-span of the entries of
C
. Let
\mathcal{W}_{a,b}:=W/\Span(C)
.

Then
\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{W}_{a,b}=ab
.

Proof.

Since
C
has
ab
entries, we clearly have
\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathcal{W}_{a,b}\geq\dim_{\Bbbk}W-ab=ab
. It will thus suffice to show that
\mathcal{W}_{a,b}
is spanned by a set of
ab
vectors. If
a\leq b
, define

\mathcal{B}=\{y_{i,b-a+1+i}|i=1,\ldots,a-1\}\cup\{z_{ab}\}\cup\{z_{ij}|1\leq i\leq a,1\leq j\leq b,i-j\neq a-b\}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{a,b}

and if
a>b
define

\mathcal{B}=\{y_{j+a-b-1,j}|j=1,\ldots,b\}\cup\{z_{ij}|1\leq i\leq a,1\leq j\leq b,i-j\neq a-b\}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{a,b}

and note that in either case
|\mathcal{B}|=ab
. We claim that
\Span(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{W}_{a,b}
.

For ease of notation, set
y_{ij}=z_{ij}=0
whenever
(i,j)\not\in\{1,\ldots,a\}\times\{1,\ldots,b\}
. Now,

\displaystyle\Phi_{a}Y-qY\Phi_{b}

\displaystyle=\Phi_{a}(y_{ij})_{ij}-q\Phi_{b}(y_{ij})_{ij}=(q^{a+1-i}y_{ij})_{ij}-q(q^{b+1-j}y_{ij})_{ij}=((q^{a+1-i}-q^{b+2-j})y_{ij})_{ij}

and

\displaystyle ZN_{b}-qN_{a}Z

\displaystyle=(z_{ij})_{ij}N_{b}-qN_{a}(z_{ij})_{ij}=(z_{i,j-1})_{ij}-qN_{b}(z_{i+1,j})_{ij}=(z_{i,j-1}-qz_{i+1,j})_{ij}

and so

c_{ij}=(q^{a+1-i}-q^{b+2-j})y_{ij}+(z_{i,j-1}-qz_{i+1,j})

for all
(i,j)\in\{1,\ldots,a\}\times\{1,\ldots,b\}
. In particular, if
(i,j)\in\{1,\ldots,a\}\times\{1,\ldots,b\}
and
i-j=a-b-1
then
q^{a+1-i}=q^{b+2-j}
and so
c_{ij}=z_{i,j-1}-qz_{i+1,j}
giving
z_{i,j-1}=qz_{i+1,j}
in
\mathcal{W}_{a,b}
.

If
a\leq b
the relations
c_{i,b-a+1+i}
for
i=1,\ldots,a-1
give
z_{i,b-a+i}=qz_{i+1,b-a+i+1}
for
i=1,\ldots,a-1
, and so

z_{i,b-a+i}=q^{b-i}z_{ab}\in\Span(\mathcal{B})

for
i=1,\ldots,a
by induction.

If
a>b
the relations
c_{j+a-b-1,j}
for
j=1,\ldots,b
give
z_{j+a-b-1,j-1}=qz_{j+a-b,j}
for
j=1,\ldots,b
and so

z_{j+a-b,j}=q^{-j}z_{a-b,0}=0\in\Span(\mathcal{B})

for all
j=1,\ldots,b
.

So in either case,
z_{ij}\in\Span(\mathcal{B})
whenever
i-j=a-b
, and so
z_{ij}\in\Span(\mathcal{B})
for all
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
.

So now take any
(i,j)\in\{1,\ldots,a\}\times\{1,\ldots,b\}
. We claim that
y_{ij}\in\Span(\mathcal{B})
. If
i-j=a-b-1
, this is by definition. Otherwise, as
a,b\leq n
,
a+1-i,b+1-j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and so
|(a+1-i)-(b+2-j)|\leq n
, giving that
q^{a+1-i}\neq q^{b+2-j}
(as
q
is banal in
\Bbbk
) and so the relation
c_{ij}
gives

(q^{a+1-i}-q^{b+2-j})y_{ij}+(z_{i,j-1}-qz_{i+1,j})=0

and so

y_{ij}=-\frac{z_{i,j-1}-qz_{i+1,j}}{q^{a+1-i}-q^{b+2-j}}\in\Span(\{z_{ij}\}_{ij})\subseteq\Span(\mathcal{B}).

So indeed,
y_{ij},z_{ij}\in\Span(\mathcal{B})
for all
i
and
j
, giving
\Span(\mathcal{B})=\mathcal{W}_{a,b}
and so
\dim_{E}\mathcal{W}_{a.b}=ab
.
∎

So now in the vector space
\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}/\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}^{2}
, write
A
and
X
as block matrices

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A_{11}&\cdots&A_{1k}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
A_{k1}&\cdots&A_{kk}\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}X_{11}&\cdots&X_{1k}\\
\vdots&&\vdots\\
X_{k1}&\cdots&X_{kk}\end{pmatrix}

where for each
i
and
j
the
A_{ij}
and
N_{ij}
are
n_{i}\times n_{j}
matrices. Now in block matrix form we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{\underline{n}}A-qA\Phi_{\underline{n}}

\displaystyle=(\delta_{ij}\Phi_{n_{i}})(A_{ij})-q(A_{ij})(\delta_{ij}\Phi_{n_{j}})=(\Phi_{n_{i}}A_{ij}-qA_{ij}\Phi_{n_{j}})

\displaystyle XN_{\underline{n}}-qN_{\underline{n}}X

\displaystyle=(X_{ij})(\delta_{ij}N_{n_{j}})-q(\delta_{ij}N_{n_{i}})(X_{ij})=(X_{ij}N_{n_{j}}-qN_{n_{i}}X_{ij})

and so

(\Phi_{\underline{n}}A-qA\Phi_{\underline{n}})+(XN_{\underline{n}}-qN_{\underline{n}}X)=\left((\Phi_{n_{i}}A_{ij}-qA_{ij}\Phi_{n_{j}})+(X_{ij}N_{n_{j}}-qN_{n_{i}}X_{ij})\right).

From this it follows that

\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}/\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}^{2}\cong\bigoplus_{1\leq i,j\leq k}\mathcal{W}_{n_{i},n_{j}}

and so

\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}/\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}^{2}=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq k}\dim_{E}\mathcal{W}_{n_{i},n_{j}}=\sum_{1\leq i,j\leq k}n_{i}n_{j}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}\right)^{2}=n^{2}.

∎

Now take any irreducible component
\mathcal{Z}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
, and take a partition
\underline{n}
of
n
with
(\Phi_{\underline{n}},N_{\underline{n}})\in\mathcal{Z}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
. As
\mathcal{Z}
is irreducible, it follows that

\dim\mathcal{Z}=\dim\Gamma(\mathcal{Z})_{\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}}\leq\dim\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q))_{\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}}\leq\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}/\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}^{2}=n^{2}.

Thus each irreducible component of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
has dimension at most
n^{2}
, so
\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
. As observed above this implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is a complete intersection of dimension
n^{2}
. In particular,
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is equidimensional of dimension
n^{2}
. Thus as
\dim_{\Bbbk}\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}/\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}^{2}=n^{2}=\dim\dim\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q))_{\mathfrak{p}_{\underline{n}}}
for all
\underline{n}
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is regular at each of the points
(\Phi_{\underline{n}},N_{\underline{n}})
.

Hence each irreducible component
\mathcal{Z}
of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is regular at at least one point, and so
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is generically reduced. As
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is a complete intersection, and thus Cohen–Macaulay, it is in fact reduced.
∎

We shall also isolate the following simple consequence of this proof:

Proposition 3.11
.

If
\Bbbk
is a field which is an
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, and
q
is banal in
\Bbbk
, then the irreducible components of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
are in bijection with the set of unordered partitions of
n
.

For any unordered partition
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
of
n
, the irreducible component of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
corresponding to
\underline{n}
is the Zariski closure of the set of points
(\Phi,N)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
such that
N\in M_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
is nilpotent has Jordan blocks of sizes
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}
.

Proof.

In the notation of the proof of Proposition
3.7
, it suffices to show that
\dim\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}=n^{2}
for each
n
. Indeed, as is a finite type reduced
\Bbbk
-scheme, each irreducible component of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
is the Zariski closure of its
{\overline{\Bbbk}}
-points, and so is in the form
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{n}}
for some
\underline{n}
. As each
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{n}}
is irreducible and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}=\bigcup_{\underline{n}}\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{n}}
, if we have
\dim\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}=n^{2}=\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
for all
\underline{n}
, then it will follow that the
\overline{\mathcal{U}}_{\underline{n}}
’s are exactly the irreducible components of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\Bbbk}
.

Now by the proof of Lemma
3.8
and the orbit-stabilizer theorem:

\dim\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}=\dim\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})+\dim C_{\underline{n}}=n^{2}+\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})-\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\stab_{\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(N_{\underline{n}}).

Now by definition, we have

\displaystyle\stab_{\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(N_{\underline{n}})

\displaystyle=\{X\in\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})|XN_{\underline{n}}=N_{\underline{n}}X\}

\displaystyle\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})

\displaystyle=\{Y\in\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})|YN_{\underline{n}}=qN_{\underline{n}}Y\}.

But now as
\Phi_{\underline{n}}\in\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
satisfies
\Phi_{\underline{n}}N_{\underline{n}}=qN_{\underline{n}}\Phi_{\underline{n}}
(and hence also
\Phi_{\underline{n}}^{-1}N_{\underline{n}}=q^{-1}N_{\underline{n}}\Phi_{\underline{n}}^{-1}
) we get that for all
X\in\stab_{\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(N_{\underline{n}})
and
Y\in\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
that

\displaystyle(X\Phi_{\underline{n}})N

\displaystyle=X\Phi_{\underline{n}}N=qXN\Phi_{\underline{n}}=qNX\Phi_{\underline{n}}=qN(X\Phi_{\underline{n}})

\displaystyle(Y\Phi_{\underline{n}}^{-1})N

\displaystyle=Y\Phi_{\underline{n}}^{-1}N=q^{-1}YN\Phi_{\underline{n}}^{-1}=q^{-1}qNY\Phi_{\underline{n}}^{-1}=N(Y\Phi_{\underline{n}}^{-1}),

and so the map
X\mapsto X\Phi_{\underline{n}}
defines an isomorphism
\stab_{\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(N_{\underline{n}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})
of
{\overline{\Bbbk}}
-varieties, giving
\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\pi^{-1}(N_{\underline{n}})=\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\stab_{\GL_{n}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(N_{\underline{n}})
, and so indeed
\dim\mathcal{U}_{\underline{n}}=n^{2}
.
∎

We shall also record the following simple fact:

Proposition 3.12
.

\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
,
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
,
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{E}
and
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
are all connected schemes.

Proof.

Since
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
is
\mathcal{O}
flat, if
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
is connected then
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
will be as well. Also Proposition
3.5
implies that
\mathcal{N}(n,q)\cong\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
and
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}\cong\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)_{E}
. Thus it will suffice to prove that
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
is connected.

For any point
(\Sigma,N)\in\mathcal{N}(n,q)({\overline{E}})
, note that
t\mapsto(\Sigma,Nt)
defines a map of schemes
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{{\overline{E}}}\to\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{{\overline{E}}}
with connected image. Thus
(\Sigma,N)
and
(\Sigma,0)
are in the same connected component of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
. But now the subscheme
V(N=0)\subseteq\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
is isomorphic to
\GL_{n,E}
, and hence is connected. Thus all points of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)({\overline{E}})
in the form
(\Sigma,0)
lie in the same connected component of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
. Thus all
{\overline{E}}
-points of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
lie in the same connected component, from whence it follows that
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{E}
is connected.
∎

3.4.
The banal case

While the matrix equation
\Phi N\Phi^{-1}=qN
defining
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)
is certainly simpler than the matrix equation
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q}
defining
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
, the scheme
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
appearing in Proposition
3.5
is defined as the maximal reduced
\mathcal{O}
-flat subscheme of
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)
, and thus might be defined by a by a set of additional equations beyond the equation
\Phi N\Phi^{-1}=qN
. There is no apparent easy way to find these additional equations, so directly working with
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
is still likely to be difficult in general.

To get around this issue, we will restrict our attention to a particular values of
q
which make the situation much nicer. We will say that
q
is
banal
if
q\not\equiv 0\pmod{\ell}
and
q^{i}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
for each
i=1,\ldots,n
. This is equivalent to saying that
q
is banal in a field of characteristic
\ell
under our above definition.

In this case, we have the following:

Proposition 3.13
.

If
q
is banal then:

(1)

\mathcal{N}(n,q)=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)
.

(2)

\mathcal{N}(n,q)
is a complete intersection, reduced, flat and equidimensional over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
n^{2}
.

(3)

\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{\mathbb{F}}
is a reduced complete intersection over
\mathbb{F}
of dimension
n^{2}
.

(4)

\mathcal{N}(n,q)\cong\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
is a connected component of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
.

Proof.

Since
q
is banal,
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathbb{F}}
are both reduced complete intersections of dimension
n^{2}
by Proposition
3.7
.

In particular it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)
has relative dimension at most
n^{2}
over
\mathcal{O}
. But
\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q))
is generated over
\mathcal{O}
by
2n^{2}
generators (the entries of the matrices
\Phi,N\in\Gamma(\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q))
) with
n^{2}
relations (given by the matrix equation
\Phi N=qN\Phi
), and so it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)
is in fact a complete intersection over
\mathcal{O}
, equidimensional of relative dimension
n^{2}
.

In particular, it is flat over
\mathcal{O}
, and reduced as
\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{E}
is reduced. By definition, this implies that
\mathcal{N}(n,q)=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)
, proving (1) and (2).

Now
\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{\mathbb{F}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{N}}(n,q)_{\mathbb{F}}
by definition, so (3) follows.

For (4), the isomorphism
\mathcal{N}(n,q)\cong\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
comes from Proposition
3.5
. It remains to show that
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
is a connected component of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
. Recall that it is indeed connected by Proposition
3.12
.

Let
P=(\Phi,\Sigma)\in\mathcal{M}(n,q)
be any point, and let
\kappa(P)
be the residue field of
P
, which is a field of either characteristic
0
or
\ell
, since
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
is a
\mathcal{O}
-scheme. Let
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}\in\overline{\kappa(P)}
be the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
. Then as
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q}
in
M_{n}(\kappa(P))
, the eigenvalues of
\Sigma
are the same as those of
\Phi\Sigma\Phi^{-1}=\Sigma^{q}
, and hence
(\lambda_{1}^{q},\lambda_{2}^{q},\ldots,\lambda_{n}^{q})
is a permutation of
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n})
. In particular, for each
j=1,\ldots,n
there is some
i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
with
\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{j}^{q^{i_{j}}-1}
.

Thus letting
M=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(q^{i}-1)
, it follows that each eigenvalue of
\Sigma
is a
M^{th}
root of unity in
\overline{\kappa(P)}
. Note that the condition that
q
is banal implies that
\ell\nmid M
. Now let
\zeta_{M}\in{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}
be a primitive
M^{th}
root of unity, and consider the finite set of polynomials

\mathcal{P}_{M}=\{(t-\zeta_{M}^{e_{1}})\cdots(t-\zeta_{M}^{e_{n}})|e_{1},\ldots,e_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}[\zeta_{M}][t].

By the above observation, there is some
p(t)\in\mathcal{P}_{M}
with
\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=p(t)
in
\kappa(P)
. On the other hand, since
\ell\nmid M
, the
M^{th}
roots of unity
1,\zeta_{M},\zeta_{M}^{2},\ldots,\zeta_{M}^{M-1}
are distinct in
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}
. Since
\kappa(P)
has characteristic either
0
or
\ell
, it follows that the polynomials in
\mathcal{P}_{M}
are all distinct in
\kappa(P)[t]
, and so in fact there is a unique
p(t)\in\mathcal{P}_{M}
with
\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=p(t)
in
\kappa(P)[t]
.

It follows from this, and the fact that
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
is reduced, that
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(n,q)=\bigsqcup_{p(t)\in\mathcal{P}_{M}}V(\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=p(t))
. Since each
V(\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=p(t))
is a closed subscheme of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
, it follows that each
V(\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=p(t))
is a union of connected components of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
.

In particular, the scheme
M^{\unip}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}:=V(\chi_{\Sigma}(t)=(t-1)^{n})
considered above is a union of connected components of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
. Since
\mathcal{M}(n,q)_{\mathcal{O}}
is reduced and
\mathcal{O}
-flat, this implies that
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}
is reduced and
\mathcal{O}
-flat as well. In particular,
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)=\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)^{\circ}_{\mathcal{O}}
, so
\mathcal{M}^{\unip}(n,q)
is a union of connected components of
\mathcal{M}(n,q)
, and hence is a connected component as it is already connected.
∎

4.
Models for local deformation rings in the banal case

For this section we will assume that
q
is banal (i.e. that
q\not\equiv 0\pmod{\ell}
and
q^{i}\not\equiv 1\pmod{\ell}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
). Again let
\overline{r}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F})
be a continuous Galois representation with
\overline{r}(\widetilde{P}_{L})=1
, and assume that
\overline{r}
is unipotent. We shall also assume, by enlarging
\mathbb{F}
if necessary, that
\mathbb{F}
contains all eigenvalues of
\overline{r}(\varphi)
.

By Propositions
3.5
and
3.13
we have
R^{\square}(\overline{r})=R^{\unip}(\overline{r})
and
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
is isomorphic to the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point
(\overline{r}(\varphi),\log\overline{r}(\sigma))
.

While the ring
\Gamma(\mathcal{N}(n,q))
is a fairly nicely behaved when
q
is banal by Proposition
3.13
, it is still in general defined by
2n^{2}
generators with
n^{2}
relations. Even for small values of
n
, like
n=3
or
n=4
, this can make it quite difficult to work with explicitly.

The purpose of this section is to construct a much simpler scheme, specific to the banal case, which can also be used to compute
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
. This ring will be simple enough to allow us to carry out explicit computations in Section
10
and
11
.

Definition 4.1
.

Fix an integer
n\geq 1
and an
ordered
partition
\underline{n}=(n_{1},n_{2},\ldots,n_{k})
. For any ring
R
, let
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(R)
be the set
2k-1
tuples
(X_{1},N_{1},X_{2},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1},X_{k})
where for each
i
,
X_{i}\in M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}(R)
and
N_{i}\in M_{n_{i}\times n_{i+1}}(R)
satisfying the equations
X_{i}N_{i}=N_{i}X_{i+1}
for each
i=1,\ldots,k-1
.

This defines an affine scheme
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
over
\Spec\mathbb{Z}
. For any ring
R
, let
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{R}=\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\mathbb{Z}}\times_{\Spec\mathbb{Z}}\Spec R
. We will write
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}=\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

4.1.
Modeling
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
via
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}

We shall first show that the spaces
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
can be used to model the local deformation ring
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
.

For this section, fix an
A\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}
, that is, an Artinian local
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
A
with residue field
\mathbb{F}
. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
denote the maximal ideal of
A
, so that
A/\mathfrak{m}_{A}=\mathbb{F}
.

As noted above,
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
is isomorphic to the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point
x_{\overline{r}}:=(\overline{r}(\varphi),\log\overline{r}(\sigma))
. It follows that there are natural bijections

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{O}}(R^{\square}(\overline{r}),A)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathcal{N}(n,q)_{x_{\overline{r}}}(A)\equiv\left\{(\Phi,N)\in\GL_{n}(A)\times M_{n\times n}(A)\middle|\parbox{112.0187pt}{$\Phi N=qN\Phi$,\\
$\Phi\equiv\overline{r}(\varphi)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$,\\
$N\equiv\log\overline{r}(\sigma)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$}\right\}.

We aim to find a simpler description of the set on the right.

First, we note the following lemmas:

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
M
be a free
A
-module of finite rank, and let
T:M\to M
be an
A
-linear operator. Let
\overline{M}=M/\mathfrak{m}_{A}M
and let
{\overline{T}}:\overline{M}\to\overline{M}
be the induced
\mathbb{F}
-linear operator.

Let the distinct eigenvalues of
{\overline{T}}
be
\overline{\lambda}_{1},\overline{\lambda}_{2},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}_{k}
, and assume all of these are in
\mathbb{F}
. For each
i
, let
\lambda_{i}\in A
be a lift of
\overline{\lambda}_{i}
. Then there is a unique decomposition

M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{k}

where for each
i
,
T(M_{i})\subseteq M_{i}
and
T-\lambda_{i}
acts nilpotently on
M_{i}
.

Moreover, each
M_{i}
is a free
A
-module and
\overline{M}_{i}:=M_{i}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{i}\subseteq\overline{M}
is the generalized eigenspace of the eigenvalue
\overline{\lambda}_{i}
of
{\overline{T}}
.

Proof.

We shall prove the existence of this decomposition by induction on
k
. When
k=0
, we have
M=0
, and so the claim is automatically true.

Now assume that the claim holds whenever
{\overline{T}}
has
k
distinct eigenvalues. Let
M
and
T
satisfy the conditions of the lemma, and assume that
{\overline{T}}
has
k+1
distinct eigenvalues
\overline{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}_{k},\overline{\lambda}_{k+1}
.

Since
A
is Artinian,
M
has finite length as an
A
-module. It follows that the chains of submodules
\{\ker(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{m}\}
and
\{(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{m}M\}
both eventually stabilize. That is, there is some
N\geq 0
such that
\ker(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{m}=\ker(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}
and
(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{m}M=(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}M
for all
m\geq N
.

Let
M_{k+1}=\ker(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}
and
M^{\prime}=(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}M
. Clearly
T(M_{k+1})\subseteq M_{k+1}
and
T(M^{\prime})\subseteq M^{\prime}
. By definition
(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}M_{k+1}=0
, so
T-\lambda_{k+1}
acts nilpotently on
M_{k+1}
. Also

(T-\lambda_{k+1})M^{\prime}=(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N+1}M=(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}M=M^{\prime}

so (as
M^{\prime}
has finite length),
T-\lambda_{k+1}
is invertible on
M^{\prime}
.

In particular this implies that
M_{k+1}\cap M^{\prime}=0
, and so there is an injection
M^{\prime}\oplus M_{k+1}\hookrightarrow M
. But also by considering the
A
-linear operator
(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}:M\to M
we see that

\displaystyle\length_{A}(M)

\displaystyle=\length_{A}\ker(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}+\length_{A}(T-\lambda_{k+1})^{N}M=\length_{A}M_{k+1}+\length_{A}M^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\length_{A}(M^{\prime}\oplus M_{k+1}).

As
M
has finite length, this gives
M=M^{\prime}\oplus M_{k+1}
. Since
M
is a free
A
-module,
M^{\prime}
and
M_{k+1}
