are thus projective, and hence free,
A
-modules.

If
\overline{M}^{\prime}=M^{\prime}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}M^{\prime}
and
\overline{M}_{k+1}=M_{k+1}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}
, then
\overline{M}=\overline{M}^{\prime}\oplus\overline{M}_{k+1}
, and
{\overline{T}}-\overline{\lambda}_{k+1}
acts invertibly on
\overline{M}^{\prime}
and nilpotently on
\overline{M}_{k+1}
. It follows that
\overline{M}_{k+1}
is the generalized
{\overline{T}}
-eigenspace of
\overline{\lambda}_{k+1}
and
\overline{\lambda}_{k+1}
is not an eigenvalue of the restriction
{\overline{T}}|_{\overline{M}^{\prime}}
. Moreover it follows that for each
i\leq k
the generalized
{\overline{T}}
-eigenspace of
\overline{\lambda}_{i}
is contained in
\overline{M}^{\prime}
, and hence coincides with the generalized
{\overline{T}}|_{\overline{M}^{\prime}}
-eigenspace of
\overline{\lambda}_{k}
.

The claim now follows by applying the inductive hypothesis to
M^{\prime}
and
T|_{M^{\prime}}
, as the eigenvalues of
{\overline{T}}|_{\overline{M}^{\prime}}
are precisely
\overline{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}_{k}
.

To prove uniqueness, note that if

M=M_{1}^{\prime}\oplus M_{2}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{k}^{\prime}

is any decomposition for which
T-\lambda_{i}
acts nilpotently on
M_{i}^{\prime}
, then for all
j\neq i
,
\lambda_{j}\not\equiv\lambda_{i}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
, and so
T-\lambda_{i}=(T-\lambda_{j})+(\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{i})
acts invertibly
M_{j}^{\prime}
. It follows that
\ker(T-\lambda_{i})^{N}=M_{i}^{\prime}
for all
N\gg 0
, and so the decomposition is indeed unique.
∎

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
\overline{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}_{k}\in\mathbb{F}
be distinct and nonzero and let
n_{1},\ldots,n_{k}\geq 1
be integers with
n_{1}+\cdots+n_{k}=n
. For each
i=1,\ldots,k
let
{\overline{U}}_{i}\in\GL_{n_{i}}(\mathbb{F})
be a unipotent matrix. Consider the block diagonal matrix

{\overline{T}}=\begin{pmatrix}\overline{\lambda}_{1}{\overline{U}}_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\overline{\lambda}_{2}{\overline{U}}_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\overline{\lambda}_{k}{\overline{U}}_{k}\end{pmatrix}\in M_{n\times n}(\mathbb{F})

For each
i=1,\ldots,k
, fix a lift
\lambda_{i}\in A
of
\overline{\lambda}_{i}
. Then any lift
T\in M_{n\times n}(A)
of
{\overline{T}}
may be written
uniquely
in the form:

T=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{1}}&B_{12}&\cdots&B_{1n}\\
B_{21}&I_{n_{2}}&\cdots&B_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
B_{k1}&B_{k2}&\cdots&I_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}Y_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}Y_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\lambda_{k}Y_{k}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{1}}&B_{12}&\cdots&B_{1n}\\
B_{21}&I_{n_{2}}&\cdots&B_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
B_{k1}&B_{k2}&\cdots&I_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}^{-1}

where

•

B_{ij}\in\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{n_{i}\times n_{j}}(A)
for all
i\neq j
; and

•

Y_{i}\equiv{\overline{U}}_{i}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.

Proof.

Label the standard basis for
A^{n}
as

e^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(1)}_{n_{1}},e^{(2)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(2)}_{n_{2}},\ldots,e^{(k)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(k)}_{n_{k}}

in order. This means that for each
i
,
{\overline{T}}
stabilizes the subspace
\Span\{{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}\subseteq\mathbb{F}^{n}
, where
{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{j}=e^{(i)}_{j}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
. Moreover as each
{\overline{U}}_{i}
is unipotent, we in fact have that
\Span\{{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
is the generalized
{\overline{T}}
-eigenspace of
\overline{\lambda}_{i}
.

Fix a lift
T
of
{\overline{T}}
. By Lemma
4.2
we may write

A^{n}=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{k}

where each
M_{i}
is a
T
-stable free
A
module with
\overline{M}_{i}:=M_{i}/\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{i}=\Span\{{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
.

For each
i
, consider the projection map
\pi^{(i)}:A^{n}\to\Span\{e^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
given by
\pi^{(i)}(e^{(i)}_{j})=e^{(i)}_{j}
and
\pi^{(i)}(e^{(\alpha)}_{j})=0
if
\alpha\neq i
. Let
{\overline{\pi}}^{(i)}:\mathbb{F}^{n}\to\Span\{{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
be the induced map.

Then as
{\overline{\pi}}^{(i)}(\overline{M}_{i})=\Span\{{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,{\overline{e}}^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
, Nakayama’s Lemma gives
\pi^{(i)}(M_{i})=\Span\{e^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
. Since
M_{i}
and
\Span\{e^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
are both free modules of rank
n_{i}
, this implies that
\pi^{(i)}|_{M_{i}}:M_{i}\to\Span\{e^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
is an isomorphism. Hence there is a unique
A
-basis
\{v^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,v^{(i)}_{n_{j}}\}
for
M_{i}
with the property that
\pi^{(i)}(v^{(i)}_{j})=e^{(i)}_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,n_{i}
.

Since
A^{n}=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{k}
, it follows that
\{v^{(i)}_{j}\}_{ij}
is an
A
-basis for
A^{n}
.

Writing

v^{(i)}_{j}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{k}\sum_{\beta=1}^{n_{a}}b^{(i\alpha)}_{j\beta}e^{(\alpha)}_{\beta}

we see that
b^{(ii)}_{j\beta}=\delta_{j\beta}
for all
i,j
and
\beta
from the assumption that
\pi^{(i)}(v^{(i)}_{j})=e^{(i)}_{j}
. Also as
M_{i}\subseteq\Span\{e^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,e^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}+\mathfrak{m}_{A}A^{n}
we get
b^{(i\alpha)}_{j\beta}\in\mathfrak{m}_{A}
for
\alpha\neq i
.

Now let
B
be the change of basis matrix between the bases
\{e^{(i)}_{j}\}_{ij}
and
\{v^{(i)}_{j}\}_{ij}
. Then
B
has the form

B=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{1}}&B_{12}&\cdots&B_{1n}\\
B_{21}&I_{n_{2}}&\cdots&B_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
B_{k1}&B_{k2}&\cdots&I_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}

for
B_{ij}\in\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{n_{i}\times n_{j}}(A)
, and we may write
T=BCB^{-1}
, where
C
is the matrix representation of
T
with respect to the basis
\{v^{(i)}_{j}\}
.

Since each of the submodules
M_{i}=\Span\{v^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,v^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
is stabilized by
T
,
C
is a block diagonal matrix:

C=\begin{pmatrix}C_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&C_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&C_{k}\end{pmatrix}.

As
\overline{\lambda}_{i}\neq 0
by assumption,
\lambda_{i}\in A^{\times}
, and so we may write
C_{i}=\lambda_{i}Y_{i}
for some
Y_{i}\in M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}(A)
.

But now
B\equiv I_{n}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
, so
{\overline{T}}\equiv BCB^{-1}\equiv C\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
, and so

\overline{\lambda}_{i}Y_{i}\equiv\lambda_{i}Y_{i}\equiv C_{i}\equiv\overline{\lambda}_{i}{\overline{U}}_{i}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}

for all
i
. And so
Y_{i}\equiv{\overline{U}}_{i}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
as
\overline{\lambda}_{i}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
. So indeed,
T
can be written in the desired form.

To prove uniqueness, say that
T=B^{\prime}C^{\prime}(B^{\prime})^{-1}
where

\displaystyle B^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}I_{n_{1}}&B^{\prime}_{12}&\cdots&B^{\prime}_{1n}\\
B^{\prime}_{21}&I_{n_{2}}&\cdots&B^{\prime}_{2n}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
B^{\prime}_{k1}&B^{\prime}_{k2}&\cdots&I_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle C^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{1}Y^{\prime}_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\lambda_{2}Y^{\prime}_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\lambda_{k}Y^{\prime}_{k}\end{pmatrix}

for
B^{\prime}_{ij}\in\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{n_{i}\times n_{j}}(A)
and
Y^{\prime}_{i}\in M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}(A)
with
Y^{\prime}_{i}\equiv{\overline{U}}_{i}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
.

Since
B^{\prime}\equiv I_{n}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
,
B^{\prime}\in\GL_{n}(A)
, and so
\{w^{(i)}_{j}:=B^{\prime}e_{j}^{(i)}\}_{ij}
is an
A
-basis for
A^{n}
. From the definition of
B^{\prime}
we see that
\pi^{(i)}(w^{(i)}_{j})=e^{(i)}_{j}
for all
i
and
j
. Since
C^{\prime}
, the matrix of
T
in the basis
\{w^{(i)}_{j}\}_{ij}
, is block diagonal, we get that
T
stabilizes
M_{i}^{\prime}:=\Span\{w^{(i)}_{1},\ldots,w^{(i)}_{n_{i}}\}
for each
i
and
A^{n}=M_{1}^{\prime}\oplus\cdots\oplus M_{k}^{\prime}
.

Now for each
i
consider the matrix
\lambda_{i}Y_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}I_{n_{i}}
. We have that

\lambda_{i}Y_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}I_{n_{i}}\equiv\overline{\lambda}_{i}{\overline{U}}_{i}-\overline{\lambda}_{i}I_{n_{i}}=\overline{\lambda}_{i}({\overline{U}}_{i}-I_{n_{i}})\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}.

Since
{\overline{U}}_{i}
is unipotent,
{\overline{U}}_{i}-I_{n_{i}}
is nilpotent. Hence
(\lambda_{i}Y_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}I_{n_{i}})^{n_{i}}\in\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}(A)
. Since
A
is Artinian and local,
\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{d}=0
for some
d>0
, and so
(\lambda_{i}Y_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}I_{n_{i}})^{n_{i}d}=0
, and so
\lambda_{i}Y_{i}^{\prime}-\lambda_{i}I_{n_{i}}
acts nilpotently on
M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}(A)
. It follows that
T-\lambda_{i}I_{n}
acts nilpotently on
M_{i}^{\prime}
for each
i
.

By the uniqueness part of Lemma
4.2
, it now follows that
M_{i}^{\prime}=M_{i}
. Now for each
i
and
j
we get that
w^{(i)}_{j}\in M_{i}
and
\pi^{(i)}(w^{(i)}_{j})
. By the uniqueness of
v^{(i)}_{j}
noted above, this gives
w^{(i)}_{j}=v^{(i)}_{j}
for all
i
and
j
. In particular, this implies that
B^{\prime}=B
, and so
B_{ij}^{\prime}=B_{ij}
for all
i\neq j
.

Lastly, as
B=B^{\prime}
we get
BCB^{-1}=T=BC^{\prime}B^{-1}
so
C=C^{\prime}
and so
\lambda_{i}Y_{i}=\lambda_{i}Y_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i
. Since
\lambda_{i}\in A^{\times}
this indeed implies
Y_{i}=Y_{i}^{\prime}
, and so the decomposition is indeed unique.
∎

Lemma 4.4
.

Let
a,b\geq 1
be integers. Pick elements
\alpha,\beta\in A
with
\alpha\not\equiv\beta\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
and matricies
U\in M_{a\times a}(A)
and
V\in M_{b\times b}(A)
with
U\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
and
V\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{B}}
unipotent in
M_{a\times a}(\mathbb{F})
and
M_{b\times b}(\mathbb{F})
respectively.

If
N\in M_{a\times b}(A)
is any matrix satisfying
\alpha UN=\beta NV
, then
N=0
.

Proof.

As
(\alpha U)N=N(\beta V)
we get that
(\alpha U)^{i}N=N(\beta V)^{i}
for all
i\geq 0
, and so
p(\alpha U)N=Np(\beta V)
for all
p(t)\in A[t]
.

As
V
is unipotent, all eigenvalues of
V\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
in
M_{b\times b}(\mathbb{F})
are
1
, and hence all eigenvalues of
(\beta V-\beta I_{n})\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
are
0
. As in the proof of Lemma
4.3
this implies that
(\beta V-\beta I_{b})^{D}=0
in
M_{b\times b}(A)
for some
D\gg 0
. Hence, letting
p(t)=(t-\beta)^{D}\in A[t]
we get

(\alpha U-\beta I_{a})^{D}N=N(\beta V-\beta I_{b})^{D}=N0=0.

Now as
U
is unipotent, all eigenvalues of
(\alpha U-\beta I_{a})\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
are equal to
\alpha-\beta\not\equiv 0\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
. Thus
(\alpha U-\beta I_{a})\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
is invertible in
M_{a\times a}(\mathbb{F})
, from whence it follows that
(\alpha U-\beta I_{a})\in\GL_{a}(A)
. So as
(\alpha U-\beta I_{a})^{D}N=0
, we indeed get
N=0
.
∎

Now consider the Galois representation
\overline{r}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F})
. Recall that we have assumed that all eigenvalues of
\overline{r}(\varphi)
lie in
\mathbb{F}
. Now note that we may write the set of eigenvalues of
\overline{r}(\varphi)
uniquely in the form

\bigsqcup_{\alpha=1}^{d}\{\overline{\lambda}_{\alpha},\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q,\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{2},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-1}\}

for
\overline{\lambda}^{(1)},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}^{(d)}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
with
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\neq\overline{\lambda}^{(\beta)}q^{i}
for all
\alpha\neq\beta
and
i=0,1,\ldots,k_{\beta}
.

For
i=1,\ldots,k_{\alpha}
, let
n^{(\alpha)}_{i}
be the (algebraic) multiplicity of
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}
as an eigenvalue of
\overline{r}(\varphi)
. Let
\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}=(n^{(\alpha)}_{,}\ldots,n^{(\alpha)}_{k_{\alpha}})
, and note that
n=\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}n^{(\alpha)}_{i}
.

To avoid having to repeatedly write out large matrices, we will write
C=\left(C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}
to mean that
C
is a block diagonal matrix

C=\begin{pmatrix}C^{(11)}&C^{(12)}&\cdots&C^{(1d)}\\
C^{(21)}&C^{(22)}&\cdots&C^{(2d)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
C^{(d1)}&C^{(d2)}&\cdots&C^{(dd)}\end{pmatrix}

where each
C^{(\alpha\beta)}
is in turn a block diagonal matrix:

C^{(\alpha\beta)}=\begin{pmatrix}C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{11}&C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{12}&\cdots&C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{1k_{\beta}}\\
C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{21}&C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{22}&\cdots&C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{2k_{\beta}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{k_{\alpha}1}&C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{k_{\alpha}2}&\cdots&C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{k_{\alpha}k_{\beta}}\end{pmatrix}

with each
C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}
a
n^{(\alpha)}_{i}\times n^{(\beta)}_{j}
matrix. We will also write
C=\diag\left(C^{(\alpha)}_{i}\right)^{\alpha}_{i}
to mean that
C=\left(C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}
with
C^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{ii}=C^{(\alpha)}_{i}
and
C^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}=0
whenever
(\alpha,i)\neq(\beta,j)
.

By changing the basis for
\mathbb{F}^{n}
, we may assume without loss of generality that
\overline{r}(\varphi)
is in Jordan normal form. That is,
\overline{r}(\varphi)
is a block diagonal matrix in the form

\overline{r}(\varphi)=\diag\left(\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}_{i}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}{\overline{U}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{\alpha}_{i}

where each
{\overline{U}}^{(\alpha)}_{i}\in M_{n^{(\alpha)}_{i}\times n^{(\alpha)}_{i}}(\mathbb{F})
is unipotent.

Lemmas
4.3
and
4.4
give the following:

Corollary 4.5
.

Assume that
\overline{r}(\varphi)=\diag\left(\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}_{i}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}{\overline{U}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{\alpha}_{i}
is in the above form. Fix a lift
\lambda^{(\alpha)}\in A
of
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}
for each
\alpha
.

Then:

(1)

If
\log\overline{r}(\sigma)=\left(\overline{N}^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}
then
\overline{N}^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}=0
unless
\alpha=\beta
and
j=i+1
. Moreover, for all
\alpha
and all
i=1,\ldots,k_{\alpha}-1
we have
{\overline{U}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\overline{N}_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}=\overline{N}_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}{\overline{U}}_{i+1}^{(\alpha)}

(2)

If
\Phi\in\GL_{n}(A)
and
N\in M_{n\times n}(A)
are any matrices satisfying
\Phi N=qN\Phi
,
\Phi\equiv\overline{r}(\varphi)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
and
N\equiv\log\overline{r}(\sigma)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
, then there are
unique
matrices
B=\left(B^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}
,
\Phi^{\prime}=\diag\left(\lambda^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{\alpha}_{i}
and
N^{\prime}=\left(N^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}
in
M_{n\times n}(A)
with the properties:

(a)

\Phi=B\Phi^{\prime}B^{-1}
and
N=BN^{\prime}B^{-1}
;

(b)

B^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{ii}=I_{n^{(\alpha)}_{i}}
for all
\alpha
and
i
, and
B^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\in\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{n^{(\alpha)}_{i}\times n^{(\beta)}_{j}}(A)
for all
(\alpha,i)\neq(\beta,j)
;

(c)

Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}\equiv{\overline{U}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
for all
i
and
\alpha
;

(d)

N^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}=0
unless
\alpha=\beta
and
j=i+1
, and
N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\equiv\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
for all
\alpha
and all
i=1,\ldots,k_{\alpha}-1
;

(e)

Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}N_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}=N_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}Y_{i+1}^{(\alpha)}
for all
\alpha
and all
i=1,\ldots,k_{\alpha}-1
.

Moreover if
B,\Phi^{\prime}
and
N^{\prime}
are any matrices in
M_{n\times n}(A)
satisfying the above properties, then
\Phi:=B\Phi^{\prime}B^{-1}
and
N:=BN^{\prime}B^{-1}
satisfy
\Phi N=qN\Phi
,
\Phi\equiv\overline{r}(\varphi)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
and
N\equiv\log\overline{r}(\sigma)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
.

Proof.

Note that (1) is simply the special case of (2) with
A=\mathbb{F}
, so it will be enough to prove (2) (in the case of
A=\mathbb{F}
, the claim that
N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\equiv\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
is simply the definition of
\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}
).

Fix any
\Phi
and
N
satisfying the given properties. By Lemma
4.3
, there exist unique matrices
B=\left(B^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}
and
\Phi^{\prime}=\diag\left(\lambda^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{\alpha}_{i}
satisfying
\Phi=B\Phi^{\prime}B^{-1}
and properties (b) and (c). Now let
N^{\prime}=B^{-1}NB=\left(N^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}
.

It remains to show that properties (d) and (e) are satisfied. First

N^{\prime}\equiv B^{-1}NB\equiv I_{n}^{-1}(\log\overline{r}(\sigma))I_{n}\equiv\log\overline{r}(\sigma)\equiv\left(\overline{N}^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}\right)^{\alpha\beta}_{ij}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}

so indeed
N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\equiv\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
.

Since
\Phi^{\prime}=B^{-1}\Phi B
and
N^{\prime}=B^{-1}NB
we have
\Phi^{\prime}N^{\prime}=qN^{\prime}\Phi^{\prime}
. Since
\Phi^{\prime}
is block diagonal, this implies that

\lambda^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}N^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}=\lambda^{(\beta)}q^{k_{\beta}-j+1}N^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}Y_{j}^{(\beta)}.

Since
Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}\equiv{\overline{U}}^{(\alpha)}_{i}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
and
Y_{j}^{(\beta)}\equiv{\overline{U}}^{(\beta)}_{j}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
are both unipotent, Lemma
4.4
implies that
N^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}=0
whenever
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}\neq\overline{\lambda}^{(\beta)}q^{k_{\beta}-j+1}
.

If
\alpha\neq\beta
then
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}\neq\overline{\lambda}^{(\beta)}q^{k_{\beta}-j+1}
for all
1\leq i\leq k_{\alpha}
and
1\leq j\leq k_{\beta}
by assumption, as

(k_{\alpha}-i)-(k_{\beta}-j+1)\in[-k_{\beta},k_{\alpha}-2].

while
\overline{\lambda}^{(\beta)}\neq\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{x}
for
x\in[-k_{\beta},k_{\alpha}]
.

If
\alpha=\beta
, and
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}=\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-j+1}
, then
q^{i-j+1}=1
(as
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\neq 0
). Since
1\leq i,j\leq n
,
-n+2\leq i-j+1\leq n
, so as
q
is banal, this implies
i-j+1=0
. So indeed
N^{(\alpha\beta)}_{ij}=0
unless
\alpha=\beta
and
j=i+1
, proving (d).

Finally for all
\alpha
and
i=1,\ldots,k_{\alpha}-1
we now have

\lambda^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}=\lambda^{(\beta)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}Y_{i+1}^{(\alpha)},

so (e) follows as
\lambda^{(\alpha)},q\in A^{\times}
.

It is now easy to verify that conditions (a)-(e) indeed imply that
\Phi N=qN\Phi
,
\Phi\equiv\overline{r}(\varphi)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
and
N\equiv\log\overline{r}(\sigma)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
.
∎

Thus we have natural bijections

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathcal{O}}(R^{\square}(\overline{r}),A)

\displaystyle\equiv\left\{(\Phi,N)\in\GL_{n}(A)\times M_{n\times n}(A)\middle|\parbox{130.08621pt}{$\Phi N=qN\Phi$,\\
$\Phi\equiv\overline{r}(\varphi)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$,\\
$N\equiv\log\overline{r}(\sigma)\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$}\right\}.

\displaystyle\equiv\prod_{(\alpha,i)\neq(\beta,j)}\mathfrak{m}_{A}M_{n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\times n_{j}^{(\beta)}}(A)\times\prod_{\alpha=1}^{d}\left\{(Y_{i}^{(\alpha)})_{i=1}^{k_{\alpha}},(N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1})\middle|\parbox{130.08621pt}{$Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}\equiv{\overline{U}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$,\\
$N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\equiv\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$,\\
$Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}N_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}=N_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}Y_{i+1}^{(\alpha)}$}\right\}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathfrak{m}_{A}^{n^{2}-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}\times\prod_{\alpha=1}^{d}\left\{(X_{i}^{(\alpha)})_{i=1}^{k_{\alpha}},(N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1})\middle|\parbox{144.54pt}{$X_{i}^{(\alpha)}\equiv{\overline{U}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{i}^{(\alpha)}}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$,\\
$N^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\equiv\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{i,i+1}\pmod{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}$,\\
$X_{i}^{(\alpha)}N_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}=N_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}X_{i+1}^{(\alpha)}$}\right\},

where we made a variable substitution
X_{i}^{(\alpha)}=Y_{i}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{i}^{(\alpha)}}
.

Now noting that
\Hom_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}[[t]],A)\cong\mathfrak{m}_{A}
, and that
\mathcal{O}
-algebras in
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}^{\wedge}
pro-representing the same functor
\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}\to{\bf Set}
are isomorphic, we get the following:

Theorem 4.6
.

Let
q
be banal and let
\overline{r}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F})
be a Galois representation with
\overline{r}(\widetilde{P}_{L})=1
and
\overline{r}(\sigma)
unipotent. Write the set of eigenvalues of
\overline{r}(\varphi)
as

\bigsqcup_{\alpha=1}^{d}\{\overline{\lambda}_{\alpha},\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q,\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{2},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-1}\}

for
\overline{\lambda}^{(1)},\ldots,\overline{\lambda}^{(d)}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
with
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\neq\overline{\lambda}^{(\beta)}q^{i}
for all
\alpha\neq\beta
and
i=0,1,\ldots,k_{\beta}
.

For
i=1,\ldots,k_{\alpha}
, let
n^{(\alpha)}_{i}
be the algebraic multiplicity of
\overline{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}q^{k_{\alpha}-i}
as an eigenvalue of
\overline{r}(\varphi)
. Let
\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}=(n^{(\alpha)}_{,}\ldots,n^{(\alpha)}_{k_{\alpha}})
.

Let
{\overline{U}}_{i}^{(\alpha)}
and
\overline{N}_{i,i+1}^{(\alpha\alpha)}
be as in Corollary
4.5
(1). For each
\alpha
, let

x^{(\alpha)}=\left({\overline{U}}_{1}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{1}^{(\alpha)}},\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{12},{\overline{U}}_{2}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{2}^{(\alpha)}},\ldots,\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{k_{\alpha}-1,k_{\alpha}},{\overline{U}}_{k_{\alpha}}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{k_{\alpha}}}^{(\alpha)}\right)\in\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathbb{F}).

Let
R^{\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}}_{x^{(\alpha)}}
denote the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}}
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point
x^{(\alpha)}
. Then there is an isomorphism

R^{\square}(\overline{r})\cong\widehat{\bigotimes}_{\alpha=1}^{d}R^{\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}}_{x^{(\alpha)}}\left[\left[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n^{2}-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}\right]\right].

Thus to understand the local deformation rings
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
in the banal, unipotent case, it will suffice to understand the spaces
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
.

Before moving on, we will note the following simple consequence of Theorem
4.6
:

Corollary 4.7
.

For any
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
, the
\mathcal{O}
-scheme
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
is reduced and flat over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}
. Moreover it is a complete intersection.

Also
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\mathbb{F}}
is a reduced complete intersection of dimension
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}
over
\mathbb{F}
.

Proof.

Let
n=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}
, and consider the local Galois representation
\overline{r}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F})
defined by
\overline{r}(I_{L})=1
and

\overline{r}(\varphi)=\begin{pmatrix}q^{k-1}I_{n_{1}}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&q^{k-2}I_{n_{2}}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&I_{n_{k}}\end{pmatrix}\in\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}).

By Theorem
4.6
we have an isomorphism

R^{\square}(\overline{r})\cong R^{\underline{n}}_{x}\left[\left[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n^{2}-\sum_{i}n_{i}^{2}}\right]\right]

where
R^{\underline{n}}_{x}
is the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point

x=(I_{n_{1}}-I_{n_{1}},0,I_{n_{2}}-I_{n_{2}},0,\ldots,0,I_{n_{k}}-I_{n_{k}})=(0,0,\ldots,0).

Now as
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
is reduced, flat and equidimensional over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
n^{2}
(by Propositions
3.5
and
3.13
) it follows that
R^{\underline{n}}_{x}
is reduced, flat and equidimensional over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}
. Similarly as
R^{\square}(\overline{r})/(\varpi)
is reduced and equidimensional over
\mathbb{F}
of dimension
n^{2}
(by Propositions
3.5
and
3.13
),
R^{\underline{n}}_{x}/(\varpi)
is reduced and equidimensional over
\mathbb{F}
of dimension
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}

Now consider the
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}:=\Gamma(\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}})
and interpret
x=\left(\varpi,X_{1},N_{1},\ldots,N_{i-1},X_{i}\right)
as a maximal ideal of
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
, so that
R^{\underline{n}}_{x}
is the completion of
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
at
x
. By definition,
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
is cut out by the equations
X_{i}N_{i}=N_{i}X_{i+1}
, all of which are homogeneous of degree
2
in the entries of
X_{i}
and
N_{i}
. It follows that
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
is a graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra with
x
a homogeneous ideal containing the irrelevant ideal of
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
. This means that
R^{\underline{n}}_{x}
is in fact the completion of
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
as a graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra. Hence
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
is also reduced, flat and equidimensional over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}
, as these properties are preserved under completions of graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebras, and
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}/(\varpi)
is reduced and equidimensional over
\mathbb{F}
of dimension
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}
.

Lastly, as
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
(respectively
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}/(\varpi)
) is generated over
\mathcal{O}
(respectively
\mathbb{F}
) by
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}n_{i}n_{i+1}
generators (the entries of
X_{i}
and
N_{i}
) with
\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}n_{i}n_{i+1}
relations (the equations
X_{i}N_{i}=N_{i}X_{i+1}
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
) the fact that
\dim_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}=\dim_{\mathbb{F}}\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}/(\varpi)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}
implies that
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}
, and thus
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
, (respectively
\mathcal{R}^{\underline{n}}/(\varpi)
and thus
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\mathbb{F}}
) is a complete intersection.
∎

Remark
.

It is likely that one could also prove this result by a similar argument to the one used in Proposition
3.7
using the analysis of the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
given below. We have omitted doing this here, as Theorem
4.6
is necessary for our main results anyway, and easily implies all of the needed properties of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
.

4.2.
The irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}

The goal of this section is to give a simple description of the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
, and of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
for a field
\Bbbk
, analogous to Proposition
3.11
.

First as
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
is flat and finite type over
\mathcal{O}
by Corollary
4.7
,
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}=\overline{\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{E}})}
, and so the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
are precisely the Zariski closures of the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{{\overline{E}}}
. Thus it will suffice to determine the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
for all fields
\Bbbk
.

First, for any ring
R
, write

\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{R}=M_{n_{1}\times n_{2},R}\times M_{n_{2}\times n_{3},R}\times\cdots\times M_{n_{k-1}\times n_{k},R},

and note that this is isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}_{R}^{n_{1}n_{2}+n_{2}n_{3}+\cdots+n_{k-1}n_{k}}
.

We have a natural map
\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{R}:\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{R}\to\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{R}
given by
\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{R}(X_{1},N_{1},X_{2},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1},X_{k})=(N_{1},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1})
.

Now, consider the algebraic group
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{R}:=\GL_{n_{1},R}\times\GL_{n_{2},R}\times\cdots\times\GL_{n_{k},R}
and define actions of
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{R}
on the
R
-schemes
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{R}
and
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{R}
via:

\displaystyle(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\cdot(X_{1},N_{1},X_{2},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1},X_{k})

\displaystyle=(g_{1}X_{1}g_{1}^{-1},g_{1}N_{1}g_{2}^{-1},g_{2}X_{2}g_{2}^{-1},g_{2}N_{2}g_{3}^{-1},\ldots,g_{k-1}N_{k-1}g_{k}^{-1},g_{k}X_{k}g_{k}^{-1})

\displaystyle(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\cdot(N_{1},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1})

\displaystyle=(g_{1}N_{1}g_{2}^{-1},g_{2}N_{2}g_{3}^{-1},\ldots,g_{k-1}N_{k-1}g_{k}^{-1}).

Clearly these actions make
\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{R}

\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{R}
-equivariant.

Write
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}=\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\Pi^{\underline{n}}=\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
and
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}=\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{\mathbb{Z}}
.

As it turns out, we can give a precise combinatorial description of the
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
-orbits of in
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
for any field
\Bbbk
.

Definition 4.8
.

For any
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
define the set
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k(k-1)/2}_{\geq 0}
of
k(k-1)/2
-tuples of nonnegative integers

(d_{ij})_{1\leq j\leq k-i\leq k-1}=(d_{11},d_{12},\ldots,d_{1,k-1};d_{21},\ldots,d_{2,k-2};\ldots;d_{k-2,1},d_{k-2,2};d_{k-1,1})

as follows.

First, set
d_{0j}=n_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,k
and
d_{ij}=0
whenever
i\not\in[0,k-1]
or
j\not\in[1,k-i]
. Then we will say that
(d_{ij})_{1\leq j\leq k-i\leq k-1}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
if

•

0\leq d_{ij}\leq\operatorname{min}\{d_{i-1,j},d_{i-1,j+1}\}
for all
i\geq 1
and all
j
.

•

For all
i\geq 1
and all
1\leq j<k-i
,

d_{ij}+d_{i,j+1}\leq d_{i+1,j}+d_{i-1,j+1}.

Note that the condition
0\leq d_{ij}\leq\operatorname{min}\{d_{i-1,j},d_{i-1,j+1}\}
implies that
0\leq d_{ij}\leq\operatorname{min}\{n_{j},\ldots,n_{i+j}\}
for all
i
and
j
, and so
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
is a finite set.

Proposition 4.9
.

Let
\Bbbk
be any field. Define a map
\Delta^{\underline{n}}:\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)\to\mathbb{Z}^{k(k-1)/2}
by

\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1}):=\left(\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1})\right)_{1\leq j\leq k-i\leq k-1}.

Then

(1)

The image of the map
\Delta^{\underline{n}}
is the set
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{k(k-1)/2}
;

(2)

Any two points
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1}),(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k-1})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)=\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}M_{n_{i}\times n_{i+1}}(\Bbbk)
are in the same
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
-orbit if and only if

\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})=\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k-1}).

Hence for any field
\Bbbk
, the set of
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
-orbits in
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
is in bijection with the set
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
.

Proof.

Let
V_{i}=\Bbbk^{n_{i}}
for all
i
, so that for any
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
, each
N_{i}
may be thought of as a
\Bbbk
-linear map
V_{i+1}\to V_{i}
.

First, for any
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\in\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
and any
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
we have

(g_{j}N_{j}g_{j+1}^{-1})(g_{j+1}N_{j+1}g_{j+2}^{-1})\cdots(g_{i+j-1}N_{i+j-1}g_{i+j}^{-1})=g_{i}(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1})g_{i+j}^{-1}

for all
i
and
j
. Thus, as
g_{j}
and
g_{i+j}
are invertible, if
(N_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,N_{k-1}^{\prime})=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k})\cdot(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})
we indeed have

\rank(N^{\prime}_{j}N^{\prime}_{j+1}\cdots N^{\prime}_{i+j-1})=\rank(g_{j}(N^{\prime}_{j}N^{\prime}_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1}^{\prime})g_{i+j}^{-1})=\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1})

for all
i
and
j
, so
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})=\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k-1})
.

Now take any
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
and set
d_{ij}=\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1})
for any
i
and
j
. Since
\rank(AB)\leq\rank(A),\rank(B)
for any linear maps
B:U\to V
and
A:V\to W
between finite dimensional vector spaces we have

d_{ij}=\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-2}N_{i+j-1})\leq\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-2})=d_{i-1,j}

and

d_{ij}=\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-2}N_{i+j-1})\leq\rank(N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1})=d_{i-1,j+1}.

for all
i
and
j
.

Also the following lemma (with
A=N_{j}
,
B=N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1}
and
C=N_{i+j}
) implies that
d_{ij}+d_{i,j+1}\leq d_{i+1,j}+d_{i-1,j+1}
for all
i\geq 1
and
1\leq j<k-i
:

Lemma 4.10
.

For any linear maps
C:U\to V
,
B:V\to W
and
A:W\to X
between finite dimensional vector spaces we have

\rank(AB)+\rank(BC)\leq\rank(B)+\rank(ABC)

Proof.

By rank-nullity

\displaystyle\rank(B)-\rank(AB)

\displaystyle=(\dim V-\dim\ker B)-(\dim V-\dim\ker AB)=\dim\ker(AB)-\dim\ker B

\displaystyle=\dim(\ker AB)/(\ker B)

and similarly

\rank(BC)-\rank(ABC)=\dim(\ker ABC)/(\ker BC).

But now as
C^{-1}(\ker B)=\ker(BC)
and
C^{-1}(\ker AB)=\ker ABC
,
C
induces an injection
(\ker ABC)/(\ker BC)\hookrightarrow(\ker AB)/(\ker B)
and so

\rank(BC)-\rank(ABC)=\dim(\ker ABC)/(\ker BC)\leq\dim(\ker AB)/(\ker B)=\rank(B)-\rank(AB)

giving the desired inequality.
∎

So indeed
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
.

It remains to prove that for any
(d_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
there is some
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
with
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})=(d_{ij})_{ij}
and that if
(N_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,N_{k-1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
is any other element with
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k-1})=(d_{ij})_{ij}
then
(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k-1})
is conjugate to
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})
. We will prove both of these claims simultaneously by induction on
k

In the case
k=1
the claim is vacuous. In the case
k=2
, it is simply the standard fact that a matrix
N\in M_{a\times b}(\Bbbk)
has rank
r
if and only if it is conjugate to the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}I_{r\times r}&0_{r\times(b-r)}\\
0_{(a-r)\times r}&0_{(a-r)\times(b-r)}\end{pmatrix}

under the action of
\GL_{a}(\Bbbk)\times\GL_{b}(\Bbbk)
.

Now assume that the claim holds for some value of
k\geq 2
. Fix any
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k},n_{k+1})
and take any
(d_{ij})\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
. By the inductive hypothesis there exist matrices
N_{i}\in M_{n_{i}\times n_{i+1}}(\Bbbk)
for
i=1,\ldots,k-1
with
\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{i+j-1})=d_{ij}
whenever
i+j\leq k
.

There is a filtration

V_{k}=U_{0}\supseteq U_{1}\supseteq U_{2}\supseteq\cdots\supseteq U_{k-1}\supseteq U_{k}=0

given by
U_{j}=\ker N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{k-1}
for each
j=1,\ldots,k-1
. Note that

\dim_{\Bbbk}U_{j}=\dim_{\Bbbk}V_{k}-\rank(N_{j}N_{j+1}\cdots N_{k-1})=n_{k}-d_{k-j,j}

and note that this also trivially holds for
j=0
and
j=k
by taking
d_{k0}=0
and
d_{0k}=n_{k}
. Thus
\dim_{\Bbbk}U_{j}-\dim_{\Bbbk}U_{j+1}=d_{k-j-1,j+1}-d_{k-j,j}
for all
j=0,\ldots,k-1
.

Now consider any
N:V_{k+1}\to V_{k}
. For each
j=1,\ldots,k-1
we have

\rank(N_{j}\cdots N_{k-1}N)=\rank(N_{j}\cdots N_{k-1}|_{\im N})=\rank(N)-\dim_{\Bbbk}\ker(N_{j}\cdots N_{k-1}|_{\im N})=\rank(N)-\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N).

Thus we will have
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N)=(d_{ij})_{ij}
if and only if

\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N)=d_{1k}-d_{k+1-j,j}

for all
j=1,\ldots,k-1
and
\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{0}\cap\im N)=\rank(N)=d_{1k}=d_{1k}-d_{k+1,0}
. Equivalently (as
U_{k}\cap\im N=0
),
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N)=(d_{ij})_{ij}
if and only if

\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N)-\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j+1}\cap\im N)=d_{k-j,j+1}-d_{k+1-j,j}

for all
j=0,\ldots,k-1
.

But now the definition of
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
we have
d_{k-j,j+1}-d_{k+1-j,j}\leq d_{k-j-1,j+1}-d_{k-j,j}
for all
j=0,\ldots,k-1
. It is now easy to see that we can pick a
N_{k}:V_{k+1}\to V_{k}
satisfying the desired conditions. Indeed, for each
j=0,\ldots,k-1
, pick a
W_{j}\subseteq U_{j}
with
W_{j}\cap U_{j+1}=0
and
\dim_{\Bbbk}W_{j}=d_{k-j,j+1}-d_{k+1-j,j}
(which is possible as
\dim_{\Bbbk}W_{j}+\dim_{\Bbbk}U_{j+1}\leq\dim_{\Bbbk}U_{j}
). Then

\dim_{\Bbbk}\sum_{a=0}^{k-1}W_{a}=\dim_{\Bbbk}\bigoplus_{a=0}^{k-1}W_{a}=\sum_{a=0}^{k-1}\dim_{\Bbbk}W_{a}=\sum_{a=0}^{k-1}(d_{k-a,a+1}-d_{k+1-a,a})=d_{1k}-d_{k+1,0}=d_{1k}\leq n_{k+1}=\dim_{\Bbbk}V_{k+1}

and so we may define a linear map
N_{k}:V_{k+1}\to V_{k}
so that
\im N_{k}=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{k-1}W_{a}
. We then have
U_{j}\cap\im N_{k}=\bigoplus_{a=0}^{j}W_{a}
and so we get

\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N_{k})-\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j+1}\cap\im N_{k})=\dim_{\Bbbk}W_{j}=d_{k-j,j+1}-d_{k+1-j,j}

as desired. Hence there exists a
N_{k}
with
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N_{k})=(d_{ij})_{ij}
.

Now assume that
(N_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,N_{k}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
is any other element with
\Delta^{\underline{n}}(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k-1},N^{\prime}_{k})=(d_{ij})_{ij}
. We claim that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k})
and
(N_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,N_{k}^{\prime})
are
\mathcal{H}(\Bbbk)
-conjugate. By the inductive hypothesis, there is some
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k},1)\in\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
with

(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k},1)(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N_{k})=(N^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,N^{\prime}_{k-1},g_{k}N_{k})

so from now on we may assume without loss of generality that
N_{i}=N_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i=1,\ldots,k-1
.

Letting
U_{j}
be as before, the above work implies that

\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N^{\prime}_{k})-\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j+1}\cap\im N^{\prime}_{k})=d_{k-j,j+1}-d_{k+1-j,j}=\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N_{k})-\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j+1}\cap\im N_{k})

for all
j=0,\ldots,k-1
.

Now we claim by induction on
j
, that for each
j=0,\ldots,k

(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N^{\prime}_{k})
is
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
-conjugate to some
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N^{\prime}_{k,j})
with
U_{j}\cap\im N^{\prime}_{k,j}=U_{j}\cap\im N_{k}
. Since
U_{k}=0
, the claim is automatic for
j=k
.

Assume the claim holds for some
j>0
. By replacing
N^{\prime}_{k}
by
N^{\prime}_{k,j}
we may assume that
U_{j}\cap\im N_{k}^{\prime}=U_{j}\cap\im N_{k}
. As noted above, we also have

\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j-1}\cap\im N^{\prime}_{k})-\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N^{\prime}_{k})=\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j-1}\cap\im N_{k})-\dim_{\Bbbk}(U_{j}\cap\im N_{k})

and so there exists an automorphism
g\in\GL_{U_{j-1}}
with
g|_{U_{j}}=I_{U_{j}}
and
U_{j-1}\cap\im gN^{\prime}_{k}=g(U_{j-1}\cap\im N^{\prime}_{k})=U_{j-1}\cap\im N_{k}
. Extend
g
to an endomorphism
g_{k}\in\GL_{n_{k}}(\Bbbk)
with
g_{k}(U_{a})=U_{a}
for all
a
.

By repeatedly applying the following lemma (to the filtration

0\subseteq\ker N_{a-1}\subseteq\ker(N_{a-2}N_{a-1})\subseteq\cdots\subseteq\ker(N_{1}\cdots N_{a-1})\subseteq V_{a}

of
V_{a}
) we can construct an element
(g_{1},\ldots,g_{k},1)\in\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
with the property that
g_{a}N_{a}=N_{a}g_{a+1}
for all
a=1,\ldots,k-1
. Hence
(N_{1},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1},N_{k}^{\prime})
is conjugate to

(g_{1}N_{1}g_{2}^{-1},g_{2}N_{2}g_{3}^{-1},\ldots,g_{k-1}N_{k-1}g_{k}^{-1},g_{k}N_{k}^{\prime})=(N_{1},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1},g_{k}N_{k}^{\prime})

and so we may set
N^{\prime}_{k,j-1}=g_{k}N_{k}^{\prime}
, as
U_{j-1}\cap\im g_{k}N_{k}^{\prime}=U_{j-1}\cap\im N_{k}
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
T:V\to W
be a linear transformation between finite dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector spaces. Take filtrations

0=X_{0}\subseteq X_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq X_{d}=V

and

0=Y_{0}\subseteq Y_{1}\subseteq\cdots\subseteq Y_{d-1}=W

with the property that
X_{j}=T^{-1}(Y_{j-1})
for all
j>0
. Assume that
G\in\GL(V)
satisfies
G(X_{j})=X_{j}
for all
j
. Then there is some
H\in\GL(W)
with
H(Y_{j})=Y_{j}
for all
j
and
HT=TG
.

Proof.

First note that by assumption
X_{1}=T^{-1}(Y_{0})=T^{-1}(0)=\ker T
. Also the condition
X_{j}=T^{-1}(Y_{j-1})
implies that
T(X_{j})=\im T\cap Y_{j-1}
for all
j>0
.

Define
H_{0}:\im T\to\im T
by
H_{0}(T(v))=T(G(v))
. Note that this is well defined. Indeed if
T(v_{1})=T(v_{2})
then
v_{1}-v_{2}\in\ker T=X_{1}
and so
G(v_{1})-G(v_{2})\in G(X_{1})=X_{1}=\ker T
giving
H_{0}(T(v_{1}))=T(G(v_{1}))=T(G(v_{2}))=H_{0}(T(v_{2}))
. Since
T
and
G
are linear, it follows that
H_{0}:\im T\to\im T
is also linear. Similarly the function
T(v)\mapsto T(G^{-1}(v))
is also a well defined linear map
\im T\to\im T
, and so it follows that
H_{0}:\im T\to\im T
is invertible.

For any
j=0,\ldots,d
, as
G(X_{j})=X_{j}
we have
H_{0}(T(X_{j}))=T(G(X_{j}))=T(X_{j})
. And so
H_{0}(\im T\cap Y_{j})=\im T\cap Y_{j}
for all
j=0,\ldots,d-1
. It follows that we may extend
H_{0}
to an automorphism
H\in\GL(W)
with
H(Y_{j})=Y_{j}
for all
j=0,\ldots,d-1
. But as
H|_{\im T}=H_{0}
we have
H(T(v))=H_{0}(T(v))=T(G(v))
for all
v\in V
by definition, and so indeed
HT=TG
.
∎

Thus we have shown (as
U_{0}=V_{k}
) that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N^{\prime}_{k})
is
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
-conjugate to some
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N^{\prime}_{k,0})
with
\im N^{\prime}_{k,0}=U_{0}\cap\im N^{\prime}_{k,0}=U_{0}\cap\im N_{k}=\im N_{k}
. Replacing
N_{k}^{\prime}
by
N^{\prime}_{k,0}
we may assume without loss of generality that
\im N_{k}^{\prime}=\im N_{k}
.

We now claim that there is some
g_{k+1}\in\GL_{n_{k+1}}(\Bbbk)
with
N_{k}^{\prime}=N_{k}g_{k+1}
, which will imply that
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N^{\prime}_{k})
is
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
-conjugate to

(1,\ldots,1,g_{k+1})\cdot(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N^{\prime}_{k}g_{k+1}^{-1})=(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1},N_{k})

finishing the proof.

Let
r=r_{kk}=\dim_{\Bbbk}\im N_{k}
. Fix a basis
\{e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}\}
for
\im N_{k}=\im N^{\prime}_{k}
. For each
i=1,\ldots,r
pick elements
v_{i},w_{i}\in V_{k+1}
with
N_{k}(v_{i})=e_{i}
and
N^{\prime}_{k}(w_{i})=e_{i}
. Pick bases
\{v_{r+1},\ldots,v_{n_{k+1}}\}
and
\{w_{r+1},\ldots,w_{n_{k+1}}\}
for
\ker N_{k}
and
\ker N_{k}^{\prime}
respectively (both of which indeed have dimension
n_{k+1}-r
). Then
\{v_{1},\ldots,v_{n_{k+1}}\}
and
\{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n_{k+1}}\}
are two bases for
V_{k+1}
satisfying
N_{k}(v_{i})=N^{\prime}_{k}(w_{i})=e_{i}
for
i\leq r
and
N_{k}(v_{i})=N^{\prime}_{k}(w_{i})=0
for
i>r
. Thus if we define
g_{k+1}\in\GL_{n_{k+1}}(\Bbbk)=\GL(V_{k+1})
by
g(w_{i})=v_{i}
for all
i
we will indeed have
N_{k}^{\prime}=N_{k}g_{k+1}
.
∎

For any
P\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
we will use
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
to denote the
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
-orbit in
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\Bbbk)
corresponding to
P
.

We can now use this to give a complete description of the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field. Let
C\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
be an
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
-orbit under the action of
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
on
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
. Then the Zariski closure of
(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(C)\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
in
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
. Moreover every irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
is in this form.

Proof.

For any
P=(N_{1},N_{2},\ldots,N_{k-1})\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
, consider the subvariety

\mathcal{V}_{P}:=\left\{(X_{1},\ldots,X_{k})\in\GL_{n_{1}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\times\cdots\times\GL_{n_{k}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\middle|X_{i}N_{i}=N_{i}X_{i+1}\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,k-1\right\}

of
M_{n_{1}\times n_{1}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\times\cdots\times M_{n_{k}\times n_{k}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\cong\mathbb{A}_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}^{n_{1}^{2}+\cdots+n_{k}^{2}}
.

Clearly
\mathcal{V}_{P}
is isomorphic to the fiber
(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}})^{-1}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
. Also as the equations
X_{i}N_{i}=N_{i}X_{i+1}
are all linear in the entries of the
X_{i}
’s, it follows that
(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(P)\cong\mathcal{V}_{P}\cong\mathbb{A}_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}^{d(P)}
for some integer
d(P)
depending on
P
. In other words, the fibers of the map
\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
on
{\overline{\Bbbk}}
-points are all affine spaces of varying degrees.

Now let
C\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
be an
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
-orbit. As
\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
is
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
-equivariant, the fibers
(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(P)
are isomorphic for all
P\in C
. It follows that
(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(C)
is the total space of a vector bundle of rank
d(P)
over
C
, for any
P\in C
. In particular

\dim\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(C)}=\dim{(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(C)}=\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}C+d(P)

Now as
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
is irreducible,
C
is also irreducible. Hence
(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(C)
is irreducible, so its Zariski closure
\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk})^{-1}(C)}\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}_{\Bbbk}
is irreducible as well.

By the orbit-stabilizer theorem,
\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}C=\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})-\stab_{\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(P)
for any
P\in C
. Now consider the natural embedding
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\GL_{n_{i}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\hookrightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{k}M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
. From the definition of the action of
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
on
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
we see that

\stab_{\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(P)=\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\cap\mathcal{V}_{P}\subseteq\prod_{i=1}^{k}M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}({\overline{\Bbbk}}).

Since
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
is Zariski open in
\prod_{i=1}^{k}M_{n_{i}\times n_{i}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
and
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\cap\mathcal{V}_{P}\neq\varnothing
(since
(I_{n_{1}},\ldots,I_{n_{k}})\in\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\cap\mathcal{V}_{P}
),
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\cap\mathcal{V}_{P}
is a Zariski open subset of
\mathcal{V}_{P}\cong\mathbb{A}_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}^{d(P)}
, and so
\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\stab_{\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(P)=d(P)
.

Combining this, we see that

\dim_{\mathcal{O}}\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}(C)}=\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}C+\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\stab_{\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}(P)=\dim_{{\overline{\Bbbk}}}\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}.

Since
\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}(C)}
is closed and irreducible, and
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
is equidimensional of relative dimension
\sum_{i=1}^{k}n_{i}^{2}
over
\mathcal{O}
(by Corollary
4.7
), this implies that
\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}(C)}
is indeed an irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
.

Also note that this implies that
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
has finitely many orbits in
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
, since
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
is finite type over
\mathcal{O}
, and hence has only finitely many irreducible components.

Now as
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
is flat and finite type over
\mathcal{O}
,
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}=\overline{\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}
. Now if
S=\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})\backslash\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
is the set of
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
-orbits in
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})
we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}})}=\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{\Bbbk}}))}=\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}\left(\bigsqcup_{C\in S}C\right)}=\overline{\bigsqcup_{C\in S}(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}\left(C\right)}=\bigsqcup_{C\in S}\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}\left(C\right)}.

So every irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
is indeed in the form
\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}\left(C\right)}
.
∎

Propositions
4.12
and
4.9
now give a natural bijection between the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
and the set
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
. For any
P\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
, let
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{E}})
be the corresponding
\mathcal{H}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{E}})
-orbit and let

\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(P)=\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(P)\right)}\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}

be the corresponding irreducible component.

4.3.
Irreducible components with unipotent types

Theorem
4.6
allows us to compute the framed local deformation ring
R^{\square}(\overline{r})
for a unipotent Galois representation
\overline{r}
at a banal prime
q
in terms of the spaces
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
for various
\underline{n}
. The goal of this section will be to give a corresponding description of the fixed type deformation ring
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})
for a unipotent type
\st_{\underline{m}}
. Note that by Proposition
3.13
(3), the fact that
\overline{r}
is unipotent and
q
is banal implies that all lifts of
\overline{r}
are also unipotent, and so
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\tau)=0
whenever
\tau
is a non-unipotent type.

Let
\underline{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{c})
be any partition of
n
. Recall that
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})
is by definition the Zariski closure of the set of all
x\in R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})({\overline{E}})
such that
\WD(r_{x})
has inertial type
\tau_{\underline{m}}
. Since each
r_{x}
is unipotent, this is equivalent to saying that
\log r_{x}(\sigma)
is conjugate to the matrix
N_{\underline{m}}
in
M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})
. Now recall that two nilpotent matrices
A,B\in M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})
are conjugate in
M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})
if and only if
\rank(A^{i})=\rank(B^{i})
for all
i\geq 0
. Thus
\WD(r_{x})
will have inertial type
\tau_{\underline{m}}
if and only if

\displaystyle\rank(\log r_{x}(\sigma))^{i}

\displaystyle=\rank N_{\underline{m}}^{i}=\sum_{a=1}^{c}\rank N_{m_{a}}^{i}=\sum_{a=1}^{c}\max\{m_{a}-i,0\}

for all
i\geq 0
. In light of this we make the following definition:

Definition 4.13
.

Take any
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
and let
n=\sum_{i}n_{i}
. Let
\underline{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{c})
be any (unordered) partition of
n
. We say that an element
P=(d_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
has
type
\st_{\underline{m}}
if

\sum_{j=1}^{k-i}d_{ij}=\sum_{a=1}^{c}\max\{m_{a}-i,0\}

for all
i\geq 0
, where we take
d_{0j}=n_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,k
, and we interpret
\sum_{j=1}^{k-i}d_{ij}=0
for
i\geq k
.

Let
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})\subseteq\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
be the set of all
P\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
with type
\st_{\underline{m}}
. Define

\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})=\bigcup_{P\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})}\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(P)\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{E}})

and

\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}}):=\bigcup_{P\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})}\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(P)=\overline{\left(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}\left(\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{E}})\right)\right)}\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}

treated as a subscheme of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
.

This definition is justified by the following lemma:

Lemma 4.14
.

Take any
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
and let
n=\sum_{i}n_{i}
. Let
\underline{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{c})
be any (unordered) partition of
n
. Take any
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{E}})
. Then the nilpotent matrix

\eta(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1}):=\begin{pmatrix}0&N_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&N_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&N_{k-1}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}\in M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})

is conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
in
M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})
if any only if
(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})\in\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
.

Moreover we have
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}=\bigsqcup_{\underline{m}}\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
and
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}=\bigcup_{\underline{m}}\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
, where the unions run over all unordered partitions
\underline{m}
of
n
.

Proof.

This follows from the above discussion and definitions after noting that

\eta(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})^{i}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&N_{1}\cdots N_{i}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&\cdots&N_{k-i}\cdots N_{k-1}\\
0&\cdots&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}

and so

\rank\eta(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})^{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{k-i}\rank(N_{j}\cdots N_{i+j-1})

for all
i=1,\ldots,k-1
(and
\rank\eta(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})^{i}=0
for
i\geq k
).

The final statement follows by noting that each
\eta(N_{1},\ldots,N_{k-1})
is nilpotent, and thus is conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
for
exactly
one unordered partition
\underline{m}
of
n
, and so the sets
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
form a partition of
\mathcal{W}^{\underline{n}}({\overline{E}})
.
∎

Now fix
\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}=(n^{(\alpha)}_{1},\ldots,n^{(\alpha)}_{k_{\alpha}})
for
\alpha=1,\ldots,d
. Let
n^{(\alpha)}=\sum_{i}n^{(\alpha)}_{i}
and let
n=\sum_{\alpha}n^{(\alpha)}
. Define the
\mathcal{O}
-scheme

\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}:=\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)}}\times_{\mathcal{O}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(d)}}.

Note that this is reduced, flat and equidimensional over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}(n^{(\alpha)}_{i})^{2}
. Moreover each irreducible component of
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}
is in the form

\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)}}(P^{(1)})\times_{\mathcal{O}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(d)}}(P^{(d)})

for some
(P^{(1)},\ldots,P^{(d)})\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}^{(1)}}\times\cdots\times\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}^{(d)}}
.

For any collection of unordered partitions
\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}=(m_{1}^{(\alpha)},\ldots,m_{c_{\alpha}}^{(\alpha)})
of
n^{(\alpha)}
, define

\bigcup_{\alpha}\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}=(m_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,m_{c_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,m_{1}^{(d)},\ldots,m_{c_{d}}^{(d)})

treated as an unordered partition of
n
.

For any unordered partition
\underline{m}
of
n
, define

\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})=\bigcup_{\bigcup_{\alpha}\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}=\underline{m}}\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)}}(\st_{\underline{m}^{(1)}})\times_{\mathcal{O}}\cdots\times_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}^{(d)}})

and note that this is a union of irreducible components of
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}
.

We can now prove the main result of this section:

Theorem 4.15
.

Let
q
be banal and let
\overline{r}:G_{L}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F})
be a Galois representation with
\overline{r}(\widetilde{P}_{L})=1
and
\overline{r}(\sigma)
unipotent. Let
\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}
and

x^{(\alpha)}=\left({\overline{U}}_{1}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{1}^{(\alpha)}},\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{12},{\overline{U}}_{2}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{2}^{(\alpha)}},\ldots,\overline{N}^{(\alpha\alpha)}_{k_{\alpha}-1,k_{\alpha}},{\overline{U}}_{k_{\alpha}}^{(\alpha)}-I_{n_{k_{\alpha}}}^{(\alpha)}\right)\in\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}}(\mathbb{F})

be as in the statement of Theorem
4.6
. Let

x=(x^{(1)},\ldots,x^{(d)})\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\mathbb{F}).

For any unordered partition
\underline{m}
of
n
we have an isomorphism

R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})\cong R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}(\st_{\underline{m}})\left[\left[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n^{2}-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}\right]\right].

where
R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}(\st_{\underline{m}})
is the complete local ring of the scheme
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point
x
(taken to be
0
if
x\not\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})(\mathbb{F})
).

Proof.

Let
R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}
be the complete local ring of
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}
at
x
, so that
R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}(\st_{\underline{m}})
is a quotient of
R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}
. Let
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}=\Spec R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}
and
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}(\st_{\underline{m}})=\Spec R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}(\st_{\underline{m}})
. By Theorem
4.6
,
R^{\square}(\overline{r})\cong R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}\left[\left[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n^{2}-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}\right]\right]
and so there is an isomorphism

\psi:(\Spec R^{\square}(\overline{r}))({\overline{E}})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}({\overline{E}})\times(\Spec\mathcal{O}[[t]])({\overline{E}})^{n^{2}-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}.

For each
\alpha
and each
y^{(\alpha)}=(X_{1}^{(\alpha)},N_{1}^{(\alpha)},\ldots,N_{k_{\alpha}-1}^{(\alpha)},X_{k_{\alpha}}^{(\alpha)})\in\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}}({\overline{E}})
set

\eta^{(\alpha)}(y^{(\alpha)})=\begin{pmatrix}0&N^{(\alpha)}_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&N^{(\alpha)}_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&N^{(\alpha)}_{k_{\alpha}-1}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}\in M_{n^{(\alpha)}\times n^{(\alpha)}}({\overline{E}})

and set

\eta(y^{(1)},y^{(2)},\ldots,y^{(d)})=\begin{pmatrix}\eta^{(1)}(y^{(1)})&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\eta^{(2)}(y^{(2)})&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&\eta^{(d)}(y^{(d)})\\
\end{pmatrix}\in M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})

for all
(y^{(1)},y^{(2)},\ldots,y^{(d)})\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}({\overline{E}})
.

Then note that
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
is the Zariski closure of the set of points
y\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}({\overline{E}})
with
\eta(y)
conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
in
M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})
.

As
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}
and
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
are finite type
\mathcal{O}
-schemes it follows that
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}(\st_{\underline{m}})
is the Zariski closure of the set of points
y\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}({\overline{E}})
with
\eta(y)
conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
.

Now take any
z\in R^{\square}(\overline{r})({\overline{E}})
and note that the Weil–Deligne representation
\WD(r_{z})
will have inertial type
\tau_{\underline{m}}
if and only if
\log r_{z}(\sigma)
is conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
in
M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})
.

Now take
\psi(z)=(y,b)\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}({\overline{E}})\times(\Spec\mathcal{O}[[t]])({\overline{E}})^{n^{2}-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}
. Note that the isomorphism constructed in Theorem
4.6
implies that
\log r_{z}(\sigma)
is conjugate to
\eta(y)
in
M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}})
, and thus
\log r_{z}(\sigma)
will be conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
if and only if
\eta(y)
is.

Thus the isomorphism
\psi
sends the set of points
z
for which
\WD(r_{z})
has inertial type
\tau_{\underline{m}}
to the set of points
(y,b)
where
\eta(y)
is conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
. Taking the Zariski closures of these respective sets of points thus gives the desired isomorphism

R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})\cong R^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}_{x}(\st_{\underline{m}})\left[\left[t_{1},\ldots,t_{n^{2}-\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}\right]\right].

∎

Remark
.

In general
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
may have more than one element (see for example Proposition
4.18
) which means that
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
, and hence
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
, will not always be irreducible. On the other hand, by Proposition
3.11
(and Proposition
3.13
(1)) the space
\mathcal{N}(n,q)
has a single irreducible component, which we can denote
\mathcal{N}(n,q)(\st_{\underline{m}})
, corresponding to Weil–Deligne representations of inertial type
\tau_{\underline{m}}
. This does not contradict Theorem
4.15
and Proposition
3.5
. Rather it simply shows that if
\overline{r}
is chosen so that the space
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)};\ldots;\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
from Theorem
4.15
has multiple components, then the formal completion of
\mathcal{N}(n,q)(\st_{\underline{m}})
at the
\mathbb{F}
-point corresponding to
\overline{r}
must also have multiple components.

4.4.
Special cases

In this section we will study the spaces
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
and
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})
in the special cases that will be needed for our main results.

First, note the following simple consequences of our definitions:

Lemma 4.16
.

For any
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
,

\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{1,1,\ldots,1})\cong\mathbb{A}_{\mathcal{O}}^{n_{1}^{2}+\cdots+n_{k}^{2}}
.

Proof.

For any
(d_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
, by definition we will have
(d_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{1,1,\ldots,1})
if and only if
\sum_{j}d_{ij}=0
for all
i\geq 1
, which is equivalent to saying that
d_{ij}=0
for all
i\geq 1
and all
j
. Thus
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{1,1,\ldots,1})
is the singleton set
\left\{(0)_{ij}\right\}
. It follows that

\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{1,1,\ldots,1})=\overline{(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}(0)}=(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}(0).

But
(\Pi^{\underline{n}})^{-1}(0)
is simply the subscheme of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
cut out by the equations
N_{1}=N_{2}=\cdots=N_{k-1}=0
, which is obviously isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n_{1}^{2}+\cdots n_{k}^{2}}_{\mathcal{O}}
(since the equations
N_{1}=N_{2}=\cdots=N_{k-1}=0
already imply that
X_{i}N_{i}=N_{i}X_{i+1}
for all
i
).
∎

Lemma 4.17
.

For any
n\geq 1
,
\mathcal{N}^{n}=\mathcal{N}^{n}(\st_{1,1,\ldots,1})\cong\mathbb{A}^{n^{2}}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathcal{N}^{n}
is simply the moduli space of matrices
X_{1}\in M_{n\times n}
, subject to no equations, so the claim follows trivially.
∎

Next, it is possible to completely describe the space
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Take any
n\geq 1
and consider the space
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
corresponding to the ordered partition
(1,1,\ldots,1)
of
n
. Then we have an isomorphism

\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}\cong\Spec\frac{\mathcal{O}[y_{1},z_{1},y_{2},z_{2},\ldots,y_{n-1},z_{n-1},t]}{(y_{1}z_{1},y_{2}z_{2},\ldots,y_{n-1}z_{n-1})}.

Hence
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
has
2^{n-1}
irreducible components, each isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

For any partition
\underline{m}=(m_{1},\ldots,m_{c})
and any
i=1,\ldots,n
let
c_{i}=\#\{a|m_{a}=i\}
. Then
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}(\st_{\underline{m}})
is a union of

|\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{\underline{m}})|=\frac{c!}{c_{1}!c_{2}!\cdots c_{n}!}

irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
.

In particular, for any
d|n
we have
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}(\st_{d,d,\ldots,d})\cong\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
. Then by definition (writing
X_{i}=(x_{i})
and
N_{i}=(z_{i})
),

\mathcal{R}^{1,1,\ldots,1}\cong\frac{\mathcal{O}[x_{1},z_{1},x_{2},z_{2},\ldots,z_{n-1},x_{n}]}{(x_{1}z_{1}-x_{2}z_{1},x_{2}z_{2}-x_{3}z_{2},\ldots,x_{n-1}z_{n-1}-x_{n}z_{n-1})}.

The variable change
y_{i}=x_{i}-x_{i+1}
for
i=1,\ldots,n-1
and
t=x_{n}
then gives the desired isomorphism. It is clear from this isomorphism that
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
indeed has
2^{n-1}
irreducible components each isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Now the action of
\mathcal{H}^{1,1,\ldots,1}({\overline{E}})\cong({\overline{E}}^{\times})^{n}
on
\mathcal{W}^{1,1,\ldots,1}({\overline{E}})\cong{\overline{E}}^{n-1}
is given by

(g_{1},\ldots,g_{n})\cdot(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1})=(g_{1}g_{2}^{-1}z_{1},\ldots,g_{n-1}g_{n}^{-1}z_{n-1}).

It is easy to see that
(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1}),(z_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{n-1}^{\prime})\in\mathcal{W}^{1,1,\ldots,1}({\overline{E}})
are in the same
\mathcal{H}^{1,1,\ldots,1}({\overline{E}})
-orbit if and only if
\{i|z_{i}=0\}=\{i|z^{\prime}_{i}=0\}
. Thus there are indeed
2^{n-1}

\mathcal{H}^{1,1,\ldots,1}({\overline{E}})
-orbits in
\mathcal{W}^{1,1,\ldots,1}({\overline{E}})
, generated by the elements of the set
\{0,1\}^{n-1}\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{1,1,\ldots,1}({\overline{E}})
. For any
Z=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n-1})\in\{0,1\}^{n-1}
, let

\eta(Z):=\begin{pmatrix}0&z_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&z_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&\cdots&z_{k-1}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}\in M_{n\times n}({\overline{E}}).

Then as
z_{i}\in\{0,1\}
for each
i
,
\eta(Z)
is already in Jordan canonical form. Thus
\eta(Z)
will be conjugate to
N_{\underline{m}}
if and only if
\eta(Z)
is a permutation of the Jordan blocks
N_{m_{1}},\ldots,N_{m_{c}}
of
N_{\underline{m}}
.

Then
|\mathscr{I}^{1,1,\ldots,1}(\st_{\underline{m}})|
is equal to the number of nontrivial permutations of the
c
-tuple
(m_{1},\ldots,m_{c})
, which is indeed equal to
\frac{c!}{c_{1}!c_{2}!\cdots c_{n}!}
, and so
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}(\st_{\underline{m}})
is indeed equal to a union of
\frac{c!}{c_{1}!c_{2}!\cdots c_{n}!}
irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
.

For the final claim, if
d|n
and
m=(d,d,\ldots,d)
, then we have
c=c_{d}=n/d
and
c_{i}=0
for
i\neq n/d
. Hence

|\mathscr{I}^{1,1,\ldots,1}(\st_{d,d,\ldots,d})|=\frac{(n/d)!}{0!\cdots 0!(n/d)!0!\cdots 0!}=1.

So
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}(\st_{d,d,\ldots,d})
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
, which is thus isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}
by the above.
∎

Corollary 4.19
.

For any ordered partition
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
of
n\geq 1
,

\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{n})\cong\begin{cases}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}}&\text{ if }\underline{n}=(1,1,\ldots,1)\\
\varnothing&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}
.

Proof.

The case
\underline{n}=(1,1,\ldots,1)
follows from Proposition
4.18
. So now assume that
\underline{n}\neq(1,1,\ldots,1)
. This in particular implies that
k<n
. Now if
(d_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{n})
. Then by definition

\sum_{j=1}^{k-(n-1)}d_{ij}=\max\{n-(n-1),0\}=1,

which is impossible if
k\leq n-1
. Thus
\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{n})=\varnothing
and so
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{n})=\varnothing
.
∎

We have thus completely determined
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{1,1,\ldots,1})
and
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{n})
for all
n
and all
\underline{n}
. To study banal deformation rings at “spherical level” for
n=4
it remains to study the spaces
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{2,2})
.

Proposition 4.20
.

Let
\underline{n}=(n_{1},\ldots,n_{k})
be an ordered partition of
n=4
. Then
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{2,2})=\varnothing
unless
\underline{n}=(2,2),(1,2,1)
or
(1,1,1,1)
. In each of these cases,
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{2,2})
is a single irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}}
. If
\underline{n}=(2,2)
then
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(2)
, which is irreducible of relative dimension
8
over
\mathcal{O}
. If
\underline{n}=(1,2,1)
then
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(1,1;0)
, which is irreducible of relative dimension
6
over
\mathcal{O}
. If
\underline{n}=(1,1,1,1)
, then
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,1,1}(\st_{2,2})\cong\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof.

By definition for
(d_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}
we have
(d_{ij})_{ij}\in\mathscr{I}^{\underline{n}}(\st_{2,2})
if and only if
d_{11}+\cdots+d_{1,k-1}=2
and
d_{ij}=0
for
i\geq 2
.

If
k=1
then
\underline{n}=(4)
and
\mathcal{N}^{4}(\st_{2,2})=\varnothing
by Lemma
4.17
.

If
k=4
then
\underline{n}=(1,1,1,1)
, and
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,1,1}(\st_{2,2})=\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathcal{O}}
by Proposition
4.18
, which is indeed an irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,\ldots,1}
.

Now let
k=2
so
\underline{n}=(n_{1},n_{2})=(1,3),(2,2)
or
(3,1)
. Then
\mathcal{I}^{n_{1},n_{2}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{2(2-1)/2}_{\geq 0}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and we have
d_{11}\in\mathcal{I}^{n_{1},n_{2}}
if and only if
0\leq d_{11}\leq\operatorname{min}\{n_{1},n_{2}\}
. But by the above,
d_{11}\in\mathcal{I}^{n_{1},n_{2}}(\st_{2,2})
if and only if
d_{11}=2
. Hence
\mathcal{I}^{n_{1},n_{2}}(\st_{2,2})=\varnothing
, and thus
\mathcal{N}^{n_{1},n_{2}}(\st_{2,2})=\varnothing
, unless
\operatorname{min}\{n_{1},n_{2}\}\geq 2
, which happens if and only if
(n_{1},n_{2})=(2,2)
. If
(n_{1},n_{2})=(2,2)
then we have
\mathcal{I}^{n_{1},n_{2}}(\st_{2,2})=\{2\}
, so
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(2)
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}
.

Now finally let
k=3
, so
\underline{n}=(n_{1},n_{2},n_{3})=(1,1,2),(1,2,1)
or
(2,1,1)
. Then
\mathcal{I}^{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{3(3-1)/2}_{\geq 0}=\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
is by definition the set of
3
-tuples of integers
(d_{11},d_{12};d_{21})
satisfying the inequalities

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq d_{11}\leq\operatorname{min}\{n_{1},n_{2}\},

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\leq d_{12}\leq\operatorname{min}\{n_{2},n_{3}\},

\displaystyle\max\{d_{11}+d_{12}-n_{2},0\}

\displaystyle\leq d_{21}\leq\operatorname{min}\{d_{11},d_{12}\}.

Note that in all three cases for
\underline{n}
we have
\operatorname{min}\{n_{1},n_{2}\}=\operatorname{min}\{n_{2},n_{3}\}=1
.

Now we will have
(d_{11},d_{12};d_{21})\in\mathscr{I}^{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}(\st_{2,2})
if and only if
d_{11}+d_{12}=2
and
d_{21}=0
. Since
d_{11},d_{12}\in\{0,1\}
this happens if and only if
(d_{11},d_{12};d_{21})=(1,1;0)
.

But from the above inequalities we see that
(1,1;0)\in\mathscr{I}^{n_{1},n_{2},n_{3}}
if and only if
0=d_{21}\geq d_{11}+d_{12}-n_{2}=2-n_{2}
, which happens if and only if
n_{2}=2
. Thus
\mathscr{I}^{1,1,2}(\st_{2,2})=\mathscr{I}^{2,1,1}(\st_{2,2})=\varnothing
and
\mathscr{I}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})=\{(1,1;0)\}
. So
\mathcal{N}^{1,1,2}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{2,1,1}(\st_{2,2})=\varnothing
and
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(1,1;0)
is a single irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}
.

The claims about dimensions follow from Corollary
4.7
, as complete intersections are equidimensional.
∎

It remains to study the two irreducible schemes
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
. This will be the subject of Sections
10
and
11
. For the convenience of the reader, we will summarize all of the relevant results from these two sections in the following two theorems:

Theorem 4.21
.

There is an isomorphism
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\cong\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\times_{\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, for some
graded

\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
. Moreover letting
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}(\st_{2,2})=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}k
for
k=\mathbb{F},E
, we have

(1)

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{E}(\st_{2,2})
are domains of dimension
7
.
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is a domain, which is flat over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
7
;

(2)

The singular locus of
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is precisely the subscheme paramaterized by

\left\{\left(\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix}\right)\right\}.

Under the isomorphism
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\cong\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
this is identified with the subscheme
\Spec(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})/\mathcal{I}_{+})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{+}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is the ideal generated by the positive degree elements of the graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
;

(3)

For
k=\mathbb{F},E
, there is a closed subscheme
\mathcal{Z}^{2,2}_{k}\subseteq\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}(\st_{2,2})
of codimension
1
such that each component of
\mathcal{Z}^{2,2}_{k}
is geometrically integral and
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}(\st_{2,2}))\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z}^{2,2}_{k}
is isomorphic to a Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{7}_{k}
containing at least one point of
\mathbb{A}^{7}_{k}(k)
.

(4)

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is normal and Gorenstein but is not a complete intersection;

(5)

\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2}))=\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2}))=\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{E}(\st_{2,2}))=0
.

Theorem 4.22
.

There is an isomorphism
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\cong\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\times_{\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is
graded

\mathcal{O}
-algebra. Moreover letting
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}k
for
k=\mathbb{F},E
, we have

(1)

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E}(\st_{2,2})
are domains of dimension
5
.
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is a domain, which is flat over
\mathcal{O}
of relative dimension
5
;

(2)

The singular locus of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is precisely the subscheme paramaterized by

\left\{\left(t,\begin{pmatrix}0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},t\right)\right\}.

Under the isomorphism
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\cong\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
this is identified with the subscheme
\Spec(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})/\mathcal{I}_{+})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
, where
\mathcal{I}_{+}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is the ideal generated by the positive degree elements of the graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
;

(3)

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is normal and Cohen–Macaulay but not Gorenstein;

(4)

For
k=\mathbb{F},E
, there is a closed subscheme
\mathcal{Z}^{1,2,1}_{k}\subseteq\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})
of codimension
1
such that each component of
\mathcal{Z}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is geometrically integral and
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2}))\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z}^{1,2,1}
is isomorphic to a Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{5}_{k}
containing at least one point of
\mathbb{A}^{5}_{k}(k)
.

(5)

There are isomorphisms
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2}))=\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2}))=\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E}(\st_{2,2}))\cong\mathbb{Z}
. If
\omega=\omega_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}
is the dualizing module of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
then these isomorphisms send
\omega
(respectively
\omega\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{F}
and
\omega\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}E
) to
2\in\mathbb{Z}
;

(6)

There is a unique self dual
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
-module
\mathcal{M}
of generic rank
1
. Moreover
\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathbb{F}
and
\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}E
are, respectively, the unique self dual
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E}(\st_{2,2})
modules of generic rank
1
;

(7)

We have
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{O}^{2}
;

(8)

The duality map
\tau_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}\mathcal{M}\to\omega
is surjective.

Using these theorems, and the previous work we can now give a complete description of the local deformation rings
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{1,1,1,1})
,
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{2,2})
and
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{4})
.

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
denote the completions (as graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebras) of the rings
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
from Theorems
4.21
and
4.22
, respectively.

Let
n=4
and let
q
be banal. Let
\overline{r}:G_{L}\to\GL_{4}(\mathbb{F})
be a Galois representation with
\overline{r}(\widetilde{P}_{L})=1
and
\overline{r}(\sigma)
unipotent. Then

(1)

Either
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{1,1,1,1})=0
or
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{1,1,1,1})\cong\mathcal{O}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{16}]]
;

(2)

Either
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{4})=0
or
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{4})\cong\mathcal{O}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{16}]]
;

(3)

If
\overline{r}(\sigma)=1
and
\overline{r}(\varphi)
is conjugate to
\overline{\lambda}\diag(q,q,1,1)
in
\GL_{4}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}})
for some
\overline{\lambda}\in{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}^{\times}
, then

R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{2,2})\cong\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{9}]];

(4)

If
\overline{r}(\sigma)=1
and
\overline{r}(\varphi)
is conjugate to
\overline{\lambda}\diag(q^{2},q,q,1)
in
\GL_{4}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}})
for some
\overline{\lambda}\in{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}^{\times}
, then

R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{2,2})\cong\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{11}]];

(5)

If
\overline{r}
does not satisfy either of the two previous conditions, then either
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{2,2})=0
or
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{2,2})\cong\mathcal{O}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{16}]]

Proof.

Let
\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}
and
x\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\mathbb{F})
be as in the statement of Theorem
4.15
, so that
\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}n^{(\alpha)}_{i}=4
.

By Theorem
4.15
, for any
\underline{m}
we will have
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})=0
if
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})=\varnothing
. Moreover if
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})\cong\mathbb{A}^{\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}_{\mathcal{O}}
then either
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})=0
or
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{\underline{m}})\cong\mathcal{O}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{16}]]
, depending on whether
x\in\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{\underline{m}})(\mathbb{F})
.

Now if
\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}
is a partition of
n^{(\alpha)}=\sum_{i}n_{i}^{(\alpha)}
for each
i
, then
\bigcup_{\alpha}\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}=(1,1,1,1)
if and only if
\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}=(1,\ldots,1)
for each
\alpha
. Hence

\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{1,1,1,1})=\prod_{\alpha}\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(\alpha)}}(\st_{1,\ldots,1})\cong\mathbb{A}^{\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}_{\mathcal{O}}.

Part (1) follows.

Now if
\bigcup_{\alpha}\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}=(4)
then we must have
d=1
and
\underline{m}^{(1)}=(4)
. Thus from Corollary
4.19
we have either
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{4})=\varnothing
or
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{4})=\mathcal{N}^{1,1,1,1}(\st_{4})\cong\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathcal{O}}
. Part (2) follows.

Now consider
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{2,2})
. If
\bigcup_{\alpha}\underline{m}^{(\alpha)}=(2,2)
then either
d=1
and
\underline{m}^{(1)}=(2,2)
or
d=2
and
\underline{m}^{(1)}=\underline{m}^{(2)}=(2)
. In the second case we have
