\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{2,2})\cong\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)}}(\st_{2})\times\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(2)}}(\st_{2})

so Corollary
4.19
gives that this is either
\varnothing
or
\mathbb{A}^{\sum_{\alpha}\sum_{i}\left(n_{i}^{(\alpha)}\right)^{2}}_{\mathcal{O}}
.

So now assume that
d=1
and
\underline{m}^{(1)}=(2,2)
. Then
\mathscr{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\underline{n}^{(d)}}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{\underline{n}^{(1)}}(\st_{2,2})
. By Proposition
4.20
, this is either
\varnothing
or
\mathbb{A}^{4}_{\mathcal{O}}
unless
\underline{n}^{(1)}=(2,2)
or
(1,2,1)
.

First assume that
\underline{n}^{(1)}=(2,2)
. By assumption this means that up to conjugation

\displaystyle\overline{r}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lambda}\begin{pmatrix}q{\overline{U}}_{1}&0\\
0&{\overline{U}}_{2}\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle\overline{r}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&N_{1}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}

for some
\overline{\lambda}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
,
N\in M_{2\times 2}(\mathbb{F})
and some unipotent
{\overline{U}}_{1},{\overline{U}}_{2}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
. Then by Theorem
4.15
,
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{2,2})\cong R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{8}]]
, where
R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})
is the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
at the point
x=({\overline{U}}_{1}-I_{2},N_{1},{\overline{U}}_{2}-I_{2})\in\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})(\mathbb{F})
. By Theorem
4.21
,
R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})\cong\mathcal{O}[[z_{1},\ldots,z_{8}]]
unless
N_{1}=0
and
{\overline{U}}_{1}={\overline{U}}_{2}
are scalar (which implies
{\overline{U}}_{1}={\overline{U}}_{2}=I_{2}
, since
{\overline{U}}_{1}
and
{\overline{U}}_{2}
are unipotent). In this latter case we have
R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})\cong\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})[[t]]
. Part (3) follows.

Now assume that
\underline{n}^{(1)}=(1,2,1)
. By assumption this means that up to conjugation

\displaystyle\overline{r}(\varphi)

\displaystyle=\overline{\lambda}\begin{pmatrix}q^{2}&0&0\\
0&q{\overline{U}}&0\\
0&0&1\end{pmatrix}

and

\displaystyle\overline{r}(\sigma)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&N_{1}&0\\
0&0&N_{2}\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}

for some
\overline{\lambda}\in\mathbb{F}^{\times}
,
N_{1}\in M_{1\times 2}(\mathbb{F}),N_{2}\in M_{2\times 1}(\mathbb{F})
and some unipotent
{\overline{U}}\in\GL_{2}(\mathbb{F})
. Again by Theorem
4.15
,
R^{\square}(\overline{r},\st_{2,2})\cong R^{1,2,1}_{x}(\st_{2,2})[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{1}0]]
, where
R^{1,2,1}_{x}(\st_{2,2})
is the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
at the point
x=(0,N_{1},{\overline{U}}-I_{2},N_{2},0)\in\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})(\mathbb{F})
. By Theorem
4.22
,
R^{1,2,1}_{x}(\st_{2,2})\cong\mathcal{O}[[z_{1},\ldots,z_{6}]]
unless
N_{1}=N_{2}=0
and
{\overline{U}}=I_{2}
. In this latter case
R^{1,2,1}_{x}(\st_{2,2})\cong\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})[[t]]
. Parts (4) and (5) follow.
∎

Part II
Global Theory

Let
F
be a CM field and let
F^{+}\subseteq F
be the maximal totally real subfield. Write
\Gal(F/F^{+})=\{1,c\}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
be the set of all finite places of
F^{+}
which split in
F
, and let
\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
be the set of all finite places of
F
lying over places in
\mathcal{S}
.

Let
\ell>n
be a prime number, and assume that for each prime
v
of
F^{+}
with
v|\ell
that
v
is split in the quadratic extension
F/F^{+}
, that is that
v\in\mathcal{S}
. Write
\Sigma_{\ell}\subseteq\mathcal{S}
(respectively
\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\ell}
) for the set of places of
F^{+}
(respectively
F
) lying over
\ell
.

We will fix an isomorphism
\iota:{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}\to\mathbb{C}
, and use this to identify embeddings
\tau:F\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
with embeddings
\tau:F\to\mathbb{C}
.

As before, let
E/\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
be a finite extension with ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, uniformizer
\varpi\in\mathcal{O}
and residue field
\mathcal{O}/\varpi=\mathbb{F}
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
denote the set

\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}:=\{(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}|\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n}\}.

For any integer
w\in\mathbb{Z}
, let
(\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+})^{\Hom(F,\mathbb{C})}_{w}
denote the set of tuples
(\lambda_{\tau,i})_{\tau,i}
indexed by integers
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
and embeddings
\tau:F\to\mathbb{C}
with the property that:

•

\lambda_{\tau,1}\geq\lambda_{\tau,2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{\tau,n}
for each
\tau:F\to\mathbb{C}
(that is,
(\lambda_{1,\tau},\ldots,\lambda_{n,\tau})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+}
);

•

\lambda_{i,\tau\circ c}=w-\lambda_{n+1-i,\tau}
for all
\tau:F\to\mathbb{C}
and all
i=1,\ldots,n
.

5.
Global deformation rings

In this section, we introduce the global Galois deformation rings that will be needed in our future arguments. Our presentation will largely follow that in
[
BLGGT14
, Section 1.5]
and
[
CHT08
, Section 2.3]
.

Consider the algebraic groups
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}:=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{1}
and
\mathcal{G}_{n}:=\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}\rtimes\{1,j\}
where the group
\{1,j\}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
acts on
\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}
via

j(g,a)j^{-1}=(a{{}^{t}g^{-1}},a)

where for
g\in\GL_{n}
,
{}^{t}g
denotes the transpose of
g
. Let
\nu:\mathcal{G}_{n}\to\GL_{1}
be the map defined by
\nu(g,a)=a
for
(g,a)\in\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}
and
\nu(j)=-1
.

For any ring
A
and any homomorphism
r:G_{F^{+}}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(A)
with
r^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}(A))=G_{F}
, let
\breve{r}:G_{F}\to\GL_{n}(A)
denote the composition
G_{F}\xrightarrow{r|_{G_{F}}}\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}(A)=\GL_{n}(A)\times\GL_{1}(A)\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{n}(A)
. Note that
\breve{r}
is
essentially conjugate self dual
in the sense that

\breve{r}^{\vee}\cong\breve{r}^{c}\otimes\mu

where
\breve{r}^{\vee}(g)=^{t}\breve{r}(g)^{-1}
,
\breve{r}^{c}(g)=\breve{r}(cgc^{-1})
and
\mu
is the character
\mu=\nu\circ r|_{G_{F}}:G_{F}\to A^{\times}
.

Now fix a finite subset
S\subseteq\mathcal{S}
, and assume that
\Sigma_{\ell}\subseteq S
. Consider a continuous homomorphism

\overline{\rho}:G_{F^{+}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{F^{+},S^{+}}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(\mathbb{F})

with
\overline{\rho}^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}(\mathbb{F}))=G_{F}
, and assume that
\breve{\overline{\rho}}:G_{F}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F})
is absolutely irreducible. Let
\mu:G_{F^{+}}\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
be a continuous character lifting
\nu\circ\overline{\rho}:G_{F^{+}}\to\mathbb{F}^{\times}
. Assume that
\mu
is de Rham, that
\mu(c_{v})=-1
for all
v|\infty
(where
c_{v}
is complex conjugation at the infinite place
v
), and that there is some integer
w
for which
\HT_{\tau}(\mu)=\{w\}
for all
\tau:F\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
.

Now fix a tuple
\lambda=(\lambda_{\tau,i})_{\tau,i}\in(\mathbb{Z}^{n}_{+})^{\Hom(F,\mathbb{C})}_{w}
. Then as in
[
CHT08
, Section 2]
, there exists a
universal deformation ring

R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}
and a universal lift
\rho^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}:G_{F^{+}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{F^{+},S}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu})
. We will refrain from giving a full definition of of
R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}
, and simply note the following properties (which are not enough to uniquely characterize
R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}
):

•

(\rho^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu})^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}(R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}))=G_{F,S}
;

•

\nu\circ\rho^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}=\mu
;

•

For each
x\in\left(\Spec R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}\right)({\overline{E}})
, each place
\widetilde{v}|\ell
of
F
and each embedding
\tau:F\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
inducing
\widetilde{v}
we have

\HT_{\tau}\left(\breve{\rho}_{x}|_{G_{F_{\widetilde{v}}}}\right)=\{\lambda_{\iota\circ\tau,1},\ldots,\lambda_{\iota\circ\tau,n}\},

where for
x\in\left(\Spec R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}\right)({\overline{E}})
we let
\rho_{x}
denote the map
\rho_{x}:G_{F^{+},S}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu})\xrightarrow{x}\mathcal{G}_{n}({\overline{E}})
induced by
x
.

See in particular
[
CHT08
, Section 2.4.1]
for more details on the precise statement that should replace the third condition above.

Now fix any finite set
\Sigma\subseteq S
containing
\Sigma_{\ell}
. For each
v\in\Sigma
fix a choice of place
\widetilde{v}
of
F
with
\widetilde{v}|v
, so that
v=\widetilde{v}\widetilde{v}^{c}
. For each
v\in\Sigma
, identify
G_{F^{+}_{v}}
with
G_{F_{\widetilde{v}}}
and let
\overline{\rho}_{v}
be the representation

\overline{\rho}_{v}=\breve{\overline{\rho}}|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}}:G_{F^{+}_{v}}=G_{F_{\widetilde{v}}}\to\GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}).

For each
v\in\Sigma\smallsetminus\Sigma_{\ell}
, let
R_{v}^{\square}=R^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{v})
denote the universal framed deformation ring of
\overline{\rho}_{v}
. For each
v\in\Sigma_{\ell}
, let
R^{\lambda}_{v}
denote the ring
R^{\square,\crys,\{\lambda_{\tau}\}}(\overline{\rho}_{v})
, where
\lambda_{\tau}=(\lambda_{\iota\circ\tau,1},\ldots,\lambda_{\iota\circ\tau,n})
and
\tau
runs over all embeddings
\tau:F\to{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
inducing
\widetilde{v}
. Let

R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}:=\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Sigma\smallsetminus\Sigma_{\ell}}}R_{v}^{\square}\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Sigma_{\ell}}}R_{v}^{\lambda}\right),

and note that we are suppressing the
\lambda
in our notation for
R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}
.

Then as in
[
CHT08
, Section 2.2]
we may also define the universal
\Sigma
-framed deformation ring
R^{\square_{\Sigma}}_{S,\lambda,\mu}
. Note that this is naturally an
R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}
-algebra and we have a natural embedding
R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}\hookrightarrow R^{\square_{\Sigma}}_{S,\lambda,\mu}
. For any
x\in\left(\Spec R^{\square_{\Sigma}}_{S,\lambda,\mu}\right)({\overline{E}})
, we will also use
\rho_{x}
for the map
\rho_{x}:G_{F^{+}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{F^{+},S}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}({\overline{E}})
induced by the restriction
x|_{R^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}}\in\left(\Spec\overline{\rho}^{\univ}_{S,\lambda,\mu}\right)({\overline{E}})
.

Note that for any
x\in\left(\Spec R^{\square_{\Sigma}}_{S,\lambda,\mu}\right)({\overline{E}})
, if the pullback of
x
under the map
R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}\to R^{\square_{\Sigma}}_{S,\lambda,\mu}
is equal to

\left(r_{x,v}\right)_{v\in\Sigma}\in\left(\Spec R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}\right)({\overline{E}})=\prod_{v\in\Sigma\smallsetminus\Sigma_{\ell}}\left(\Spec R_{v}^{\square}\right)({\overline{E}})\times\prod_{v\in\Sigma_{\ell}}\left(\Spec R_{v}^{\lambda}\right)({\overline{E}})

then for each
v\in\Sigma
, the lift
r_{x,v}
of
\overline{\rho}_{v}
is conjugate to
\breve{\rho}_{x}|_{G_{F_{\widetilde{v}}}}
.

6.
Unitary groups and Hecke algebras

Let
B
be a central simple
F
-algebra with
\dim_{F}B=n^{2}
and assume that

•

B^{\op}\cong B\otimes_{F,c}F

•

For any finite place
w
of
F
with
w\not\in\mathcal{S}
(i.e. which is inert or ramified over
F^{+}
),
B\otimes_{F}F_{w}\cong M_{n}(F_{w})
.

Say that
B
is split at
w
if
B_{w}\cong M_{n}(F_{w})
. Let
\widetilde{\Delta}
be the set of all places of
F
at which
B
does not split. By assumption,
\widetilde{\Delta}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
and we have
w\in\widetilde{\Delta}
if and only if
w^{c}\in\widetilde{\Delta}
. Let
\Delta
be the set of all finite places of
F^{+}
lying under an element of
\widetilde{\Delta}
.

Now let
\ddagger:B\to B
be any involution satisfying
(xy)^{\ddagger}=y^{\ddagger}x^{\ddagger}
and
\ddagger|_{F}=c
.
Define an algebraic group
G_{\ddagger}/F^{+}
by

G_{\dagger}(R)=\{x\in(B\otimes_{F^{+}}R)^{\times}|xx^{\ddagger\otimes 1}=1\}.

Assume that there exists a
\ddagger
for which
G_{\ddagger}
has the following properties:
1
1

1

Note that as in
[
CHT08
]
, the existence of such a
\ddagger
requires certain restrictions on
[F:\mathbb{Q}]
and on
B
.

•

For all
v\in\mathcal{S}\smallsetminus\Delta
,
G_{\ddagger}(F^{+}_{v})
is quasisplit;

•

For all
v|\infty
,
G_{\ddagger}(F^{+}_{v})\cong U_{n}(\mathbb{R})
, where
U_{n}(\mathbb{R})
is the compact real unitary group.

From now on, let
G=G_{\ddagger}
.

As in
[
CHT08
, Section 3.3]
we can fix an order
\mathcal{O}_{B}\subseteq B
for which
\mathcal{O}_{B}^{\ddagger}=\mathcal{O}_{B}
and
\mathcal{O}_{B,w}:=\mathcal{O}_{B}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{F}}F_{w}
is a maximal compact in
B_{w}
for all
w\in\mathcal{S}
.

Define an algebraic group
G/F^{+}
by

G(R)=\{x\in(B\otimes_{F^{+}}R)^{\times}|xx^{\ddagger\otimes 1}=1\},

for any
F^{+}
-algebra
R
, and use
\mathcal{O}_{B}
to give this an integral structure
2
2

2

As noted in
[
CHT08
]
, this integral structure may be rather poorly behaved at places
v\not\in\mathcal{S}^{+}
, but this is not relevant for our purposes
. By our assumption on
\ddagger
,
G(F^{+}_{\tau})\cong U_{n}(\mathbb{R})
for any infinite place
\tau
of
F^{+}
. That is,
G
is compact at all finite places.

Now take any
v\in\mathcal{S}
, and let
v
split in
F
as
v=ww^{c}
. The inclusion
F^{+}\hookrightarrow F
induces isomorphisms
F_{v}^{+}\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{w}
and
F_{v}^{+}\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{w^{c}}
compatible with the isomorphism
c:F_{w}\to F_{w^{c}}
induced by
c:F\to F
. Also note that we canonically have
F\otimes_{F^{+}}F^{+}_{v}\cong F_{w}\oplus F_{w^{c}}
. Identifying this with
F^{+}_{v}\oplus F^{+}_{v}
via the maps above, we see that induced isomorphism
c\otimes 1:F\otimes_{F^{+}}F^{+}_{v}\xrightarrow{\sim}F\otimes_{F^{+}}F^{+}_{v}
is just identified with the map
(x,y)\mapsto(y,x)
.

For any place
w
of
F
, let
B_{w}
denote
B\otimes_{F}F_{w}
, and note that
\ddagger
induces an isomorphism
\ddagger_{w}:B_{w}\xrightarrow{\sim}B^{\op}_{w^{c}}
of
F^{+}
-algebras, satisfying
\ddagger_{w^{c}}\circ\ddagger_{w}=\id
and moreover
\mathcal{O}_{B,w}^{\ddagger}=\mathcal{O}_{B,w^{c}}
for
w\in\mathcal{S}
. By the above, for any place
v\in\mathcal{S}
with
v=ww^{c}
we have
B\otimes_{F^{+}}F^{+}_{v}\cong B_{w}\oplus B_{w^{c}}
and under this isomorphism,
\ddagger\otimes 1
is identified with the map
(x,y)\mapsto(y^{\ddagger_{w^{c}}},x^{\ddagger_{w}})
.

This gives us an isomorphism

\displaystyle G(F_{v}^{+})

\displaystyle=\{x\in(B\otimes_{F^{+}}F^{+}_{v})^{\times}|xx^{\ddagger\otimes 1}=1\}\xrightarrow{\sim}\{(x,y)\in B_{w}^{\times}\oplus B_{w^{c}}^{\times}|xy^{\ddagger_{w^{c}}}=yx^{\ddagger_{w}}=1\}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\sim}\{(x,(x^{-1})^{\ddagger_{w}})|x\in B_{w}^{\times}\}\xrightarrow{\sim}B_{w}^{\times}.

Now for any
w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
we can identify
B_{w}=M_{r_{v}}(D_{w})
for some integer
r_{v}|n
and some division algebra
D_{w}
over
F_{w}
of dimension
(n/r_{v})^{2}
. Since
B_{w^{c}}\cong B_{w}^{\op}=M_{r_{v}}(D_{w})^{\op}\cong M_{r_{v}}(D_{w}^{\op})
, we may identify
D_{w^{c}}=D_{w}^{\op}
(and note that this implies that
r_{v}
indeed depends only on
v
, and not on
w
)

By definition
w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}\smallsetminus\widetilde{\Delta}
if and only if
D_{w}=F_{w}
(or equivalently,
r_{v}=n
).

As in
[
CHT08
, Section 3.3]
, we can and do pick the identifications
B_{w}=M_{r_{v}}(D_{w})
so that the isomorphisms
G(F_{v}^{+})\xrightarrow{\sim}B_{w}^{\times}
induce a collection of isomorphisms
\iota_{w}:G(F^{+}_{v})\xrightarrow{\sim}B_{w}^{\times}=\GL_{r_{v}}(D_{w})
, for all
v\in\mathcal{S}
and all
w|v
, with the following properties:

•

If
w\not\in\widetilde{\Delta}
, then
\iota_{w}(\mathcal{O}_{B,w})=\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F,w})
.

•

If
w\in\widetilde{\Delta}
, then
\iota_{w}(\mathcal{O}_{B,w})=\GL_{r_{v}}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{w}})
, where
\mathcal{O}_{D_{w}}\subseteq D_{w}
is the ring of integers.

•

If
w\not\in\widetilde{\Delta}
, the map

\GL_{n}(F_{w})\xrightarrow{\iota_{w}^{-1}}G(F_{v}^{+})\xrightarrow{\iota_{w^{c}}}\GL_{n}(F_{w^{c}})\to\GL_{n}(F_{w})

(where the last map is induced by the isomorphism
F_{w^{c}}\xrightarrow{\sim}F_{w}
coming from complex conjugation) is given by
x\mapsto{{}^{t}x^{-1}}
.

We will also use
\iota_{w}^{-1}
to denote the injection
\GL_{r_{v}}(D_{w})\xrightarrow{\iota_{w}^{-1}}G(F^{+}_{v})\hookrightarrow G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
.

6.1.
Hecke modules

Now we will say that a
level
is a compact open subgroup
K\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
in the form
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}
for some
K_{v}\subseteq G(F^{+}_{v})
satisfying the following:

•

There is a finite set
\Sigma(K)\subseteq\mathcal{S}
such that
\Sigma(K)\cap(\Delta\cup\Sigma_{\ell})=\varnothing
and for all
v\in\mathcal{S}\smallsetminus\Sigma(K)
,
K_{v}=G(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v})
.

•

For all places
v\not\in\mathcal{S}
,
K_{v}
is a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup of
G(F^{+}_{v})
.

In more general contexts, one could also consider levels where
K_{v}
is also nonmaximal finitely many
v\not\in\mathcal{S}
. However for our applications in this paper we will never need to consider such levels, and so for convenience we exclude such levels from our definition.

For any level
K\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
, consider the Shimura set

X_{K}=G(F^{+})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})/K

and let
M(K)=H^{0}(X_{K},\mathcal{O})
.

Let
\widetilde{\Sigma}(K)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
be the set of places of
F
lying over the places in
\Sigma(K)
. For any
w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}\smallsetminus(\widetilde{\Sigma}(K)\cup\widetilde{\Delta})
and any
j=0,1,\ldots,n
let
T_{w}^{(j)}
denote the double coset operator:

T_{w}^{(j)}=\left[K\iota_{w}^{-1}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\varpi I_{j}&0\\
0&I_{n-j}\end{pmatrix}\right)K\right]

acting on
M(K)
, and note that
T_{w}^{(0)}=1
and
T_{w^{c}}^{(j)}=(T_{w}^{(n)})^{-1}T_{w}^{(n-j)}
.

Let
\widetilde{S}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}
be a finite set of finite places of
F
containing
\widetilde{\Sigma}(K)\cup\widetilde{\Delta}\cup\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\ell}
and define

\mathbb{T}(K)=\mathcal{O}\left[T_{w}^{(1)},T_{w}^{(2)},\ldots,T_{w}^{(n)},(T_{w}^{(n)})^{-1}\middle|w\in\widetilde{\mathcal{S}}\smallsetminus\widetilde{S}\right]\subseteq\End_{\mathcal{O}}(M(K))

and note that this is a finite rank commutative
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, which is independent of the choice of
\widetilde{S}
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}(K)
be a
non-Eisenstein
maximal ideal of
\mathbb{T}(K)
, and note that the localization
\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
is reduced and finite flat over
\mathcal{O}
. Then we have the following well known result:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\Sigma=\Sigma(K)\cup\Delta\cup\Sigma_{\ell}
. There is a unique continuous homomorphism

\rho^{\operatorname{mod}}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{F^{+}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{F^{+},\Sigma}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})

satisfying the following properties:

(1)

(\rho^{\univ}_{\mathfrak{m}})^{-1}(\mathcal{G}_{n}^{0}(\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}))=G_{F}
;

(2)

\nu\circ\rho^{\operatorname{mod}}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\varepsilon^{1-n}\delta_{F/F^{+}}^{u}
, where
\varepsilon:G_{F}\to\mathcal{O}^{\times}
is the cyclotomic character,
\delta_{F/F^{+}}
is the unique quadratic character of
G_{F^{+}}/G_{F}
and
u\in\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
is some integer depending on
B
.

(3)

For
v\in\mathcal{S}\smallsetminus\Sigma
, if
v
splits in
F
as
v=ww^{c}
, then
\breve{\rho}^{\operatorname{mod}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(\Frob_{w})\in\GL_{n}(\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})
has characteristic polynomial:

\sum_{j=0}^{n}(-1)^{j}(\Nm_{F/\mathbb{Q}}(w))^{j(j-1)/2}T^{(j)}_{w}t^{n-j}.

(4)

For any homomorphism
x:\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to{\overline{E}}
, if
\rho^{\operatorname{mod}}_{x}
is the composition
G_{F^{+}}\xrightarrow{\rho^{\operatorname{mod}}_{\mathfrak{m}}}\mathcal{G}_{n}(\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})\xrightarrow{x}\mathcal{G}_{n}({\overline{E}})
, then for any place
w
of
F
lying over
\ell
, and any embedding
\tau:F\hookrightarrow{\overline{\mathbb{Q}}}_{\ell}
inducing
w
, we have

\HT_{\tau}(\breve{\rho}^{\operatorname{mod}}_{x}|_{G_{F_{w}}})=\{n-1,n-2,\ldots,1,0\}

(5)

Take any
v\in\Delta
, and let
v=ww^{c}
in
F
. Write
B_{w}\cong M_{n/d_{v}}(D_{w})
as above, with
\dim_{F_{w}}D_{w}=d_{v}^{2}
. Assume that
K_{v}=G(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v})\cong\GL_{n/d_{v}}(\mathcal{O}_{D_{w}})
is a maximal compact subgroup. Then for any homomorphism
x:\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to{\overline{E}}
,
\breve{\rho}^{\operatorname{mod}}_{x}|_{G_{F_{w}}}
has inertial type
\tau_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}}
(where
\rho_{x}:G_{F^{+}}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}({\overline{E}})
is as above).

Proof.

Parts (1), (2), (3) and (4) follow from
[
CHT08
, Proposition 3.4.4]
(using
[
HKV20
, Proposition 6.5.1]
for a more general form of the needed base change results).

For (5) fix any
x:\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to{\overline{E}}
. Then
x
corresponds to an automorphic representation
\sigma_{x}
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+}})
. By
[
HKV20
, Proposition 6.5.1]
and the construction of
\rho^{\operatorname{mod}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
, there is an automorphic representation
\Pi_{x}=\mathrm{BC}(\sigma_{x})
of
\GL_{n}(\mathbb{A}_{F})
with the property that, for each place
v\in\mathcal{S}
with
v=ww^{c}
in
F
:

•

\breve{\rho_{x}}|_{G_{F_{w}}}\cong\rec_{L_{w}}(\Pi_{x,w})
;

•

If
v\not\in\Delta
,
\Pi_{x,w}=\sigma_{x,v}\circ\iota_{w}^{-1}
;

•

If
v\in\Delta
,
\mathbf{LJ}(\Pi_{x,w})=\sigma_{x,v}\circ\iota_{w}^{-1}
, where
\mathbf{LJ}
is the map from Section
2.3
.

As
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible (as
\mathfrak{m}
is assumed to be non-Eisenstein),
\breve{\rho}_{x}
is absolutely irreducible as well, which implies that
\Pi
is necessarily cuspidal. In particular,
\Pi_{x,w}
is generic for all
w
, so Proposition
2.5
implies that
\Pi_{x,w}
has type
\st_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}}
for all
w\in\widetilde{\Delta}
, so
\breve{\rho_{x}}|_{G_{F_{w}}}\cong\rec_{L_{w}}(\Pi_{x,w})
has inertial type
\tau_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}}
, proving (5).
∎

We will also let

\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{F^{+}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{F^{+},\Sigma}\xrightarrow{\rho_{\mathfrak{m}}^{\operatorname{mod}}}\mathcal{G}_{n}(\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}/\mathfrak{m})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{n}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}})

denote the mod
\mathfrak{m}
reduction of
\rho^{\operatorname{mod}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Since we assumed that
\mathfrak{m}
was non-Eisenstein,
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}
is absolutely irreducible.

We will also need the following generic multiplicity one result:

Proposition 6.2
.

Assume that
K_{v}=G(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v})
for all
v\in\mathcal{S}
and
K_{v}
is a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup of
G(F^{+}_{v})
for all
v\not\in\mathcal{S}
. Also assume that the extension
F/F^{+}
is unramified at all finite places.

Then for any homomorphism
x:\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to{\overline{E}}
we have

\dim_{{\overline{E}}}(M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes_{x}{\overline{E}})=1.

Proof.

As in the proof of Proposition
6.1
,
x
corresponds to an automorphic representation
\sigma_{x}=\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\sigma_{x,v}
of
G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+}})
over
\mathbb{C}
. Let
m(\sigma_{x})
be the multiplicity with which
\sigma_{x}
occurs in the
G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+}})
representation
L_{\mathrm{disc}}^{2}(G(F^{+})\backslash G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+}}))

By the assumptions on
K_{v}
we get that
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{x,v}^{K_{v}}=1
for all
v
, so
\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\sigma_{x}^{K}=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}\bigotimes^{\prime}_{v}\sigma_{x,v}^{K_{v}}=1
. It follows from this that

\dim_{{\overline{E}}}(M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\otimes_{x}{\overline{E}})=m(\sigma_{x}).

Now
[
Lab11
, Theoreme 5.4]
implies that
m(\sigma_{x})=1
, completing the proof. Note that the conditions of that theorem are indeed satisfied in our setup, as
F/F^{+}
is unramified,
K_{v}
is maximal hyperspecial at all
v\not\in\mathcal{S}
, and
G
is compact at all infinite places.
∎

We note that the automorphic multiplicity one result needed in the above proof has been announced in far greater generality in
[
KMSW14
, Theorem 1.7.1]
(although in the case relevant to us this fact is still conditional on a sequel to that paper, as we are considering unitary groups associated to nonsplit central simple algebras), so it is likely that the conditions of Proposition
6.2
can be weakened significantly.

6.2.
Sufficiently small levels

In order to apply the standard Taylor–Wiles–Kisin patching method, one must restrict attention to levels satisfying the following condition:

Definition 6.3
.

A level
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}
is
sufficiently small
if for all
t\in G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
the order of the finite group
t^{-1}G(F^{+})t\cap K
is not divisible by
\ell
.

Fortunately, the hypotheses of Theorem
1.1
already imply that all levels we consider will automatically be sufficiently small (as we assume that
D
is nonsplit, and so
\Delta
is nonempty, and any
v_{0}\in\Delta
splits in
F
and is banal):

Proposition 6.4
.

If there exists a finite prime
v_{0}
of
F^{+}
which splits in
F
and is banal, then any level
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
is sufficiently small.

Proof.

Consider the Galois extension
F(\zeta_{\ell})/F^{+}
. As
v_{0}
splits in
F/F^{+}
, and does not ramify in
F^{+}(\zeta)/F^{+}
(as
v_{0}\nmid\ell
),
v_{0}
is unramified in
F(\zeta_{\ell})/F^{+}
. By the Chebotarev density theorem, there are infinitely many primes
v
of
F^{+}
with
\Frob_{v}=\Frob_{v_{0}}
in
\Gal(F(\zeta_{\ell})/F^{+})
. But for any such
v
, this implies that
\Frob_{v}=\Frob_{v_{0}}=1
in
\Gal(F/F^{+})
, so
v
is split in
F/F^{+}
, and
q_{v}=\Frob_{v}=\Frob_{v_{0}}=q_{v_{0}}\in\Gal(F^{+}(\zeta_{\ell})/F^{+})\subseteq(\mathbb{Z}/\ell\mathbb{Z})^{\times}
, so
v
is also banal.

Thus there are infinitely many banal primes
v\in\mathcal{S}
, and so we may fix a banal prime
v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}\smallsetminus(\Delta\cup\Sigma(K))
, so that
K_{v_{1}}=G(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})\cong\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})
(in fact, for our argument it would be enough to simply pick
v_{1}\in\mathcal{S}\smallsetminus\Delta
, so that
K_{v_{1}}\subseteq\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})
up to conjugation). Let
r=\operatorname{char}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}/v_{1})\neq\ell
.

Now take any
t=(t_{v})_{v}\in G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
. As the map
G(F^{+})\to G(F^{+}_{v_{1}})
,
g\mapsto t_{v_{1}}^{-1}gt_{v_{1}}
is injective,
t^{-1}G(F^{+})t\cap K
is isomorphic to a subgroup of
K_{v_{1}}\cong\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})
. But if
U(v_{1})=\ker(\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})\twoheadrightarrow\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}/v_{1}))
then
U(v_{1})
is a pro-
r
group, and

|\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})/U(v_{1})|\cong|\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+}}/v_{1})|=\prod_{i=0}^{n-1}(q_{v_{1}}^{n}-q_{v_{1}}^{i})=q^{n(n-1)/2}_{v_{1}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}(q_{v_{1}}^{i}-1)

and so
\ell\nmid|\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})/U(v_{1})|
, as
v_{1}
is banal. Hence
\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})
is a pro prime to
\ell
group, and so any finite subgroup of
\GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v_{1}})
has order prime to
\ell
, giving that
\ell\nmid|t^{-1}G(F^{+})t\cap K|
.
∎

6.3.
Duality

We now recall the following consequence of the main result of
[
CHT08
, Section 5.2]
:

Proposition 6.5
.

In addition to our running assumptions, assume further that either
B\cong M_{n}(F)
or
B\cong M_{n/2}(D)
, for a quaternion algebra
D
over
F
. Then there is a perfect pairing

\langle\ ,\ \rangle:M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\times M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathcal{O}

which is equivariant for the action of all Hecke operators at all primes where
B
does not ramify.

Proof.

This is essentially proved in
[
CHT08
, Section 5.2]
. For completeness, we shall summarize the results of that paper in our notation.

Consider the opposite algebra
B^{\op}
of
B
. The involution
\ddagger
on
B
induces a corresponding involution on
B^{\op}
, which we will also denote
\ddagger
. Let
G^{\prime}/F^{+}
be the unitary group associated to
B^{\op}
and
\ddagger
, which will also be compact at all infinite places of
F^{+}
. For any
K^{\prime}\subseteq G^{\prime}(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
let
X^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}}
be the corresponding Shimura set, and let
M^{\prime}(K^{\prime})=H^{0}(X^{\prime}_{K^{\prime}},\mathcal{O})
.

Note that one has
G^{\prime}\cong G^{\op}
as groups, and so in particular there is a group isomorphism
I:G\xrightarrow{\sim}G^{\prime}
given by
g\mapsto g^{-1}
. For any
K\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
the Jacquet–Langlands correspondence gives a natural identification between the Hecke algebras
\mathbb{T}(K)
and
\mathbb{T}(I(K))
acting on
M(K)
and
M^{\prime}(I(K))
respectively, and so we may treat both
M(K)
and
M^{\prime}(I(K))
as
\mathbb{T}(K)
-modules. Now
I
induces a bijection
X_{K}\xrightarrow{\sim}X^{\prime}_{I(K)}
and so induces an isomorphism
M(K)\cong M^{\prime}(I(K))
of
\mathcal{O}
-modules. However, under the identification above this map is not a
\mathbb{T}(K)
-module homomorphism. Instead it interchanges the operator
T_{w}^{(j)}
on
M(K)
with the operator
T_{w^{c}}^{(j)}
on
M^{\prime}(I(K))
.

The work of
[
CHT08
, Section 5.2]
(as well as the proof of Proposition 5.3.5 of
loc. cit.
) constructs a perfect pairing

\langle\ ,\ \rangle:M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\times M^{\prime}(I(K))_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\mathcal{O}

which is equivariant for the actions of all Hecke operators at all places where
B
does not ramify.

But now the condition that either
B\cong M_{n}(F)
or
B\cong M_{n}(D)
implies that
B\cong B^{\op}
as
F
-algebras. From this it is easy to see that there is an isomorphism
M_{(}K)\cong M^{\prime}(I(K))
preserving the action of all Hecke operators. Combining this with the above perfect pairing gives the desired result.
∎

7.
Patching

In this section we combine the results of Sections
5
and
6
to produce the patched module needed for our main argument.

First recall the definition of an
adequate subgroup
from
[
Tho12
]
(and recall that we have assumed that
\ell>n
):

Definition 7.1
.

A subgroup
H\subseteq\GL_{n}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell})
is
adequate
if:

•

H^{1}(H,{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell})=0
;

•

H^{0}(H,\ad^{0}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}^{n}))=H^{1}(H,\ad^{0}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}^{n}))=0
, where
\ad^{0}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}^{n})\subseteq M_{n\times n}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell})
is the set of trace
0
matrices, and
H
acts on
\ad^{0}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}^{n})
by conjugation;

•

The elements of
H
with order prime to
\ell
span
M_{n\times n}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell})
as a
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}
-vector space.

As shown in
[
Tho12
, Appendix A]
, if
\ell\geq 2(n+1)
, then any subgroup
H\subseteq\GL_{n}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell})
which acts absolutely irreducibly on
{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}^{n}
is adequate.

Fix a central simple
F
-algebra
B
satisfying the conditions from Section
6
, and let
\Delta
and
G
be as in that section.

Fix a level
K=\prod_{v}K_{v}\subseteq G(\mathbb{A}_{F^{+},f})
where:

•

For all
v\in\mathcal{S}
,
K_{v}=G(\mathcal{O}_{F^{+},v})
;

•

For all
v\not\in\mathcal{S}
,
K_{v}
is a hyperspecial maximal compact subgroup of
G(F_{v}^{+})
.

Fix a non-Eisentein maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}\subseteq\mathbb{T}(K)
with
\mathbb{T}(K)/\mathfrak{m}=\mathbb{F}
(which can always be achieved by enlarging
\mathcal{O}
if necessary) and consider the representation
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}:G_{F^{+}}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}})
defined after Proposition
6.1
.

We shall apply the results of Section
5
with
\overline{\rho}=\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
.

Take
\Sigma=\Delta\cup\Sigma_{\ell}
. Also pick
\mu=\varepsilon^{1-n}\delta_{F/F^{+}}^{u}
, so that
w=n-1
, and define
\lambda=(\lambda_{\tau,i})_{\tau,i}\in(Z^{n}_{+})^{\Hom(F,\mathbb{C})}
by
\lambda_{\tau,i}=n-i
for all
\tau
and all
i=1,\ldots,n
. As we have assumed that
\ell>n
, this
\lambda
is in the Fontaine–Laffaille range, and so each of the rings
R_{v}^{\lambda}
for
v\in\Sigma_{\ell}
is a power series ring by Proposition
3.1
.

As before take

R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}=\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Delta}}R_{v}^{\square}\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Sigma_{\ell}}}R_{v}^{\lambda}\right)

where
R_{v}^{\square}
is again the unrestricted framed deformation ring of
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v}=\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}|_{G_{F_{\widetilde{v}}}}
.

Now for each
v\in\Delta
, write
v=ww^{c}
in
F
, and let
B_{w}\cong M_{n/d_{v}}(D_{w})
for some
d_{v}|n
and some division algebra
D_{w}
over
F_{w}
(and note that
d_{v}
depends only on
v
and not on
w
, as
B_{w^{c}}=(B_{w})^{\op}\cong M_{n/d_{v}}(D_{w}^{\op})
).

Define the quotient

R_{\Sigma}^{\loc,B}:=\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Delta}}R_{v}^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v},\st_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}})\right)\widehat{\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}}\left(\widehat{\bigotimes_{v\in\Sigma_{\ell}}}R_{v}^{\lambda}\right)

of
R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}
, so that
\Spec R^{\loc,B}_{\Sigma}
is a union of irreducible components of
\Spec F^{\loc}_{\Sigma}
(in our applications,
R^{\loc,B}_{\Sigma}
will in fact be a domain). Note that by Proposition
6.1
,
\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m}}
has a lift with inertial type
\tau_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}}
for all
v\in\Delta
, so
R_{v}^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v},\st_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}})\neq 0
. Hence
R^{\loc,B}_{\Sigma}\neq 0
.

Now for each
v\in\Delta
,
\Spec R_{v}^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v},\st_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}})
is a union of irreducible components of
\Spec R^{\square}_{v}
, by Proposition
3.4
(1), and hence defines a local deformation problem (in the sense of
[
CHT08
, Definition 2.2.2]
) by
[
BLGHT11
, Lemma 3.2]
. By
[
CHT08
, Proposition 2.2.9]
, there is a canonical choice of quotient
R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
of
R^{\univ}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
charachterized by the property that for any
A\in\mathcal{C}_{\mathcal{O}}^{\wedge}
and any
R^{\univ}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}\to A
inducing a representation
\rho_{A}:G_{F^{+}}\twoheadrightarrow G_{F^{+},\Sigma}\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(R^{\univ}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu})\to\mathcal{G}_{n}(A)
, the map
R^{\univ}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}\to A
factors through
R^{\univ}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}\twoheadrightarrow R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
if and only if for each
v\in\Delta
, the map
R^{\square}_{v}\to A
induced by
\breve{\rho}_{A}|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}}:R_{v}^{\square}\to\GL_{n}(A)
factors through
R_{v}^{\square}\twoheadrightarrow R_{v}^{\square}(\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v},\st_{d_{v},\ldots,d_{v}})
.

Note that Proposition
6.1
implies that there exists a surjective map
R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
. Hence we may regard
M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
as an
R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
-module

A standard patching argument now produces the following:

Theorem 7.2
.

Assume that
\breve{\overline{\rho}}_{\mathfrak{m}}(G_{F(\zeta_{\ell})})\subseteq\GL_{n}({\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell})
is adequate and that there exists a finite prime
v_{0}
of
F^{+}
which splits in
F
and is banal. Then there exist:

•

Rings
R_{\infty}^{B}:=R^{\loc,B}_{\Sigma}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{g}]]
and
S_{\infty}=\mathcal{O}[[y_{1},\ldots,y_{r}]]
, for some integers
g,r\geq 0
;

•

An embedding
S_{\infty}\to R_{\infty}^{B}
making
R_{\infty}^{B}
into a finite
S_{\infty}
-algebra;

•

A finitely generated
R_{\infty}^{B}
-module
M_{\infty}
.

satisfying:

(1)

\dim S_{\infty}=\dim R_{\infty}^{B}
;

(2)

M_{\infty}
is a maximal Cohen–Macaulay
R_{\infty}^{B}
-module;

(3)

There is a surjective map
R_{\infty}^{B}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})R_{\infty}^{B}\twoheadrightarrow R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
such that the action of
R_{\infty}^{B}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})R_{\infty}^{B}
on
M_{\infty}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})M_{\infty}
, factors through
R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
and we have an isomorphism

M_{\infty}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})M_{\infty}\cong M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
of
R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
-modules;

(4)

If
F/F^{+}
is unramified at all finite places, then
M_{\infty}
has generic rank either
0
or
1
on each irreducible component of
\Spec R_{\infty}^{B}
.

(5)

Provided that either
B\cong M_{n}(F)
or
B\cong M_{n/2}(D)
for a quaternion algebra
D
over
F
,
M_{\infty}
is self-dual, in the sense that

M_{\infty}\cong\RHom_{R_{\infty}^{B}}(M_{\infty},\omega^{\bullet}_{R_{\infty}^{B}})[\dim R_{\infty}^{B}]

as
R_{\infty}^{B}
-modules, where
\omega^{\bullet}_{R_{\infty}^{B}}
is the dualizing complex of
R_{\infty}^{B}
. (In the case when
R_{\infty}^{B}
is Cohen–Macaulay, this may be written more simply as
M_{\infty}\cong\Hom_{R_{\infty}^{B}}(M_{\infty},\omega_{R_{\infty}^{B}})
).

Proof.

If
R_{\infty}^{B}
is replaced by
R_{\infty}:=R^{\loc}_{\Sigma}[[x_{1},\ldots,x_{g}]]
, the construction of
M_{\infty}
, and parts (1),(2) and (3), follow by a standard Taylor–Wiles–Kisin patching argument (see for example
[
BLGG11
, Section 3.6]
and
[
Tho12
, Section 6]
), where we use Proposition
6.4
to avoid any issues with non sufficiently small levels.

Proposition
6.1
(5) then ensures that the action of
R_{\infty}
on
M_{\infty}
factors through
R_{\infty}^{B}
, giving (1), (2) and (3).

Proposition
6.2
, and the fact that
M_{\infty}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})M_{\infty}\cong M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
, then ensures that
M_{\infty}
has generic rank
1
on each component of
\Spec R_{\infty}^{B}
on which it is supported, proving (4).

Part (5) then follows from Proposition
6.5
and

[
IKM22
, Theorem 11.3]
. Note that the perfect pairings from Proposition
6.5
are equivariant for the Hecke operators at the “Taylor–Wiles primes” and so the patching construction does not interfere with the duality.
∎

8.
The structure of
\overline{M}_{\infty}

We now specialize to the setting of Theorem
1.1
. In particular, we now take
n=4
and assume each prime
v\in\Delta
is banal, and moreover that
d_{v}=2
for all
v\in\Delta
. Define the integers:

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=\#\left\{v\in\Delta\middle|d_{v}=2,\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v}\text{ is unramified, and }\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v}(\Frob_{v})\sim\overline{\lambda}\diag(q_{v}^{2},q_{v},q_{v},1)\text{ for some }\overline{\lambda}\in{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}^{\times}\right\}

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\#\left\{v\in\Delta\middle|d_{v}=2,\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v}\text{ is unramified, and }\overline{\rho}_{\mathfrak{m},v}(\Frob_{v})\sim\overline{\lambda}\diag(q_{v},q_{v},1,1)\text{ for some }\overline{\lambda}\in{\overline{\mathbb{F}}}_{\ell}^{\times}\right\}.

Now as each
R^{\lambda}_{v}
(for
v\in\Sigma_{\ell}
) is a power series ring by Proposition
3.1
, Proposition
4.23
(recalling that
R_{\infty}^{B}\neq 0
) gives that

R_{\infty}^{B}\cong\left(\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}a}\widehat{\otimes}\left(\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]

for some integer
s\geq 0
. In particular,
R_{\infty}^{B}
is a Cohen–Macaulay normal domain, and (as
M_{\infty}\neq 0
) Theorem
7.2
(4) gives that
M_{\infty}
has generic rank
1
over
R_{\infty}^{B}
.

Let
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2}):=\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})/(\varpi)
,
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2}):=\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})/(\varpi)
and

\overline{R}^{B}_{\infty}:=R^{B}_{\infty}/(\varpi)=\left(\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}a}\widehat{\otimes}\left(\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]].

Then
\overline{R}^{B}_{\infty}
is also a Cohen–Macaulay normal domain. Also let
\overline{M}_{\infty}:=M_{\infty}/\varpi M_{\infty}
. Then
\overline{M}_{\infty}
is also a maximal Cohen–Macaulay
\overline{R}^{B}_{\infty}
-module of generic rank
1
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be the
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
-module from Theorem
4.22
(
6
), and let
\overline{M}:=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
.

The main result of this section is the following:

Theorem 8.1
.

There is an isomorphism

\overline{M}_{\infty}\cong\overline{M}^{\boxtimes a}\boxtimes\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})^{\boxtimes b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]

of
\overline{R}^{B}_{\infty}
-modules.

Remark
.

It is likely that one could in fact prove the integral version of this theorem, namely that
M_{\infty}\cong M^{\boxtimes a}\boxtimes\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})^{\boxtimes b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]
, where
M:=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
, by a similar method. We have refrained from doing so here to avoid having to prove a mixed characteristic version of Proposition
8.3
. Fortunately knowing the structure of
\overline{M}_{\infty}
is sufficient to prove Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
, so the full structure of
M_{\infty}
is not needed for our results.

We will prove Theorem
8.1
by a class group argument, similar to the one used in
[
Man21
, Theorem 3.3]
. Before proving this, we shall note that Theorem
8.1
implies Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
:

Proof of Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
.

By Theorem
7.2
(3) we have

M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}/\mathfrak{m}M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\cong\frac{M_{\infty}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})M_{\infty}}{\mathfrak{m}M_{\infty}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})M_{\infty}}=M_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{\infty}^{B}}M_{\infty}=\overline{M}_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}\overline{M}_{\infty}.

Now the description in Theorem
8.1
gives

\displaystyle\overline{M}_{\infty}/\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}\overline{M}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\cong\overline{M}_{\infty}\otimes_{\overline{R}^{B}_{\infty}}\mathbb{F}\cong\left(\overline{M}^{\boxtimes a}\boxtimes\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})^{\boxtimes b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]\right)\otimes_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}\mathbb{F}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\overline{M}\otimes_{\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})}\mathbb{F}\right)^{\otimes a}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\left(\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\otimes_{\widehat{R}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})}\mathbb{F}\right)^{\otimes b}

\displaystyle\cong\left(\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}\right)^{\otimes a}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}}\mathbb{F}^{\otimes b}\cong\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2^{a}}

where we used the fact that

\overline{M}\otimes_{\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})}\mathbb{F}\cong\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}\mathbb{F}\cong\mathcal{M}/(\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{M}+\varpi\mathcal{M})\cong\frac{\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{M}}{\varpi(\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{M})}\cong\frac{\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}}{\varpi\mathcal{O}^{\oplus 2}}\cong\mathbb{F}^{\oplus 2}

by Theorem
4.22
(
7
).

Thus
\dim_{\mathbb{F}}M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}/\mathfrak{m}M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}=2^{a}
, proving the first part of Theorem
1.1
. At this point, one could deduce the second part of Theorem
1.1
using the fact that
\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
are Cohen–Macaulay by the strategy from
[
Sno18
, Section 5]
. However this fact will also follow from our argument below.

For any Cohen–Macaulay ring
A
and any finitely generated
A
-module
M
, we will let
\tau_{M}
denote the natural map
M\otimes_{A}\Hom_{A}(M,\omega_{A})\to\omega_{A}
, where
\omega_{A}
is the dualizing module of
A
. By the argument in
[
Man21
, Proposition 4.14]
(using
[
Eme02
, Lemmas 2.4, 2.6]
) showing that
\tau_{M_{\infty}}
is surjective will imply that the map

R_{\infty}^{B}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})R_{\infty}^{B}\to\End_{R_{\infty}^{B}}(M_{\infty}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})M_{\infty})=\End_{\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}}(M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})

is an isomorphism. As this map factors as

R_{\infty}^{B}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})R_{\infty}^{B}\twoheadrightarrow R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\to\End_{\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}}(M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})

this implies that
R_{\infty}^{B}/(y_{1},\ldots,y_{r})R_{\infty}^{B}\twoheadrightarrow R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}
,
R^{B}_{\Sigma,\lambda,\mu}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}
and
\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}\twoheadrightarrow\End_{\mathbb{T}(K)_{\mathfrak{m}}}(M(K)_{\mathfrak{m}})
are all isomorphisms, which proves the second part of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
.

Note that by
[
Man21
, Lemma 3.4]
,
\tau_{M}
is surjective if and only if there exists an
A
-module surjection
M\otimes_{A}\Hom_{A}(M,\omega_{A})\to\omega_{A}
.

Let
\overline{\omega}^{1,2,1}
denote the dualizing module of
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
. As
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is Gorenstein we have

\omega_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}\cong\left(\overline{\omega}^{1,2,1}\right)^{\boxtimes a}\boxtimes\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})^{\boxtimes b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]].

By Theorem
4.22
(
8
),
\tau_{\mathcal{M}}
is surjective. Tensoring with
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
this implies that
\tau_{\overline{M}}:\overline{M}\otimes_{\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}\overline{M}\to\overline{\omega}^{1,2,1}
is surjective. Thus by Theorem
8.1
and the above description of
\omega_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}
we may construct a surjection
\overline{M}_{\infty}\otimes_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}\overline{M}_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow\omega_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}
, which implies that
\tau_{\overline{M}_{\infty}}
is surjective.

Now as
\varpi
is a regular element on
R_{\infty}^{B}
, and hence on
M_{\infty}
and
\omega_{R_{\infty}^{B}}
, it follows that
\tau_{\overline{M}_{\infty}}
is the mod-
\varpi
reduction of
\tau_{M_{\infty}}
(as in
[
Man21
, Section 3A]
), and so by Nakayama’s Lemma,
\tau_{M_{\infty}}
is also surjective, completing the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
∎

8.1.
Class groups

We will now recall the key properties of class groups that will be needed in the proof of Theorem
8.1
.

Fix a noetherian, normal domain
A
. We will say that a finitely generated
A
-module
M
is
reflexive
if the natural map
M\to\Hom_{A}(\Hom_{A}(M,A),A)
is an isomorphism. It is well known that if
M
is finitely generated, then
\Hom_{A}(M,A)
is always reflexive, so in fact a finitely generated
A
-module
M
is reflexive if any only if
M\cong\Hom_{A}(\Hom_{A}(M,A),A)
.

Let
K(A)
be the fraction field of
A
. We will say that the
generic rank
of an
A
-module
M
is
\dim_{K(A)}(M\otimes_{A}K(A))
.

Let
\Cl(A)
denote the set of (isomorphism classes) of finitely generated reflexive
A
-modules of generic rank
1
. Then
\Cl(A)
has the structure of an abelian group with group operation:

M+N:=\Hom_{A}(\Hom_{A}(M\otimes_{A}N,A),A).

Now further assume that
A
is Cohen–Macaulay. Let
\omega_{A}
be its dualizing module. For any finitely generated
A
-module
M
, let
M^{*}=\Hom_{A}(M,\omega_{A})
. Observe that any finitely generated maximal Cohen–Macaulay
A
-module is automatically reflexive, and hence
\omega_{A}\in\Cl(A)
. Moreover it is well known that for any
M\in\Cl(A)
,
M^{*}\in\Cl(A)
as well and
M^{*}=\omega-M
in
\Cl(A)
.

In particular,
M
is self-dual (in the sense that
M\cong M^{*}
) if and only if
2M=\omega
in
\Cl(A)
. This means that if
\Cl(A)
is
2
-torsion free, then there is at most one solution to the equation
2x=\omega
in
\Cl(A)
, and so there is at most one self-dual reflexive
A
-module of generic rank
1
.

The following two results will allow us to compute
\Cl(\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})
:

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field, and take
\Bbbk
-algebras
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
which are noetherian, normal domains, and such that each
\Spec A_{i}
is geometrically integral.

Assume that for each
i
, there is a closed subscheme
\mathcal{Z}_{i}\subseteq\Spec A_{i}
of codimension at least
1
such that each component of
\mathcal{Z}_{i}
is geometrically integral, and
(\Spec A_{i})\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z}_{i}
is isomorphic to an open affine subset of
\mathbb{A}^{d_{i}}_{\Bbbk}
for some
d_{i}
, which contains at least one point of
\mathbb{A}^{d_{i}}_{\Bbbk}(\Bbbk)
.

Then the natural map

\varphi:\Cl(A_{1})\oplus\Cl(A_{2})\oplus\cdots\oplus\Cl(A_{m})\to\Cl(A_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{2}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{m})

given by
(M_{1},M_{2},\ldots,M_{m})\mapsto M_{1}\boxtimes M_{2}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes M_{m}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

For each
i
, let
\mathcal{U}_{i}=(\Spec A_{i})\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z}_{i}
and fix some point
p_{i}\in\mathcal{U}_{i}(\Bbbk)\subseteq(\Spec A_{i})(\Bbbk)
, interpreted as a prime ideal
p_{i}\subseteq A_{i}
with
A_{i}/p_{i}=\Bbbk
. Since
\mathcal{U}_{i}
is isomorphic to a Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{d_{i}}_{\Bbbk}
,
\Spec A_{i}
is regular at
p_{i}
. It follows that if
M_{i}
is any reflexive, generic rank
1

A_{i}
-module, then
M_{i}/p_{i}M_{i}=\mathbb{F}
.

For any
j=1,\ldots,m
, let

I_{j}=p_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes p_{j-1}\boxtimes 0\boxtimes p_{j+1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes p_{m}\in\Spec(A_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{m}),

and note that
(A_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{m})/I_{j}\cong\Bbbk\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}\Bbbk\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{j}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\Bbbk\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}\Bbbk\cong A_{j}
.

Then for any
(M_{1},\ldots,M_{m})\in\Cl(A_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\Cl(A_{m})
we get that

\displaystyle\frac{M_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes M_{m}}{I_{j}(M_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes M_{m})}

\displaystyle\cong\frac{M_{1}}{p_{1}M_{1}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\frac{M_{j-1}}{p_{j-1}M_{j-1}}\boxtimes\frac{M_{j}}{0}\boxtimes\frac{M_{j+1}}{p_{j+1}M_{j+1}}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\frac{M_{m}}{p_{1}M_{m}}

\displaystyle\cong\Bbbk\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\Bbbk\boxtimes M_{j}\boxtimes\Bbbk\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes\Bbbk\cong M_{j}

as
A_{j}
-modules. It follows that
\varphi
is indeed injective.

For surjectivity, write
\mathcal{X}=\Spec(A_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{m})=\Spec(A_{1})\times_{\Bbbk}\cdots\times_{\Bbbk}\Spec(A_{m})
and let

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{j=1}^{m}\left(\mathcal{Z}_{j}\times_{\Bbbk}\prod_{i\neq j}\Spec(A_{i})\right)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}\smallsetminus\mathcal{Z}=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\mathcal{U}_{i}.

Now let
\mathcal{I}
be the set of irreducible components of
\mathcal{Z}
which have codimension
1
in
\mathcal{X}
. By
[
Har77
, Proposition II.6.5]
, there is an exact sequence

\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{I}}\xrightarrow{\psi}\Cl(\mathcal{X})\twoheadrightarrow\Cl(\mathcal{U})\to 0

where
\psi:\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{I}}\to\Cl(\mathcal{X}):=\Cl(A_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{m})
sends each
D\in\mathcal{I}
to the reflexive module associated to
D
.

But now as each
\mathcal{U}_{i}
is a Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{d_{i}}_{\Bbbk}
,
\mathcal{U}
is a Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{d_{1}+\cdots+d_{m}}_{\Bbbk}
. Hence (by
[
Har77
, Proposition II.6.5]
again), there is a surjective map
0=\Cl(\mathbb{A}^{d_{1}+\cdots+d_{m}}_{\Bbbk})\twoheadrightarrow\Cl(\mathcal{U})
, and so
\Cl(\mathcal{U})=0
, which implies that
\psi:\mathbb{Z}^{\mathcal{I}}\to\Cl(\mathcal{X})
is surjective, and so
\Cl(\mathcal{X})
is generated by the set
\{\psi(D)|D\in\mathcal{I}\}
.

But now by assumption, each irreducible component of each
\mathcal{Z}_{j}
is geometrically integral, as is each
\Spec A_{i}
. It follows that every irreducible component of
Y\subset\mathcal{Z}
must be in the form
Y=Y_{j}\times_{\Bbbk}\prod_{i\neq j}\Spec(A_{i})
for some irreducible component
Y_{j}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{j}
. It now easily follows (as
Y
will have codimension
1
in
\mathcal{X}
if and only if
Y_{j}
has codimension
1
in
\Spec A_{j}
) that for any
D\in\mathcal{I}
, its image
\psi(D)\in\Cl(\mathcal{X})
is in the image of the map

\displaystyle\Cl(A_{j})

\displaystyle\to\Cl(A_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus\Cl(A_{m})\to\Cl(\mathcal{X})=\Cl(A_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{m})

\displaystyle M_{j}

\displaystyle\mapsto(A_{1},\ldots,M_{j},\ldots,A_{j})\mapsto A_{1}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes M_{j}\boxtimes\cdots\boxtimes A_{m}

for some
j
, and so is in the image of
\varphi
. It follows that
\varphi
is indeed surjective, and hence is an isomorphism.
∎

Proposition 8.3
.

Let
\Bbbk
be a field and let
A_{1},\ldots,A_{m}
be finitely generated
graded

\Bbbk
-algebras which are noetherian normal domains.

Assume that for each
i
,
\Proj A_{j}
is a (geometrically integral) projective variety of dimension at least
3
.

Let
R=(A_{1}\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{2}\otimes_{\Bbbk}\cdots\otimes_{\Bbbk}A_{m})[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]
for some integer
s\geq 0
, and let
\widehat{R}
denote the completion of
R
as a graded
\Bbbk
-algebra.

Then if each
A_{i}
is Cohen–Macaulay, the natural map
\Cl(R)\to\Cl(\widehat{R})
given by
M\mapsto(M\otimes_{R}\widehat{R})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is simply
[
Man21
, Proposition 3.19]
, combined with the observation that (by
[
Eis95
, Exercise 18.16]
) the conditions that
A_{i}
is Cohen–Macaulay and
\dim\Proj A_{i}\geq 3
imply all of the necessary vanishing results for sheaf cohomology.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
8.1
:

Proof of Theorem
8.1
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})
be the rings from Theorems
4.21
and
4.22
, and let

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}^{B}:=\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}a}\widehat{\otimes}\left(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2})\right)^{\widehat{\otimes}b}[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}].

Then
\mathcal{R}^{B}_{\infty}
is a finitely generated graded
\mathbb{F}
-algebra, and the completion of
\mathcal{R}^{B}_{\infty}
at the irrelevant ideal is
\overline{R}^{B}_{\infty}
.

Now by Propositions
8.2
and
8.3
, using Theorems
4.21
and
4.22
and in particular the fact that
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2}))=0
and
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2}))\cong\mathbb{Z}
, we have an isomorphism

\Cl(\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})\cong\Cl(\mathcal{R}^{B}_{\infty})\cong\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2}))^{\oplus a}\oplus\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}(\st_{2,2}))^{\oplus b}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{a}.

In particular,
\Cl(\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})
is
2
-torsion free, and hence there is, up to isomorphism, at most one self-dual
\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}
-module of generic rank
1
.

Now as
\overline{M}
is a self-dual
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
-module of generic rank
1
, and
\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is Gorenstein, it follows that

\overline{M}^{\boxtimes a}\boxtimes\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})^{\boxtimes b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]]

is self-dual with generic rank
1
.

Also by Theorem
7.2
,
M_{\infty}
is a self-dual
R_{\infty}^{B}
module of generic rank
1
, and hence
\overline{M}_{\infty}
is also a self-dual
\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}
module of generic rank
1
. Thus by the above, we indeed have

\overline{M}_{\infty}\cong\overline{M}^{\boxtimes a}\boxtimes\widehat{R}_{\mathbb{F}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})^{\boxtimes b}[[t_{1},\ldots,t_{s}]],

completing the proof.
∎

Remark
.

The proof of Theorem
8.1
relied on a number of strong assumptions about the rings
\widehat{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
and
\widehat{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
, in particular that they are Cohen–Macaulay. While this was provable in the special cases needed for our main results in this paper, proving that components of local Galois deformation are Cohen–Macaulay is quite difficult in general, which would make it difficult to directly generalize this method.

However these requirements are far less necessary than may appear to be based on the above argument, and can likely be weakened significantly with a little extra work.

In particular, the Cohen–Macaulay assumption in Proposition
8.3
was needed only to ensure that certain sheaf cohomology groups vanish. The argument in
[
Man21
, Proposition 3.19]
in fact shows that without this assumption, the cokernel of the map
\Cl(R)\to\Cl(\widehat{R})
can be described explicitly as a inverse limit of certain sheaf cohomology groups, each of which is a finite dimensional
\Bbbk
-vector space. Provided that
\operatorname{char}\Bbbk\neq 2
, this implies that
\Cl(\widehat{R})[2]\cong\Cl(R)[2]
, so if our goal is simply to prove that
\Cl(\widehat{R})
is
2
-torsion free, the Cohen–Macaulay assumption is not needed. Note that in the case when
\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}
is not Cohen–Macaulay one must replace the definition of
M^{*}
by
M^{*}\cong\RHom_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}}(M,\omega^{\bullet}_{\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}})
, however as
M^{*}
is automatically concentrated in a single degree whenever
M
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay, one can still interpret
M^{*}
in terms of
\Cl(\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})
whenever
M
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay, so this is only a minor inconvenience.

The smoothness and normality assumptions can also likely be weakened to the condition that
\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}
is regular in codimension
1
. Indeed, if
(\Spec\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})^{\mathrm{sm}}
is the smooth locus of
\Spec\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}
, then
(\Spec\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})\smallsetminus(\Spec\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})^{\mathrm{sm}}
has codimension
2
. As
\overline{M}_{\infty}
is maximal Cohen–Macaulay over
\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}
,
\overline{M}_{\infty}
is uniquely determined by the restriction
\overline{M}_{\infty}|_{(\Spec\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})^{\mathrm{sm}}}
, and hence it suffices to determine the class group of
(\Spec\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})^{\mathrm{sm}}
, which is automatically normal and smooth. It is likely that a careful analysis of the situation will allow us to prove that
\Cl((\Spec\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B})^{\mathrm{sm}})
is
2
-torsion free in much greater generality than we can prove that
\overline{R}_{\infty}^{B}
itself is normal.

Part III
Computations

To prove our main results, it remains to prove Theorems
4.21
and
4.22
, which we will do in Sections
10
and
11
, by analyzing the spaces
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
and
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
.

9.
Commutative algebra preliminaries

Here we present the main commutative algebra results that will be needed for our computations in Section
10
and
11
.

9.1.
Graded algebras

Let
R
be a commutative ring. By a
graded
R
-algebra
we will always mean a commutative graded ring
A=A_{0}\oplus A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\oplus\cdots
, with
A_{0}=R
(which will then make
A
into an
R
-algebra).

We will say that the
irrelevant ideal
of
A
is the ideal
A_{+}=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}\oplus\cdots
generated by the elements of positive degree. Then
A/A_{+}\cong R
, so
A_{+}
is a prime ideal if
R
is a domain, and is a maximal ideal if
R
is a field.

Most of the rings we will consider in Sections
10
and
11
will be finitely generated graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebras. For any finitely generated graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
A
, define
A_{\mathbb{F}}:=A/\varpi A
and
A_{E}:=A[1/\varpi]
, and note that
A_{\mathbb{F}}
is a finitely generated graded
\mathbb{F}
-algebra and
A_{E}
is a finitely generated graded
E
-algebra. As it is often easier to work with algebras over a field rather than over
\mathcal{O}
, many of our arguments will works with the rings
A_{\mathbb{F}}
and
A_{E}
rather than
A
directly. In this section, we will introduce some results that allow us to deduce properties of
A
from those of
A_{\mathbb{F}}
and
A_{E}
.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
R
be a local Noetherian ring with maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{R}
. Let
A
be a finitely generated graded
R
-algebra. Consider the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{A}=\mathfrak{m}_{R}A+A_{+}
of
A
. Then:

(1)

the localization map
A\to A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
is injective;

(2)

The map
\mathfrak{P}\mapsto\mathfrak{P}_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
induces a bijection between the minimal primes of
A
and those of
A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
.

(3)

For each minimal prime
\mathfrak{P}
of
A
, the rings
A/\mathfrak{P}
and
A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}/\mathfrak{P}_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
have the same dimension.

Proof.

Take any
s\in A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{m}_{A}
, and write
s=s_{0}+s_{1}+\cdots
with
s_{i}\in A_{i}
. By the definition of
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
,
s_{0}\in A_{0}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{m}_{A}=R\smallsetminus\mathfrak{m}_{R}=R^{\times}
.

We claim that
s
is not a zero divisor in
A
. Indeed, assume that
sa=0
for some
a\in A\smallsetminus\{0\}
. Write
a=a_{0}+a_{1}+\cdots
for
a_{i}\in A_{i}
. Let
j\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
be the smallest integer with
a_{j}\neq 0
. Then

0=sa=(s_{0}+s_{1}+\cdots)(a_{j}+a_{j+1}+\cdots)=s_{0}a_{j}+(s_{1}a_{j}+s_{0}a_{j+1})+\cdots

This implies that each homogeneous piece of
sa
is equal to
0
, so
s_{0}a_{j}=0
. But now as
A_{j}
is a
R
-module and
s_{0}\in R^{\times}
, this implies that
a_{j}=s_{0}^{-1}(s_{0}a_{j})=0
in
A_{j}
, contradicting the choice of
j
. So indeed
s
is not a zero divisor.

Claims (1) and (2) immediately follow from this. Indeed, if
f/1=0
in
A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
for some
f\in A
, then
sf=0
for some
s\in A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{m}_{A}
by definition, and so
f=0
, proving (1).

For (2), the localization map
\mathfrak{P}\mapsto\mathfrak{P}_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
induces a bijection between the prime ideals of
A
contained in
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
and the prime ideals of
A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
. But any minimal prime
\mathfrak{P}
of
A
consists entirely of zero divisors in
A
, and so
\mathfrak{m}_{A}
contains all minimal primes of
A
, proving (2).

Also note that any minimal prime
\mathfrak{P}
of
A
must be homogeneous by
[
Sta24
,
Lemma 00JU
]
.

For (3), write
C=A/\mathfrak{P}
and
\mathfrak{m}_{C}=\mathfrak{m}_{A}/\mathfrak{P}
. As
C
is a domain, the map
\mathcal{O}\to C
has image either
\mathbb{F}=\mathcal{O}/\varpi
or
\mathcal{O}
. In the first case,
C
is a finite type
\mathbb{F}
-algebra which is an integral domain, and so
\dim A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}/\mathfrak{P}_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}=\dim C_{\mathfrak{m}_{C}}=\dim C=\dim A/\mathfrak{P}
by
[
Sta24
,
Lemma 00OS
]
.

In the second case,
\mathfrak{P}\cap A_{0}=0
, so as
\mathfrak{P}
is homogeneous,
\mathfrak{P}\subseteq A_{+}
. Let
\mathfrak{p}=A_{+}/\mathfrak{P}\subseteq B
. Then
\mathfrak{p}
is a prime ideal with
\mathfrak{p}\subsetneq\mathfrak{m}_{C}
and so

\dim A_{\mathfrak{m}}/\mathfrak{P}_{\mathfrak{m}}=\dim C_{\mathfrak{m}_{C}}=\height\mathfrak{m}_{C}\geq 1+\height\mathfrak{p}=1+\dim C_{\mathfrak{p}}.

But now
C_{\mathfrak{p}}
is the localization of the integral domain
C[1/\varpi]
at the maximal ideal
\mathfrak{p}[1/\varpi]
and so by
[
Sta24
,
Lemma 00OS
]
again
\dim C_{\mathfrak{p}}=\dim C[1/\varpi]\geq\dim C-1
. Combining this gives
\dim C_{\mathfrak{m}_{C}}\geq\dim C
and so
\dim C_{\mathfrak{m}_{C}}=\dim C
, again giving
\dim A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}/\mathfrak{P}_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}=\dim A/\mathfrak{P}
. This proves (3).
∎

Proposition 9.2
.

Let
A
be a finitely generated graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra. Assume that
A[1/\varpi]
and
A/\varpi A
are equidimensional of the same dimension and have the same number of minimal primes, and that
A/\varpi A
is reduced. Then
A
is flat over
\mathcal{O}
and equidimensional, and the minimal primes of
A
are in bijection with those of
A[1/\varpi]
. Furthermore if
A[1/\varpi]
is reduced then
A
is reduced as well.

Proof.

Note that all of the remaining claims follow from the claim that
A
is
\mathcal{O}
-flat. Certainly this implies that
A\subseteq A[1/\varpi]
, so if
A[1/\varpi]
is reduced then
A
is reduced. Furthermore if
\mathfrak{P}\subseteq A
is any minimal prime, then
\varpi\not\in\mathfrak{P}
, and so
\mathfrak{P}[1/\varpi]
is a minimal prime of
A[1/\varpi]
and we have
\dim A[1/\varpi]/\mathfrak{P}[1/\varpi]=\dim(A/\mathfrak{P})[1/\varpi]=\dim A/\mathfrak{P}-1
. Moreover every minimal prime of
A[1/\varpi]
can be written as
\mathfrak{P}[1/\varpi]
for a minimal prime
\mathfrak{P}
of
A
. So indeed
A
is equidimensional of dimension
\dim A[1/\varpi]+1
, and its minimal primes are in bijection with those of
A[1/\varpi]
.

So it remains to show that
A
is
\mathcal{O}
-flat, or equivalently that
\varpi
is not a zero divisor on
A
.

Let
\mathfrak{m}_{A}=\varpi A+A_{+}
be as in Lemma
9.1
and let
B=A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}
. By Lemma
9.1
(1), there is an injection
A\hookrightarrow B
, so it will suffice to prove that
\varpi
is not a zero divisor on
B
. We shall prove this via
[
Sno18
, Proposition 2.2.1]
. Note that
B
is Catenary, as it is the localization of a finitely generated
\mathcal{O}
-algebra.

Consider the finitely generated graded
\mathbb{F}
- and
E
-algebras
A_{\mathbb{F}}=A/\varpi A
and
A_{E}=A[1/\varpi]
from before. Let
A_{\mathbb{F},+}
and
A_{E,+}
be their irrelevant ideals.

We have
B/\varpi B=A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}/\varpi A_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}=(A/\varpi A)_{\mathfrak{m}_{A}}=(A_{\mathbb{F}})_{A_{\mathbb{F},+}}
. Thus by Lemma
9.1
(with
R=\mathbb{F}
) we get that
B/\varpi B
is equidimensional of dimension
\dim A_{\mathbb{F}}
and has the same number of minimal primes as
A_{\mathbb{F}}
. As as
A_{\mathbb{F}}
is reduced by assumption, its localization
B/\varpi B
is reduced as well.

Also we have a sequence of localizations
A_{E}\to B[1/\varpi]\to(A_{E})_{A_{E,+}}
, as every element of
A\smallsetminus\mathfrak{m}_{A}
is certainly a unit in
(A_{E})_{A_{E,+}}
. By Lemma
9.1
again,
(A_{E})_{A_{E,+}}
is equidimensional of dimension
\dim A_{E}
with the same number of minimal primes as
A_{E}
. But this is only possible if
B[1/\varpi]
is also equidimensional of dimension
\dim A_{E}
with the same number of minimal primes as
A_{E}
and
(A_{E})_{A_{E,+}}
.

Thus as, by assumption,
\dim A_{\mathbb{F}}=\dim A_{E}
and
A_{\mathbb{F}}
and
A_{E}
have the same number of minimal primes,
[
Sno18
, Proposition 2.2.1]
indeed applies to
B
, giving that
\varpi
is not a zero divisor on
\varpi
, completing the proof.
∎

Corollary 9.3
.

Let
A
be a finitely generated graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra. Assume that
A[1/\varpi]
and
A/\varpi A
are both domains of dimension
d
. Then
A
is domain of dimension
d+1
, which is flat over
\mathcal{O}
.

Proof.

This is simply the special case of Proposition
9.2
in which
A/\varpi A
and
A[1/\varpi]
are each reduced with a single minimal prime.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
R
be a ring and let
I,J\subseteq R
be two ideals for which
R/I
and
R/J
are both reduced. Then
I\cap J=IJ
in
R
if and only if
R/IJ
is reduced.

Proof.

Since
R/I
and
R/J
are reduced,
I
and
J
are radical ideals, i.e.
I=\sqrt{I}
and
J=\sqrt{J}
. It follows that
\sqrt{I\cap J}=\sqrt{I}\cap\sqrt{J}=I\cap J
. Now as
I\cap J\subseteq I,J
we get
(I\cap J)^{2}\subseteq IJ\subseteq I\cap J
and so
\sqrt{IJ}=\sqrt{I\cap J}=I\cap J
. Hence
I\cap J=IJ
if and only if
IJ=\sqrt{IJ}
, which indeed happens if and only if
R/IJ
is reduced.
∎

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
A
be a finitely generated graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra, and let
A_{\mathbb{F}}=A/\varpi A
and
A_{E}=A[1/\varpi]
. Assume that
\dim A_{\mathbb{F}}=\dim A_{E}
. Let
\mathfrak{p},\mathfrak{q}\subseteq A
be two homogeneous ideals. Let
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{F}},\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{F}}\subseteq A_{\mathbb{F}}
be the images of
\mathfrak{p}
and
\mathfrak{q}
in
A_{\mathbb{F}}
, and let
\mathfrak{p}_{E}=\mathfrak{p}[1/\varpi],\mathfrak{q}_{E}=\mathfrak{q}[1/\varpi]\subseteq A_{E}
. Assume that there is some integer
d\geq 0
such that for each
k=\mathbb{F},E
the following conditions are satisfied:

•

\mathfrak{p}_{k}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{k}
are distinct prime ideals of
A_{k}
of height
d
;

•

\mathfrak{p}_{k}\cap\mathfrak{q}_{k}=\mathfrak{p}_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k}
in
A_{k}
.

Then
\mathfrak{p}\cap\mathfrak{q}=\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{q}
in
A
.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.4
, it is enough to prove that the rings
C_{1}:=A/\mathfrak{p}
,
C_{2}:=A/\mathfrak{q}
and
C_{3}:=A/\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{q}
are reduced. Let
I_{1}=\mathfrak{p}
,
I_{2}=\mathfrak{q}
and
I_{3}=\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{q}
, so that
C_{i}=A/I_{i}
. Also write
I_{1,k}=\mathfrak{p}_{k}
,
I_{2,k}=\mathfrak{q}_{k}
and
I_{3,k}=\mathfrak{p}_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k}
for each
k=\mathbb{F},E
and set
C_{i,k}=A_{k}/I_{i,k}
. We claim that for each
i=1,2,3
,
C_{i,\mathbb{F}}=C_{i}/\varpi C_{i}
and
C_{i,E}=C_{i}[1/\varpi]
.

For
C_{i,\mathbb{F}}
, note that note that for each
i
,
I_{i,\mathbb{F}}=(I_{i}+\varpi A)/\varpi A
, that is,
I_{i,\mathbb{F}}
is the image of
I_{i}
under the quotient map
\pi:A\twoheadrightarrow A_{\mathbb{F}}
. For
i=1,2
this is by the definition of
\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{F}}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{F}}
. For
i=3
it follows from
\pi(I_{3})=\pi(\mathfrak{p}\mathfrak{q})=\pi(\mathfrak{p})\pi(\mathfrak{q})=\mathfrak{p}_{\mathbb{F}}\mathfrak{q}_{\mathbb{F}}=I_{3,\mathbb{F}}
. Thus

C_{i}/\varpi C_{i}=(A/I_{i})/\varpi(A/I_{i})=(A/\varpi A)/((I_{i}+\varpi A)/\varpi A)=A_{\mathbb{F}}/I_{i,\mathbb{F}}=C_{i,\mathbb{F}}.

For
C_{i,E}
, we have

C_{i}[1/\varpi]=(A/I_{i})[1/\varpi]=A[1/\varpi]/I_{i}[1/\varpi]=A_{E}/I_{i}[1/\varpi].

For
i=1,2
we have
I_{i}[1/\varpi]=I_{i,E}
by definition. For
i=3
, inverting
\varpi
in the map
\mathfrak{p}\otimes_{A}\mathfrak{q}\to A
gives
I_{3}[1/\varpi]=\mathfrak{p}[1/\varpi]\mathfrak{q}[1/\varpi]=I_{3,E}
. So indeed,
C_{i}[1/\varpi]=C_{i,E}
for each
i
.

Now take any
k=\mathbb{F},E
. By assumption
C_{1,k}=A_{k}/\mathfrak{p}_{k}
and
C_{2,k}=A_{k}/\mathfrak{p}_{k}
are both domains of dimension
d
. By Corollary
9.3
,
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are domains, and in particular are reduced. Also by Lemma
9.4
, the assumption that
\mathfrak{p}_{k}\cap\mathfrak{q}_{k}=\mathfrak{p}_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k}
implies that
C_{3,k}=A_{k}/\mathfrak{p}_{k}\mathfrak{q}_{k}
is reduced. Also by assumption
C_{3,k}
has two distinct minimal primes:
\mathfrak{p}_{k}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{k}
, and
A_{k}/\mathfrak{p}_{k}
and
A_{k}/\mathfrak{q}_{k}
both have dimension
\dim A_{\mathbb{F}}-d=\dim A_{E}-d
. So for each
k=\mathbb{F},E
,
C_{3,k}
is reduced and equidimensional of dimension
\dim A_{\mathbb{F}}-d=\dim A_{E}-d
, with exactly two minimal primes. Hence by Proposition
9.2
,
C_{3}
is reduced, completing the proof.
∎

9.2.
Class groups and dualizing modules

Let
A
be a
normal
Noetherian domain. In this section, we will present some techniques for computing the class group
\Cl(A)
and (in the case when
A
is Cohen–Macaulay) the dualizing module
\omega_{A}
of
A
.

Let
K(A)
denote the fraction field of
A
. For any divisor
D
of
A
, define the
A
-module

\mathcal{O}(D)=\{f\in K(A)|\operatorname{div}(f)+D\geq 0\}.

The assumption that
A
is normal implies that
\mathcal{O}(0)=A
. Now note that we have:

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2},\ldots,\mathfrak{p}_{n}\subseteq A
be
distinct
height
1
primes of
A
. Let
D_{i}=\Spec A/\mathfrak{p}_{i}
. Then

\mathcal{O}(-D_{1}-D_{2}-\cdots-D_{n})=\mathfrak{p}_{1}\cap\mathfrak{p}_{2}\cap\cdots\cap\mathfrak{p}_{n}.

as
A
-modules.

Proof.

As
\mathcal{D}:=D_{1}+D_{2}+\cdots+D_{n}
is an effective divisor, the condition
\operatorname{div}(f)\geq\mathcal{D}
implies that
f\in\mathcal{O}(0)=A
. Thus, using the fact that
D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{n}
are
distinct
prime divisors, we indeed get

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle=\{f\in K(A)|\operatorname{div}(f)-\mathcal{D}\geq 0\}=\{f\in K(A)|\operatorname{div}(f)\geq\mathcal{D}\}

\displaystyle=\{f\in A|\operatorname{div}(f)\geq\mathcal{D}\}=\{f\in A|\operatorname{div}(f)\geq D_{1}+D_{2}+\cdots+D_{n}\}

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\{f\in A|\operatorname{div}(f)\geq D_{i}\}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\{f\in A|\ord_{\mathfrak{p}_{i}}(f)>0\}=\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}\mathfrak{p}_{i}.

∎

Now write
\Div(A)
for the group of divisors on
A
. This is the free abelian group generated by the prime divisors on
A
. Say that two divisors
D_{1},D_{2}\in\Div(A)
are
linearly equivalent
, and write
D_{1}\sim D_{2}
if
D_{1}-D_{2}=\operatorname{div}(f)
for some
f\in K(A)^{\times}
. It is well known that
\mathcal{O}(D_{1})\cong\mathcal{O}(D_{2})
as
A
-modules if and only if
D_{1}\sim D_{2}
, and moreover that the map
D\mapsto\mathcal{O}(D)
defines an isomorphism
\Div(A)/{\sim}\cong\Cl(A)
, where as before,
\Cl(A)
is group of reflexive
A
-modules of generic rank
1
.

From now on, we will identify
\Cl(A)
with
\Div(f)/{\sim}
. For
D\in\Div(A)
, write
[D]
for the corresponding element
\mathcal{O}(D)
of
\Cl(A)
.

Now we recall a few standard results that will be needed for our computations of class groups in Sections
10
and
11
:

Lemma 9.7
.

If
A
is a noetherian UFD, then
\Cl(A)=0
.

Proof.

This follows from
[
Sta24
,
Lemma 0AFT
]
.
∎

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
A
be a noetherian normal domain, and let
x\in A\smallsetminus\{0\}
be any nonzero element. Say that

\operatorname{div}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}D_{i}

for distinct prime divisors
D_{1},\ldots,D_{n}
and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\geq 1
. Then there is an exact sequence

\mathbb{Z}^{n}/\langle(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\rangle\to\Cl(A)\to\Cl(A[1/x])\to 0

where the first map is given by
(j_{1},\ldots,j_{n})\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}j_{i}D_{i}
and the second map is given by
M\mapsto M[1/x]:=M\otimes_{A}A[1/x]
.

Proof.

This follows from a repeated application of
[
Har77
, Proposition II.6.5]
, and the fact that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}[D_{i}]=[\operatorname{div}(x)]=0
in
\Cl(A)
.
∎

Corollary 9.9
.

If
A
is a normal noetherian domain of dimension
d
, and
x\in A
is any element for which
A/(x)
is a domain of dimension
d-1
, then the map
\Cl(A)\to\Cl(A[1/x])
defined by
M\mapsto M\otimes_{A}A[1/x]
for any
M\in\Cl(A)
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is simply the special case of Lemma
9.8
with
n=1
and
a_{1}=1
.
∎

We now turn our attention to computing dualizing modules.

Lemma 9.10
.

Let
A
be a reduced Noetherian ring, and let
P_{0},P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
be the minimal primes of
A
. Then

A[P_{0}]=P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n}

as ideals of
A
.

Proof.

As
A
is reduced we have
P_{0}\cap P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n}=0
. It follows that
P_{0}(P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n})=0
and so
P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n}\subseteq A[P_{0}]
. On the other hand, if
x\in A[P_{0}]
then for any
i=1,\ldots,n
we have
xP_{0}=0\subseteq P_{i}
. Since
P_{i}
is prime and
P_{0}\not\subseteq P_{i}
, this is only possible if
x\in P_{i}
for each
i
. Thus indeed
A[P_{0}]=P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n}
.
∎

Lemma 9.11
.

Let
A
be a reduced complete local Noetherian ring, and assume that
A
is Gorenstein. Let
P_{0},P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
be the minimal primes of
A
and let
B=A/P_{0}
. Assume that
B
is Cohen–Macaulay and normal.

For each
i=1,\ldots,n
let
D_{i}=\Spec A/(P_{0}+P_{i})\subseteq\Spec B
. Assume (after reordering the
P_{i}
’s if necessary) that for some
m\leq n
,
D_{1},\ldots,D_{m}
are
distinct
prime divisors of
\Spec B
and for each
i>m
,
\dim D_{i}\leq\dim B-2
.

Then if
\omega_{B}
is the dualizing module of
B
we have

\omega_{B}\cong\mathcal{O}(-D_{1}-D_{2}-\cdots-D_{m})

and so
\omega_{B}=-[D_{1}]-\cdots-[D_{m}]
in
\Cl(B)
.

Proof.

The case
n=0
is trivial (as then
B=A
is Gorenstein and so
\omega_{B}=B
) so from now on we will assume
n>0
.

First, as
A
is Gorenstein, and hence Cohen–Macaulay, it is equidimensional and so
\dim A=\dim B
. Thus by
[
Man21
, Lemma 4.12]
and Lemma
9.10
we have

\omega_{B}\cong\Hom_{B}(B,\omega_{B})\cong\Hom_{A}(B,\omega_{A})\cong\Hom_{A}(A/P_{0},A)=A[P_{0}]=P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n}

Now let
I=(P_{0}+A[P_{0}])/P_{0}=(P_{0}+(P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n}))/P_{0}\subseteq B
be the image of
A[P_{0}]=P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n}
under the quotient map
A\twoheadrightarrow A/P_{0}=B
. Since
A[P_{0}]\cap P_{0}=0
, we have
I\cong A[P_{0}]\cong\omega_{B}
as
B
-modules.

For
i=1,\ldots,m
let
Q_{i}=(P_{0}+P_{i})/P_{0}\subseteq B
. By assumption
Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{m}
are distinct height one primes of
B
. Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(-D_{1}-\cdots-D_{m})

\displaystyle=Q_{1}\cap\cdots\cap Q_{m}

by Lemma
9.6
. So it will suffice to show that
I=Q_{1}\cap\cdots\cap Q_{m}
as ideals of
B
. Clearly
P_{0}+(P_{1}\cap\cdots\cap P_{n})\subseteq P_{0}+P_{i}
for each
i
, and so
I\subseteq Q_{1}\cap\cdots\cap Q_{m}
.

But now note that as
B
is Cohen–Macaulay,
I\cong\omega_{B}
is a maximal Cohen–Macaulay
B
-module, so
\depth(B)=\depth(I)=\dim(B)
. The exact sequence
0\to I\to B\to B/I\to 0
now implies that
\depth(B/I)\geq\dim(B)-1
. As
\dim B/I<\dim B
(since
B
is a domain and
I\neq 0
) this implies that
B/I
is a Cohen–Macaulay ring of dimension
\dim(B)-1
. In particular, it is equidimensional of dimension
\dim(B)-1
, and so every minimal prime of
B/I
is of the form
Q/I
for a height
1
prime
Q
of
B
containing
I
.

Now let
Q
be any height
1
prime of
B
, and consider the localization
(B/I)_{Q}=B_{Q}/I_{Q}
. Write
Q=Q^{\prime}/P_{0}
, for some prime
Q^{\prime}
of
A
. As
P_{0}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
we have
(A[P_{0}])_{Q^{\prime}}=A_{Q^{\prime}}[(P_{0})_{Q^{\prime}}]
. Now as
P_{0}
is a minimal prime of
A
contained in
Q^{\prime}
,
(P_{0})_{Q^{\prime}}
will be a minimal prime of
A_{Q^{\prime}}
. Moreover all minimal primes of
A_{Q^{\prime}}
are in the form
(P_{i})_{Q^{\prime}}
for some
i
satisfying
P_{i}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
. Also as
A
is reduced, the ring
A_{Q^{\prime}}
will be reduced as well, so Lemma
9.10
gives

I_{Q}=I_{Q^{\prime}}=\frac{(P_{0})_{Q^{\prime}}+A_{Q^{\prime}}[(P_{0})_{Q^{\prime}}]}{(P_{0})_{Q^{\prime}}}=\frac{(P_{0})_{Q^{\prime}}+\bigcap_{i>0,P_{i}\subseteq Q^{\prime}}(P_{i})_{Q^{\prime}}}{(P_{0})_{Q^{\prime}}}.

Now if there is no
i>0
with
P_{i}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
this gives
I_{Q}=B_{Q}
and so
(B/I)_{Q}=0
. Now assume that
P_{i}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
for some
i>0
. This implies
P_{0}+P_{i}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
and so there is a quotient map

B/Q_{i}=A/(P_{0}+P_{i})\twoheadrightarrow A/Q^{\prime}=B/Q.

As
Q
is a height
1
prime of
B
this implies
\dim(B/Q_{i})\geq\dim(B/Q)=\dim(B)-1
. By assumption this is only possible if
i\leq m
and
Q=Q_{i}
(since for
i\leq m
,
B/Q_{i}
is a domain of dimension
\dim(B)-1
). In this case,
P_{0}
and
P_{i}
are the only minimal primes of
A
contained in
Q^{\prime}
(as if
P_{i},P_{j}\subseteq Q^{\prime}
we would have
Q_{i}=Q=Q_{j}
by the above, contradicting the fact that the
Q_{i}
’s are assumed to be distinct), and so we get

I_{Q_{i}}=((P_{0})_{Q_{i}}+(P_{i})_{Q_{i}})/((P_{0})_{Q_{i}})=((P_{0}+P_{i})_{Q_{i}})/((P_{0})_{Q_{i}})=(Q_{i})_{Q_{i}}\subseteq B_{Q_{i}}.

So for any height
1
prime
Q
of
B
, we have
(B/I)_{Q}=0
, unless
Q=Q_{i}
for some
i=1,\ldots,m
, in which case we have
(B/I)_{Q_{i}}=B_{Q_{i}}/(Q_{i})_{Q_{i}}
.

From this we get that the minimal primes of
B/I
are precisely
Q_{1}/I,\ldots,Q_{m}/I
, which implies
\sqrt{I}=Q_{1}\cap\cdots\cap Q_{m}
. Also as
(B/I)_{Q_{i}}=B_{Q_{i}}/(Q_{i})_{Q_{i}}
is a field, and hence reduced, for each
i=1,\ldots,m
,
B/I
is generically reduced. Since
B/I
is Cohen–Macaulay, this implies that it is reduced, and so
I=\sqrt{I}=Q_{1}\cap\cdots\cap Q_{m}
, completing the proof.
∎

9.3.
Gröbner bases

In this subsection, we will review the properties of Gröbner bases we will need for our computations. Fix a field
\Bbbk
and a polynomial ring
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. For any
\mathbf{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}
, write
x^{\mathbf{a}}=x_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{a_{n}}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}=\{x^{\mathbf{a}}|\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}\}
be the set of all monomials in
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. For any
x^{\mathbf{a}}\in\mathfrak{M}
write
\deg x^{\mathbf{a}}=a_{1}+\cdots+a_{n}
for the total degree of
x^{a}
.

We will say that a
monomial ordering
on
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
is a total order
\succ
on
\mathfrak{M}
satisfying:

•

For any
m,p\in\mathfrak{M}
,
m\preceq mp
.

•

For any
m_{1},m_{2},p\in\mathfrak{M}
,
m_{1}\preceq m_{2}
if and only if
m_{1}p\preceq m_{2}p
.

For convenience, we will also write
0\prec m
for all monomials
m\in\mathfrak{M}

We will mainly be concerned with the following monomial ordering, called the
reverse lexicographic order with variable ordering
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
defined by:

x^{\mathbf{a}}\prec x^{\mathbf{b}}
iff
\deg x^{\mathbf{a}}<\deg x^{\mathbf{b}}
or
\deg x^{\mathbf{a}}=\deg x^{\mathbf{b}}
and
a_{i}>b_{i}
, where
i
is the largest index such that
a_{i}\neq b_{i}
.

(So for example
x_{1}x_{3}\prec x_{2}^{2}\prec x_{1}x_{2}
).

For any
\displaystyle f=\sum_{\mathbf{a}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{n}}c_{\mathbf{a}}x^{\mathbf{a}}\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
we will say the the
leading term
of
f
is
\LT(f)=c_{\mathbf{a}}x^{\mathbf{a}}
where
x^{\mathbf{a}}
is the largest monomial (with respect to
\succ
) for which
c_{\mathbf{a}}\neq 0
(and we say
\LT(0)=0
). While this depends on the choice of
\succ
, we will suppress this from our notation.

We will also let the
leading coefficient
and
leading monomial
of
f
be
\LC(f)=c_{\mathbf{a}}
and
\LM(f)=x^{\mathbf{a}}
, respectively (and again,
\LC(0)=\LM(0)=0
).

For any ideal
I\subseteq\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, we will let
\init(I)
be the ideal:

\init(I)=\left(\LT(f)\middle|f\in I\right)\subseteq\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]

(which again depends on the choice of
\succ
). We will say that a finite set
\mathscr{G}=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\}\subseteq\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
is a
Gröbner basis
(with respect to
\succ
) for
I
if
I=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{t})
and
\init(I)=(\LT(g_{1}),\ldots,\LT(g_{t}))
.

We will need the following useful criterion for identifying Gröbner bases. First, for any nonzero
g,h\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
define

\mathsf{S}(g,h)=\frac{\LT(h)}{\gcd(\LT(g),\LT(h))}g-\frac{\LT(g)}{\gcd(\LT(g),\LT(h))}h.

Given a set
