\mathscr{G}=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\}\subseteq\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
and a pair of elements
g_{i},g_{j}\in\mathscr{G}
, we say that
(g_{i},g_{j})
satisfies (
B
) (with respect to
\mathscr{G}
and
\prec
) if:

(B)

\begin{split}\text{There are polynomials $f^{(ij)}_{1},\ldots,f^{(ij)}_{t}\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]$ for which}\\
\mathsf{S}(g_{i},g_{j})=f^{(ij)}_{1}g_{1}+f^{(ij)}_{2}g_{2}+\cdots+f^{(ij)}_{t}g_{t}\\
\text{and $\LM(f^{(ij)}_{s})\LM(g_{s})\preceq\LM(\mathsf{S}(g_{i},g_{j}))$ for each $s=1,\ldots,t$}.\end{split}

Clearly
(g_{i},g_{i})
always satisfies (
B
) and
(g_{i},g_{j})
satisfies (
B
) if and only if
(g_{j},g_{i})
does.

Then we have (see
[
Eis95
, Theorem 15.8]
, as well as exercises 15.19 and 15.20):

Proposition 9.12
.

[Buchberger’s Criterion]
If
I=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{t})\subseteq\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
is an ideal, then
\mathscr{G}=\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\}
is a Gröbner basis for
I
if and only if
(g_{i},g_{j})
satisfies (
B
) for each
1\leq i<j\leq t
.

Moreover, (
B
) automatically holds whenever
\gcd(\LT(g_{i}),\LT(g_{j}))=1
.

Our computations will depend heavily on the following property of Gröbner bases (see
[
Eis95
, Theorem 15.13]
):

Proposition 9.13
.

If
\succ
is the reverse lexicographical order with the variable ordering
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
,
3
3

3

While most of the results mentioned in this section still hold with
\succ
replaced by an arbitrary monomial ordering, Proposition
9.13
specifically requires the reverse lexicographical order.
then for any
r\leq n
,
(x_{n},x_{n-1},\ldots,x_{r})
is a regular sequence for
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/I
if and only if it is a regular sequence for
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/\init(I)
.

Equivalently, if
\mathscr{G}=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{t})
is a Gröbner basis for
I
, then
(x_{n},x_{n-1},\ldots,x_{r})
is a regular sequence for
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]/I
if and only
\LT(g_{1}),\ldots,\LT(g_{t})\in\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{r-1}]
(i.e. none the leading terms of
g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}
contain any of the variables
x_{r},\ldots,x_{n}
).

The proof of Theorem
4.22
will also require us to compute the intersection of two ideals. Fortunately Gröbner bases also give us a simple way of calculating such intersections.

First, for any monomial ordering
\succ
on
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
, define a new ordering
\succ_{t}
on
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t]
by:

t^{a}x^{\mathbf{a}}\succ_{t}t^{b}x^{\mathbf{b}}
iff
a>b
or
a=b
and
x^{\mathbf{a}}\succ x^{\mathbf{b}}

(so in particular, any monomial containing
t
will be greater than any monomial not containing
t
). Then
[
Eis95
, Proposition 15.29, Excercise 15.43]
gives:

Proposition 9.14
.

Let
I=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{p})
and
J=(g_{1},\ldots,g_{q})
be two ideals of
k[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
and let
\succ
be any monomial ordering on
\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
(
\{f_{1},\ldots,f_{s}\}
and
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{t}\}
are not assumed to be Gröbner bases). Define the ideal

\mathcal{K}=(tf_{1},tf_{2},\ldots,tf_{p},(t-1)g_{1},(t-1)g_{2},\ldots,(t-1)g_{q})=\left(ta+(1-t)b\middle|a\in I,b\in J\right)\subseteq\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n},t].

If
\mathscr{G}
is a Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{K}
with respect to
\succ_{t}
then the elements of
\mathscr{G}
which do not contain the variable
t
(i.e. the set
\mathscr{G}\cap\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
) form a Gröbner basis for
I\cap J
with respect to
\succ
. In particular,
I\cap J=(\mathscr{G}\cap\Bbbk[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}])
.

10.
The space
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
4.21
. Write
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}
, so that
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}
is the quotient of
\mathcal{O}[A,B,C,D,S,T,U,V,X,Y,Z,W]
cut out by the matrix equation

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&V\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}

By Proposition
4.20
,
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}
. Let

\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{R}^{2,2}/(S+V-X-W,SV-TU-XW+YZ)

Proposition 10.1
.

\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
as subschemes of
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}
.

Proof.

By definition,
(\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2}))({\overline{E}})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{2,2}({\overline{E}})
is the set of points
(X_{1},N_{1},X_{2})\in\mathcal{N}^{2,2}({\overline{E}})
with
\tr X_{1}=\tr X_{2}
and
\det X_{1}=\det X_{2}
. Thus the equation
X_{1}N_{1}=N_{1}X_{2}
implies that
(X_{1},N_{1},X_{2})\in(\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2}))
whenever
N_{1}\in\GL_{2}({\overline{E}})\subseteq\mathcal{W}^{2,2}({\overline{E}})
. So
(\Pi^{2,2})^{-1}(\GL_{2}({\overline{E}}))\subseteq(\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2}))({\overline{E}})
.

By Proposition
4.20
,

\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(2)=\overline{(\Pi^{2,2})^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{2,2}(2))}=\overline{(\Pi^{2,2})^{-1}(\GL_{2}({\overline{E}}))},

so the claim follows as
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is Zariski closed inside
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}
.
∎

From this proposition, it follows that
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
is an irreducible component of
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
of relative dimension
8
over
\mathcal{O}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}=\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})/(S+V)
, so that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{O}[A,B,C,D,S,T,U,X,Y,Z]/\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
where
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
is the ideal cut out by the equations:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&-S\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle S^{2}+TU

\displaystyle=X^{2}+YZ.

One can easily verify that there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}[t]
given by

(A,B,C,D,S,T,U,V,X,Y,Z,W)\mapsto(A,B,C,D,t+S,T,U,t-S,t+X,Y,Z,t-X).

We shall take
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
to be the ring
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
from Theorem
4.21
(we are omitting the
\st_{2,2}
to simplify notation). By definition,
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is a graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra with irrelevant ideal
\mathcal{I}_{+}=(A,B,C,D,S,T,U,X,Y,Z)
, and the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}[t]
identifies
\Spec(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})/\mathcal{I}_{+})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with the subscheme

\left\{\left(\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix}\right)\right\}.

of
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
.

Now for any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathcal{A}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{A}
and
0_{\mathcal{A}}=(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}/\mathcal{I}_{+})\times_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{A}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{A}=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{A}
, so that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{A}=\mathcal{A}[A,B,C,D,S,T,U,X,Y,Z]/\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{A}}
. We shall prove the following:

Proposition 10.2
.

For
k=\mathbb{F},E
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is a normal Cohen–Macaulay domain of dimension
7
, which is not a complete intersection.
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is smooth away from the point
0_{k}
.

Note that this implies parts (
1
) and (
2
) of Theorem
4.21
. Indeed, Corollary
9.3
implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is a domain of relative dimension
7
over
\mathcal{O}
, which in particular implies that
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}[t]
is a domain, giving
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})
. (
1
) follows. (
2
) follows from the statement that
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}
is smooth away from
0_{\mathbb{F}}
by noting that if
x\in\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})({\overline{\mathbb{F}}})
and
R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})
is the complete local ring at
x
, then
R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})
will be regular if any only if
R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})/(\varpi)
is (as
\varpi\in\mathfrak{m}_{R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})}\smallsetminus\mathfrak{m}_{R^{2,2}_{x}(\st_{2,2})}^{2}
), and this latter ring is the complete local ring of
\mathcal{N}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})_{\mathbb{F}}
at the point
x
.

Also this implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is Cohen–Macaulay, as
\varpi
is a regular element on
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}/(\varpi)=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathbb{F}}
is Cohen–Macaulay. As
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is Cohen–Macaulay (and hence
S_{2}
) and smooth away
0_{\mathcal{O}}
(and hence
R_{1}
) Serre’s criterion implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is normal.

From now on let
k
denote either
\mathbb{F}
or
E
. Let
\succ
be the reverse lexicographical ordering on
k[A,B,C,D,S,T,U,X,Y,Z]
with variable ordering
(X,Y,Z,A,D,B,C,S,T,U)
. For ease of reading, we will underline the leading term of every polynomial under a monomial ordering.

Then we have:

Proposition 10.3
.

The polynomials

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=\underline{XA}+ZB-AS-CT,

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle=\underline{YA}-XB-BS-DT,

\displaystyle g_{3}

\displaystyle=\underline{ZD}+XC+CS-AU,

\displaystyle g_{4}

\displaystyle=\underline{XD}-YC-DS+BU,

\displaystyle g_{5}

\displaystyle=\underline{X^{2}}+YZ-S^{2}-TU

form a Gröbner basis for
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
with respect to
\succ
. In particular,
(U,T,S,C,B)
is a regular sequence for
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
.

Proof.

Note that
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{5}\}
is just the generating set for
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
given above. Hence it generates
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
, and so it remains to verify Buchberger’s Criterion (Proposition
9.12
).

By the last part of Proposition
9.12
, we do not need to check (
B
) for
(g_{1},g_{3}),(g_{2},g_{3}),(g_{2},g_{4}),(g_{2},g_{5})
or
(g_{3},g_{5})
. For the remaining
5
pairs of polynomials we have the expressions:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=Yg_{1}-Xg_{2}=\underline{X^{2}B}+YZB-YAS+XBS+XDT-YCT=-Sg_{2}+Tg_{4}+Bg_{5}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},g_{4})

\displaystyle=Dg_{1}-Ag_{4}=\underline{ZDB}+YAC-DCT-ABU=Cg_{2}+Bg_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},g_{5})

\displaystyle=Xg_{1}-Ag_{5}=-\underline{YZA}+XZB-XAS+AS^{2}-XCT+ATU=-Sg_{1}-Zg_{2}+Tg_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{3},g_{4})

\displaystyle=Xg_{3}-Zg_{4}=\underline{X^{2}C}+YZC+ZDS+XCS-XAU-ZBU=-Ug_{1}+Sg_{3}+Cg_{5}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{4},g_{5})

\displaystyle=Xg_{4}-Dg_{5}=-\underline{YZD}-XYC-XDS+DS^{2}+XBU+DTU=Ug_{2}-Yg_{3}-Sg_{4}

all of which satisfy the criterion
\LM(f^{(ij)}_{s})\LM(g_{s})\preceq\LM(\mathsf{S}(g_{i},g_{j}))
from (
B
). So indeed,
\{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5}\}
is a Gröbner basis for
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
. The last claim follows Proposition
9.13
.
∎

Now let

\mathcal{S}_{k}=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}/(U,T,S,C,B)=\frac{k[A,D,X,Y,Z]}{(AX,AY,DX,DZ,X^{2}+YZ)}

Letting
\alpha=Y-A
and
\beta=D-Z
we may rewrite this as
\mathcal{S}_{k}=k[X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta]/\mathcal{J}
where

\mathcal{J}=\bigg(X(Y+\alpha),Y(Y+\alpha),X(Z+\beta),Z(Z+\beta),X^{2}+YZ\bigg)\subseteq k[X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta]

Now let
\succ_{\mathcal{S}}
be the reverse lexicographical ordering on
k[X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta]
with variable ordering
(X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta)
.

Proposition 10.4
.

The polynomials

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\underline{X}(\underline{Y}+\alpha),

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\underline{Y}(\underline{Y}+\alpha),

\displaystyle h_{3}

\displaystyle=\underline{X}(\underline{Z}+\beta),

\displaystyle h_{4}

\displaystyle=\underline{Z}(\underline{Z}+\beta),

\displaystyle h_{5}

\displaystyle=\underline{X^{2}}+YZ

form a Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{J}
with respect to
\succ_{\mathcal{S}}
. In particular,
(\beta,\alpha)=(D-Z,Y-A)
is a regular sequence for
\mathcal{S}_{k}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathcal{J}=(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},h_{5})
, so we must verify Proposition
9.12
. By the last part of Proposition
9.12
, we do not need to check (
B
) for
(h_{1},h_{4}),(h_{2},h_{3}),(h_{2},h_{4}),(h_{2},h_{5})
or
(h_{4},h_{5})
. For the remaining
5
pairs of polynomials we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle=Yh_{1}-Xh_{2}=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{1},h_{3})

\displaystyle=Zh_{1}-Yh_{3}=\underline{XZ\alpha}-XY\beta=-\beta h_{1}+\alpha h_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{1},h_{5})

\displaystyle=Xh_{1}-Yh_{5}=-\underline{Y^{2}Z}+X^{2}\alpha=-Zh_{2}+\alpha h_{5}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{3},h_{4})

\displaystyle=Zh_{3}-Xh_{4}=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{3},h_{5})

\displaystyle=Xh_{3}-Zh_{5}=-\underline{YZ^{2}}+X^{2}\beta=-Yh_{4}+\beta h_{5}

so indeed
\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},h_{5}\}
is a Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{J}
. Again, the last claim follows by Proposition
9.13
.
∎

Combining these propositions, we get that
(U,T,S,C,B,D-Z,Y-A)
is a regular sequence for
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}_{k}
. But now

\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}}{(U,T,S,C,B,D-Z,Y-A)}=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{k}}{(\alpha,\beta)}=\frac{k[X,Y,Z]}{(XY,Y^{2},XZ,Z^{2},X^{2}+YZ)},

which clearly has dimension
0
(observe that
X^{3}=-XYZ=0
in this ring, so
X,Y
and
Z
are all nilpotent). It follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
7
.

Moreover, as the ideal
(XY,Y^{2},XZ,Z^{2},X^{2}+XY)
has
5
generators in its minimal generating set,
\mathcal{S}_{k}/(\alpha,\beta)
is not a complete intersection, and so
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}_{k}
isn’t either.

To complete the proof of Proposition
10.2
we need to show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is a domain, smooth away from
0_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}},k}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}=\mathcal{H}^{2,2}_{k}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}=(\GL_{2,k})^{2}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
treated as an algebraic group over
k
, and note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
acts on
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
via

(g,h,t)\cdot\left(\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&-S\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}\right)=\left(tg\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&-S\end{pmatrix}g^{-1};g\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}h^{-1};th\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}h^{-1}\right)

(for
g,h\in\GL_{2,k}
and
t\in\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
). One can check that the action of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
on
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})({\overline{k}})
has
13
orbits, generated by the points:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle x_{\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{2}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle z_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{2}\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\right)

for
\varepsilon,\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
. Now as
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
is a connected group variety of dimension
9
, for any
p\in(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k})({\overline{k}})
the orbit
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(p)
is irreducible of dimension
9-\dim\stab_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})}(p)
. By computing stabilizers it is now easy to compute the dimension of each of these
13
orbits (we omit the details):

Lemma 10.5
.

The dimensions of the
13
orbits of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
on
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})({\overline{k}})
are as follows:

For
p\in\{x,y,z,x_{0},x_{1}\}
the dimension of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(p)
is:

\displaystyle\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x)

\displaystyle=7,

\displaystyle\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y)

\displaystyle=6,

\displaystyle\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(z)

\displaystyle=5,

\displaystyle\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{0})

\displaystyle=4,

\displaystyle\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{1})

\displaystyle=6,

and the dimensions of the remaining
8
orbits are given in the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c|}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})&(0,0)&(0,1)&(1,0)&(1,1)\\
\hline\cr\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})&3&4&4&5\\
\hline\cr\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(z_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})&0&2&2&4\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

In particular,
\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x)=7
and all other orbits have dimension strictly smaller than
7
. Thus
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2})({\overline{k}})
has a single component of dimension
7
:
\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x)}
. As
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
7
,
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
must be equidimensional of dimension
7
, and so it follows that
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
must be irreducible and
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})({\overline{k}})=\overline{\mathcal{H}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x)}
.

Also note that the only fixed point of the action is
z_{00}=0_{k}
.

To show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is a domain, it remains to show that
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is reduced. Again as it is Cohen–Macaulay, it suffices to show it is generically reduced. Now we can compute:

Lemma 10.6
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is regular at all of the points
x,x_{\varepsilon},y,y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}},z,z_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}
, except for
z_{00}
.

Proof.

Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{5}
be as in Proposition
10.3
. Write
\vec{g}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{1}&g_{2}&g_{3}&g_{4}&g_{5}\end{pmatrix}
. Then we can compute that

\begin{pmatrix}\partial\vec{g}/\partial A\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial B\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial C\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial D\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial S\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial T\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial U\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial X\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial Y\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial Z\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}X-S&Y&-U&0&0\\
Z&-X-S&0&U&0\\
-T&0&X+S&-Y&0\\
0&-T&Z&X-S&0\\
-A&-B&C&-D&-2S\\
-C&-D&0&0&-U\\
0&0&-A&B&-T\\
A&-B&C&D&2X\\
0&A&0&-C&Z\\
B&0&D&0&Y\end{pmatrix}

One can easily verify that this matrix has rank
10-7=3
at each of the
12
points specified (and is the zero matrix at at
z_{00}
) so as
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
has codimension
10-7=3
in
\mathbb{A}^{10}_{k}
, it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is regular at the requested points.
∎

Now the action of
\mathcal{H}_{k}({\overline{k}})
on
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})
implies that if
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is regular at some point
p
, then it is regular at all points in
\mathcal{H}_{k}({\overline{k}})p
. Thus the lemma implies that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is regular at all points in
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})({\overline{k}})\smallsetminus\mathcal{H}_{k}({\overline{k}})z_{00}=(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})({\overline{k}})\smallsetminus\{0_{k}\}
. Thus
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})\smallsetminus\{0_{k}\}
is smooth.

In particular, this implies that
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is generically reduced. As
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is Cohen–Macaulay and irreducible, this implies that it is reduced, and so it is a domain.

This completes the proof of Proposition
10.2
, and so of parts (
1
) and (
2
) of Theorem
4.21
, and also shows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is Cohen–Macaulay.

At this point, one could show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is Gorenstein by directly showing that the Artinian ring
\mathcal{S}_{\mathbb{F}}
is Gorenstein. We will omit this computation, as this result also follows from part (
5
) of Theorem
4.21
. Indeed, let
\omega
be the dualizing module of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
. Since
\omega
is a generic rank
1
reflexive module, it corresponds to an element
[\omega]\in\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2})
. If
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2})=0
, it will then follow that
\omega
is free over
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
, and hence
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}
is Gorenstein, proving Theorem
4.21
(
4
).

So it remains to prove Theorem
4.21
(
3
,
5
). By Corollary
9.9
, to prove (
5
) it is enough to prove that
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})=0
for
k=\mathbb{F},E
.

Now consider the closed subscheme
\mathcal{D}_{k}=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}/(AD-BC)
of
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
.

Lemma 10.7
.

\mathcal{D}_{k}
is geometrically integral of dimension
6
.

Proof.

As
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is Cohen–Macaulay and
AD-BC
is a regular element on
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
(as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
is a domain),
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
6
. This implies that
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is equidimensional of dimension
6
, and geometrically reduced provided that it is generically geometrically reduced.

Now the action of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}=(\GL_{2,k})^{2}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
on
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}
clearly preserves
\mathcal{D}_{k}
, so
\mathcal{D}_{k}({\overline{k}})
is a union of orbits of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
. In fact, by the description of the orbits of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
above we see that
\mathcal{D}_{k}({\overline{k}})
is a union of the
10
orbits
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(z)
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(z_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})
for
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
. Of these orbits,
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y)
has dimension
6
, and the other
9
orbits all have dimension strictly smaller than
6
by Lemma
10.5
. Thus
\mathcal{D}_{k}
has a unique irreducible component of dimension
6
, namely
\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y)}
. Since
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is equidimensional of dimension
6
, this implies
\mathcal{D}_{k}=\overline{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y)}
, and so is geometrically irreducible.

Now consider the ring
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}_{k}/(AD-BC)[A^{-1}]
, and note that this is the quotient of
k[A,B,C,D,S,T,U,X,Y,Z][A^{-1}]
, given by the equations:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&-S\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle S^{2}+TU

\displaystyle=X^{2}+YZ,

\displaystyle AD=BC

These equations imply

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=A^{-1}BC,

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=X+A^{-1}CZ-A^{-1}CT,

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=A^{-1}CX+A^{-1}DZ+A^{-1}SC,

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=A^{-1}BS+A^{-1}DT+A^{-1}BX

implying that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{2,2}/(AD-BC)[A^{-1}]
is generated as a
k
-algebra by
A^{\pm 1},B,C,T,Y,Z
, and is thus a quotient of
k[A^{\pm 1},B,C,T,Y,Z]
. Since
\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}/(AD-BC)[A^{-1}]=6
, we get that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}/(AD-BC)[A^{-1}]=k[A,B,C,T,Y,Z][A^{-1}]
. As
k[A,B,C,T,Y,Z][A^{-1}]
is reduced of dimension
6
, it follows that
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is generically geometrically reduced, and hence geometrically reduced.
∎

Now it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}/(AD-BC)
is a domain of dimension
6
, so Corollary
9.9
gives that
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k})\cong\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}[1/(AD-BC)])
.

But now in the ring
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}[1/(AD-BC)]
, the matrix
\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}
is invertible. Thus the equations defining
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{k}[1/(AD-BC)]
simplify to

\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&-S\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{AD-BC}\begin{pmatrix}D&-B\\
-C&A\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&-S\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}A&B\\
C&D\end{pmatrix}

as this matrix equation clearly implies

S^{2}+TU=\det\begin{pmatrix}S&T\\
U&-S\end{pmatrix}=\det\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}=X^{2}+YZ.

Hence
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}_{k}[1/(AD-BC)]\cong k[A,B,C,D,S,T,U]\left[\frac{1}{AD-BC}\right]
, which is the localization of a UFD, and hence also a UFD. Thus
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{2,2})\cong\Cl(\mathcal{R}^{2,2}_{k}[1/(AD-BC)])=0
, proving Theorem
4.21
(
5
). Also this implies that
(\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}_{k})\smallsetminus\mathcal{D}_{k}=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{2,2}_{k}[1/(AD-BC)]
, which is isomorphic to a Zariski open subset of
\mathbb{A}^{7}_{k}
(and which obviously contains points of
\mathbb{A}^{7}_{k}(k)
), so by Lemma
10.7
,
4.21
(
3
) holds with
\mathcal{Z}^{2,2}_{k}:=\mathcal{D}_{k}
. This completes the proof of Theorem
4.21
.

11.
The space
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})

The goal of this section is to prove Theorem
4.22
. We will follow a similar approach to the one used in Section
10
.

Write
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}
, so that
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}
is the quotient of
\mathcal{O}[A,B,C,D,R,S,X,Y,Z,W]
cut out by the matrix equations

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=R\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=S\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}.

By Proposition
4.20
,
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is an irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}
. Let

\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}/(AC+BD,R+S-X-W,RS-XW+YZ).

Proposition 11.1
.

\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
as subschemes of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}
.

Proof.

The equation
AC+BD=0
, and the functions
R,S,X+W
and
XW-YZ
are all clearly preserved by the action of the group
\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}=\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\GL_{2}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
on
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}
, and so the subscheme
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\subseteq\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}
is stabilized by
\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}
.

Consider the point
x=\left(\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)\in\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}({\overline{E}})
. We have

\Delta^{1,2,1}\left(x\right)=\left(\rank\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix},\rank\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix},\rank\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}\right)=(1,1;0)

so by Proposition
4.9
we get
x\in\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}(1,1;0)
and so
\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}(1,1;0)=\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}({\overline{E}})x
. By Proposition
4.20
,
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})=\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(1,1;0)
, so

\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})=\overline{(\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}(\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}({\overline{E}})x)}=\overline{\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}({\overline{E}})(\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}(x)}.

Now
(\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}(x)
is the subscheme of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}
cut out by the equations
A=D=1,B=C=0
, so on
(\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}(x)
we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=R\begin{pmatrix}1&0\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=S\begin{pmatrix}0\\
1\end{pmatrix}.

giving
X=R,Y=0
and
W=S
. But the equations
B=C=Y=0
,
X=R
and
W=S
clearly imply the equations
AC+BD=0
,
R+S-X-W=0
and
RS-XW+YZ=0
, so we see that
(\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}(x)\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})({\overline{E}})
.

Since
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})({\overline{E}})
is stabilized by the action of
\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}({\overline{E}})
it follows that
\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}({\overline{E}})(\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}(x)\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
, so taking Zariski closures indeed gives
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
.
∎

From this proposition, it follows that
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
is an irreducible component of
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
of relative dimension
6
over
\mathcal{O}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}=\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})/(R+S)
, so that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}\cong\mathcal{O}[A,B,C,D,R,X,Y,Z]/\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
is the ideal generated by the equations:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=R\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-R\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle AC+BD

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle R^{2}

\displaystyle=X^{2}+YZ.

One can easily verify that there is an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}[t]
given by

(A,B,C,D,R,S,X,Y,Z,W)\mapsto(A,B,C,D,t+R,t-R,t+X,Y,Z,t-X)

We shall take
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
to be the ring
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
from Theorem
4.22
(we are again omitting the
\st_{2,2}
to simplify notation). By definition,
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
is a graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra with irrelevant ideal
\mathcal{I}_{+}=(A,B,C,D,R,X,Y,Z)
, and the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}[t]
identifies
\Spec(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}(\st_{2,2})/\mathcal{I}_{+})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}
with the subscheme

\left\{\left(t,\begin{pmatrix}0&0\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}t&0\\
0&t\end{pmatrix},\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix},t\right)\right\}.

of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
.

Now for any
\mathcal{O}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{\mathcal{A}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{A}
and
0_{\mathcal{A}}=(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}/\mathcal{I}_{+})\times_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{A}
. Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{A}=\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{A}
, so that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{2,2}_{A}=\mathcal{A}[A,B,C,D,R,X,Y,Z]/\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{\mathcal{A}}
. We shall prove the following:

Proposition 11.2
.

For
k=\mathbb{F},E
,
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is a normal Cohen–Macaulay domain of dimension
5
, which is not Gorenstein.
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is smooth away from the point
0_{k}
.

By the same argument as in Section
10
, this will imply parts (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) of Theorem
4.22
.

Let
\succ
be the reverse lexicographical ordering on
k[A,B,C,D,R,X,Y,Z]
with variable ordering
(X,Y,Z,A,C,B,D,R)
. Then we have:

Proposition 11.3
.

The polynomials

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=\underline{XA}+ZB-AR,

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle=\underline{YA}-XB-BR,

\displaystyle g_{3}

\displaystyle=\underline{XC}+YD+CR,

\displaystyle g_{4}

\displaystyle=\underline{ZC}-XD+DR,

\displaystyle g_{5}

\displaystyle=\underline{X^{2}}+YZ-R^{2},

\displaystyle g_{6}

\displaystyle=\underline{AC}+BD

form a Gröbner basis for
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
with respect to
\succ
. In particular,
(R,D,B)
is a regular sequence for
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
.

Proof.

Note that
\{g_{1},\ldots,g_{6}\}
is just the generating set for
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}
given above. Hence it generates
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
, and so it remains to verify Buchberger’s Criterion (Proposition
9.12
).

By the last part of Proposition
9.12
, we do not need to check (
B
) for
(g_{1},g_{4}),(g_{2},g_{3}),(g_{2},g_{4}),(g_{2},g_{5}),(g_{4},g_{5})
or
(g_{5},g_{6})
. For the remaining
9
pairs of polynomials we have the expressions:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},g_{2})

\displaystyle=Yg_{1}-Xg_{2}=\underline{X^{2}B}+YZB-YAR+XBR=-Rg_{2}+Bg_{5}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},g_{3})

\displaystyle=Cg_{1}-Ag_{3}=\underline{ZCB}-YAD-2ACR=-Dg_{2}+Bg_{4}-2Rg_{6}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},g_{5})

\displaystyle=Xg_{1}-Ag_{5}=-\underline{YZA}+XZB-XAR+AR^{2}=-Rg_{1}-Zg_{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},g_{6})

\displaystyle=Cg_{1}-Xg_{6}=\underline{ZCB}-XBD-ACR=Bg_{4}-Rg_{6}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{2},g_{6})

\displaystyle=Cg_{2}-Yg_{6}=-\underline{XCB}-YBD-BCR=-Bg_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{3},g_{4})

\displaystyle=Zg_{3}-Xg_{4}=\underline{X^{2}D}+YZD+ZCR-XDR=Rg_{4}+Dg_{5}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{3},g_{5})

\displaystyle=Xg_{3}-Cg_{5}=-\underline{YZC}+XYD+XCR+CR^{2}=Rg_{3}-Yg_{4}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{3},g_{6})

\displaystyle=Ag_{3}-Xg_{6}=\underline{YAD}-XBD+ACR=Dg_{2}+Rg_{6}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{4},g_{6})

\displaystyle=Ag_{4}-Zg_{6}=-\underline{XAD}-ZBD+ADR=-Dg_{1},

all of which satisfy the criterion
\LM(f^{(ij)}_{s})\LM(g_{s})\preceq\LM(\mathsf{S}(g_{i},g_{j}))
from (
B
). So indeed,
\{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6}\}
is a Gröbner basis for
\widetilde{\mathcal{I}}_{k}
. The last claim follows from Proposition
9.13
.
∎

Now let

\mathcal{S}_{k}=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(R,B,D)=\frac{k[A,C,X,Y,Z]}{(AX,AY,CX,CZ,AC,X^{2}+YZ)}

Letting
\alpha=Y-A
and
\beta=C-Z
we may rewrite this as
\mathcal{S}_{k}=k[X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta]/\mathcal{J}
where

\mathcal{J}=\bigg(X(Y+\alpha),Y(Y+\alpha),X(Z+\beta),Z(Z+\beta),(Y+\alpha)(Z+\beta),X^{2}+YZ\bigg)\subseteq k[X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta]

Now let
\succ_{\mathcal{S}}
be the reverse lexicographical ordering on
k[X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta]
with variable ordering
(X,Y,Z,\alpha,\beta)
.

Proposition 11.4
.

The polynomials

\displaystyle h_{1}

\displaystyle=\underline{X}(\underline{Y}+\alpha),

\displaystyle h_{2}

\displaystyle=\underline{Y}(\underline{Y}+\alpha),

\displaystyle h_{3}

\displaystyle=\underline{X}(\underline{Z}+\beta),

\displaystyle h_{4}

\displaystyle=\underline{Z}(\underline{Z}+\beta),

\displaystyle h_{5}

\displaystyle=(\underline{Y}+\alpha)(\underline{Z}+\beta),

\displaystyle h_{6}

\displaystyle=\underline{X^{2}}+YZ

form a Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{J}
with respect to
\succ_{\mathcal{S}}
. In particular,
(\beta,\alpha)=(C-Z,Y-A)
is a regular sequence for
\mathcal{S}_{k}
.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathcal{J}=(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},h_{5},h_{6})
, so we must verify Proposition
9.12
. By the last part of Proposition
9.12
, we do not need to check (
B
) for
(h_{1},h_{4}),(h_{2},h_{3}),(h_{2},h_{4}),(h_{2},h_{6}),(h_{4},h_{6})
or
\mathsf{S}(h_{5},h_{6})
. For the remaining
9
pairs of polynomials we have:

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{1},h_{2})

\displaystyle=Yh_{1}-Xh_{2}=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{1},h_{3})

\displaystyle=Zh_{1}-Yh_{3}=\underline{XZ\alpha}-XY\beta=-\beta h_{1}+\alpha h_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{1},h_{5})

\displaystyle=Zh_{1}-Xh_{5}=XZ(Y+\alpha)-X(Z+\beta)(Y+\alpha)=-\underline{X\beta}(\underline{Y}+\alpha)=-\beta h_{1}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{1},h_{6})

\displaystyle=Xh_{1}-Yh_{6}=-\underline{Y^{2}Z}+X^{2}\alpha=-Zh_{2}+\alpha h_{6}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{2},h_{5})

\displaystyle=Zh_{2}-Yh_{5}=YZ(Y+\alpha)-Y(Y+\alpha)(Z+\beta)=-\underline{Y\beta}(\underline{Y}+\alpha)=-\beta h_{2}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{3},h_{4})

\displaystyle=Zh_{3}-Xh_{4}=0

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{3},h_{5})

\displaystyle=Yh_{3}-Xh_{5}=XY(Z+\beta)-X(Z+\beta)(Y+\alpha)=-\underline{X\alpha}(\underline{Z}+\beta)=-\alpha h_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{3},h_{6})

\displaystyle=Xh_{3}-Zh_{6}=-\underline{YZ^{2}}+X^{2}\beta=-Yh_{4}+\beta h_{6}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(h_{4},h_{5})

\displaystyle=Yh_{4}-Zh_{5}=YZ(Z+\beta)-Z(Y+\alpha)(Z+\beta)=-\underline{Z\alpha}(\underline{Z}+\beta)=-\alpha h_{4}

so indeed
\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3},h_{4},h_{5},h_{6}\}
is a Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{J}
. Again, the last claim follows by Proposition
9.13
.
∎

Combining these propositions, we get that
(R,D,B,C-Z,Y-A)
is a regular sequence for
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
. But now

\frac{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}}{(R,D,B,C-Z,Y-A)}=\frac{\mathcal{S}}{(\alpha,\beta)}=\frac{k[X,Y,Z]}{(XY,Y^{2},XZ,Z^{2},YZ,X^{2}+YZ)}=\frac{k[X,Y,Z]}{(X^{2},Y^{2},Z^{2},XY,XZ,YZ)},

which clearly has dimension
0
. It follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
5
. Moreover, it is easy to check that
{k[X,Y,Z]}/{(X^{2},Y^{2},Z^{2},XY,XZ,YZ)}
is non-Gorenstein (with dualizing module
(X,Y,Z)
) and so
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is non-Gorenstein (although we will give a different proof of this below).

To complete the proof of Proposition
11.2
we need to show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is a domain, smooth away from
0_{k}
.

Let
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}=\mathcal{H}^{1,2,1}_{k}\times\mathbb{G}_{m,k}=\GL_{2,k}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m,k})^{3}
treated as an algebraic group over
k
, and note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
acts on
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
via

(g,r,s,t)\cdot\left(R;\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}\right)=\left(rR;s\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix}g^{-1};rg\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}g^{-1};tg\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}\right)

(for
g\in\GL_{2,k}
and
r,s,t\in\mathbb{G}_{m,k}
). It is easy to check that the action of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
on
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k})({\overline{k}})
has
12
orbits, generated by the points:

\displaystyle x_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(1;\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{1}&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\
0&-1\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\varepsilon_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(0;\begin{pmatrix}0&\varepsilon_{1}\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{2}\\
0\end{pmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle z_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(0;\begin{pmatrix}\varepsilon_{1}&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix};\begin{pmatrix}0\\
\varepsilon_{2}\end{pmatrix}\right)

for
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
. Now as
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
is a connected group variety of dimension
7
, for any
x\in(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k})({\overline{k}})
the orbit
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x)
is irreducible of dimension
7-\dim\stab_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})}(x)
. As in Lemma
10.5
, it is now easy to compute the dimension of each of these
12
orbits (we again omit the details):

Lemma 11.5
.

The dimensions of the
12
orbits of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
on
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k})({\overline{k}})
are given in the following table:

\begin{array}[]{c|c|c|c|c|}(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})&(0,0)&(0,1)&(1,0)&(1,1)\\
\hline\cr\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})&3&4&4&5\\
\hline\cr\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})&2&3&3&4\\
\hline\cr\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(z_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})&0&2&2&3\\
\hline\cr\end{array}

In particular,
\dim\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{11})=5
and all other orbits have dimension strictly smaller than
5
, so by the same logic as in Section
10
,
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
must be irreducible of dimension
5
and

(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k})({\overline{k}})=\overline{\mathcal{H}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{11})}
.

Again, the only fixed point of the action is
z_{00}=0_{k}
.

To show that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is a domain, it remains to show that
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is reduced. Again as it is Cohen–Macaulay, it suffices to show it is generically reduced. Now we can compute:

Lemma 11.6
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is regular at all of the points
x_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}
,
y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}
and
z_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}}
, except for
z_{00}
.

Proof.

Let
g_{1},\ldots,g_{6}
be as in Proposition
11.3
. Write
\vec{g}=\begin{pmatrix}g_{1}&g_{2}&g_{3}&g_{4}&g_{5}&g_{6}\end{pmatrix}
. Then we can compute that

\begin{pmatrix}\partial\vec{g}/\partial A\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial B\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial C\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial D\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial R\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial X\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial Y\\
\partial\vec{g}/\partial Z\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}X-R&Y&0&0&0&C\\
Z&-X-R&0&0&0&D\\
0&0&X+R&Z&0&A\\
0&0&Y&-X+R&0&B\\
-A&-B&C&D&-2R&0\\
A&-B&C&-D&2X&0\\
0&A&D&0&Z&0\\
B&0&0&C&Y&0\end{pmatrix}

One can easily verify that this matrix has rank
8-5=3
at each of the
11
points specified (and is the zero matrix at at
z_{00}
) so as
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
has codimension
8-5=3
in
\mathbb{A}^{8}_{k}
, it follows that
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is regular at the requested points.
∎

As in Section
10
, this implies that
(\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k})\smallsetminus\{0_{k}\}
is smooth, and hence it is generically reduced and so (as it is Cohen–Macaulay and irreducible) it is a domain. This completes the proof of Proposition
11.2
, and so of parts (
1
), (
2
) and (
3
) of Theorem
4.22
.

We now turn our attention to the computation of
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1})
and
\omega
. Again let
k=\mathbb{F}
or
E
. We shall first show that
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, which will again imply
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
by Corollary
9.9
.

We will use Lemma
9.8
. We first consider the localization
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]
.

Lemma 11.7
.

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]=k[A,B,D,R,Z][A^{-1}]
. In particular,
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}])=0
.

Proof.

The equations
\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}=R\begin{pmatrix}A&B\end{pmatrix}
and
AC+BD=0
imply that in
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]
we have

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle=R-A^{-1}BZ,

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle=A^{-1}B(R+X),

\displaystyle C

\displaystyle=-A^{-1}BD.

Thus
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]
can be generated by
A^{\pm 1},B,D,R
and
Z
. Moreover one can verify that these equations imply
\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}=-R\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}
and
R^{2}=X^{2}+YZ
. It follows that indeed
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]=k[A,B,D,R,Z][A^{-1}]
. As this is the localization of a UFD, it is also a UFD and so
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}])=0
.
∎

Now define the closed subschemes

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(A,B),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(C,D),

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}

\displaystyle=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(R),

of
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
. Then

Lemma 11.8
.

\mathcal{D}_{k}
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}
are all geometrically integral of dimension
4
.

Proof.

Note that by definition, the action of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}
on
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
preserves
\mathcal{D}_{k}
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}
. Hence
\mathcal{D}_{k}({\overline{k}})
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}({\overline{k}})
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\overline{k}})
are all unions of orbits of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})
. From the description of the orbits given above we see that in fact:

•

\mathcal{D}_{k}({\overline{k}})
is a union of the orbits
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{0\varepsilon_{2}}),\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y_{0\varepsilon_{2}})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(z_{0\varepsilon_{2}})
for
\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
;

•

\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}({\overline{k}})
is a union of the orbits
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{\varepsilon_{1}0}),\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y_{\varepsilon_{1}0})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(z_{\varepsilon_{1}0})
for
\varepsilon_{1}\in\{0,1\}
;

•

\mathcal{Z}_{k}({\overline{k}})
is a union of the orbits
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(x_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}_{k}({\overline{k}})(y_{\varepsilon_{1}\varepsilon_{2}})
for
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\in\{0,1\}
.

By Lemma
11.5
, each of these contains exactly one orbit of dimension
4
, and all other orbits have dimension strictly less than
4
. Thus by the same logic as in Lemma
10.7
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}
all have dimension
4
with a single
4
-dimensional geometrically irreducible component. Call these
4
-dimensional irreducible components
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\circ}
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{{}^{\prime}\circ}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\circ}
.

Now let
\mathcal{U}=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]
, which is a Zariski open subset of
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
. By the computations in Lemma
11.7
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]/(C,D)=\Spec k[A,B,D,R,Z][A^{-1}]/(-A^{-1}BD,D)

\displaystyle=\Spec k[A,B,D,R,Z][A^{-1}]/(D)=\Spec k[A,B,R,Z][A^{-1}]

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}

\displaystyle=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[A^{-1}]/(R)=\Spec k[A,B,D,R,Z][A^{-1}]/(R)=\Spec k[A,B,D,Z][A^{-1}].

In particular
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}
are both geometrically reduced of dimension
4
, which in particular implies that
\mathcal{U}
intersects
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{{}^{\prime}\circ}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\circ}
nontrivially and
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{{}^{\prime}\circ}\cap\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\circ}\cap\mathcal{U}
are reduced. In other words
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{{}^{\prime}\circ}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\circ}
are generically geometrically reduced.

An analogous computation (using
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}[C^{-1}]
) gives that
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\circ}
is also generically geometrically reduced.

It now suffices to show that
\mathcal{D}_{k}
,
\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}
are all Cohen–Macaulay. Indeed, this will imply that they are equidimensional of dimension
4
, and so they equal
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\circ}
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{{}^{\prime}\circ}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{\circ}
, respectively, all of which are irreducible. As these are also all generically geometrically reduced and Cohen–Macaulay, they are geometrically reduced, which finishes the argument.

As
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is a domain,
R
is a regular element, and so
\mathcal{Z}_{k}=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(R)
is automatically Cohen–Macaulay.

Now
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is cut out in
\Spec k[C,D,R,S,X,Y,Z,W]
by the equations
\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}=S\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}
,
RS=XW-YZ
and
R+S=X+W
.

Making the variable change
x=X-S
,
w=W-S
and substituting in
R=X+W-S=x+w+S
, these equations become
\begin{pmatrix}x&Y\\
Z&w\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\end{pmatrix}
and
xw-YZ=0
in
\Spec k[x,w,C,D,S,Y,Z]
.

It is easy to check that
\underline{xw}-YZ,\underline{xC}+YD
and
\underline{wD}+ZC
forms a Gröbner basis for the ideal defining
\mathcal{D}_{k}
with respect to the reverse lexicographical monomial order with variable ordering
(x,w,C,D,Y,Z,S)
and so Proposition
9.13
implies that
(Z,Y,S)
is regular for
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(A,B)
. Now
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(A,B,S,Y,Z)\cong k[x,w,C,D]/(xw,xC,wD)
, and it’s easy to check that
(C-D,x+w+C)
is a regular sequence for this ring. Thus
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is indeed Cohen–Macaulay. By symmetry,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}
is Cohen–Macaulay as well, completing the proof.
∎

So
\mathcal{D}_{k}
,
\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{Z}_{k}
are all prime divisors in
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
. The definition of
\mathcal{Z}_{k}
gives
\operatorname{div}(R)=\mathcal{Z}_{k}
, so
\mathcal{Z}_{k}\sim 0
. Also we have

Lemma 11.9
.

\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}/(A)
is reduced and equidimensional of dimension
4
. It has
2
irreducible components:
\mathcal{D}_{k}
, and
\mathcal{E}_{k}:=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}/(A,D,Z,R+X)
. Hence
\operatorname{div}(A)=\mathcal{D}_{k}+\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

Proof.

Lemma
11.8
implies that
\mathcal{D}_{k}
is reduced and irreducible of dimension
4
. Also, one can show that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}}/{(A,D,Z,R+X)}=k[B,C,X,Y]
and so
\mathcal{E}\cong\mathbb{A}^{4}_{k}
, which is obviously reduced and irreducible.

Write
\mathcal{F}_{k}=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}/(A)
. As
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
is Cohen–Macaulay and
A
is a regular element on
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
(as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is a domain),
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is Cohen–Macaulay of dimension
4
. This implies that
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is equidimensional of dimension
4
, and reduced provided that it is generically reduced.

By definition,
\mathcal{D}_{k}\cup\mathcal{E}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{k}
, and so
\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{k}
are both irreducible components of
\mathcal{F}_{k}
. Now take any
\mathfrak{q}\in\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(A)
, that is, a prime ideal
\mathfrak{q}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
containing
A
.

Now as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}/(A)
is the quotient of
k[B,C,D,R,X,Y,Z]
defined by the equations

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}0&B\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=R\begin{pmatrix}0&B\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}X&Y\\
Z&-X\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-R\begin{pmatrix}C\\
D\end{pmatrix},

\displaystyle BD

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle R^{2}

\displaystyle=X^{2}+YZ.

we get that
BD,BZ,BR+BX\in\mathfrak{q}
. As
\mathfrak{q}
is prime, this implies that either
B\in\mathfrak{q}
or
D,Z,R+X\in\mathfrak{q}
. In the first case,
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{D}_{k}
and in the second case,
\mathfrak{q}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. This implies that
\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{k}
are the only irreducible components
\mathcal{F}_{k}
.

Now let
\mathcal{U}_{B}=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}[B^{-1}]
and
\mathcal{U}_{D}=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}[D^{-1}]
. By the same logic as above, inverting
B
in the above equations implies that
D=Z=R+X=0
, while inverting
D
implies
B=0
. Hence
\mathcal{F}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{B}
is a closed subscheme of
\mathcal{E}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{B}
and
\mathcal{F}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{D}
is a closed subscheme of
\mathcal{D}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{D}
. As we already have
\mathcal{D}_{k},\mathcal{E}_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_{k}
as schemes this implies that
\mathcal{F}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{B}=\mathcal{E}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{B}
and
\mathcal{F}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{D}=\mathcal{D}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{D}
as schemes. Since
\mathcal{E}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{B}
and
\mathcal{D}_{k}\cap\mathcal{U}_{D}
are reduced and clearly nonempty, this implies that
\mathcal{F}_{k}
is generically reduced on each component, and so is reduced.

The statement that
\operatorname{div}(A)=\mathcal{D}_{k}+\mathcal{E}_{k}
is now immediate.
∎

As
\mathcal{D}_{k}
and
\mathcal{E}_{k}
are geometrically integral, Theorem
4.22
(
4
) now follows with
\mathcal{Z}_{k}^{1,2,1}:=\mathcal{D}_{k}\cup\mathcal{E}_{k}
.

Now by Lemmas
9.8
,
11.7
and
11.9
, there is an exact sequence
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\to\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k})\to\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[1/A])=0
and so
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
is generated by
[\mathcal{D}_{k}]
and
[\mathcal{E}_{k}]
. Moreover, as
\operatorname{div}(A)=\mathcal{D}_{k}+\mathcal{E}_{k}
,
[\mathcal{D}_{k}]=-[\mathcal{E}_{k}]
in
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
, and so
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
is generated by
[\mathcal{D}_{k}]
.

We claim that
[\mathcal{D}_{k}]
has infinite order in
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
, and hence
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Assume that
m[\mathcal{D}_{k}]=0
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then by definition, there is some
f\in K(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
with
\operatorname{div}(f)=m\mathcal{D}
(which in particular implies
\operatorname{div}(f)\geq 0
, so
f\in\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
). But this implies that
\supp f\cap\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[1/A]=\varnothing
, and so
f
is a unit in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[1/A]
. But now by Lemma
11.7
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}[1/A]^{\times}=k[A,B,D,R,Z,W][A^{-1}]^{\times}=\{uA^{x}|u\in k^{\times},x\in\mathbb{Z}\}.

But if
f=uA^{\times}
then
m\mathcal{D}_{k}+0\mathcal{E}_{k}=\operatorname{div}(f)=x\operatorname{div}(A)=x\mathcal{D}_{k}+x\mathcal{E}_{k}
and so
m=x=0
. So indeed
\Cl(\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Now define an isomorphism
i_{k}:\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathcal{D}_{k}\mapsto-1
.

Also define the isomorphism
i:\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}
to be the composition
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1})\xrightarrow{\sim}\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E})\xrightarrow{i_{E}}\mathbb{Z}
where the first map is the isomorphism
[M]\mapsto[M\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}E]
from Corollary
9.9
.

Let
\mathcal{D}=\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}/(A,B)
. From Lemma
11.9
and Corollary
9.3
it follows that
\mathcal{D}
is a prime divisor of
\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
. Note that
i(\mathcal{D})=i_{E}(\mathcal{D}\cap\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E})=i_{E}(\mathcal{D}_{E})=-1
, so
\mathcal{D}
is a generator of
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1})
.

Let
\widetilde{\omega}
be the dualizing module of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
and for
k=\mathbb{F},E
let
\widetilde{\omega}_{k}=\widetilde{\omega}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}k
, and note that this is the dualizing module of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
. To prove Theorem
4.22
(
5
) it remains to show that
i_{k}([\widetilde{\omega}_{k}])=2
(as then
i([\widetilde{\omega}])=i_{E}([\widetilde{\omega}_{E}])=2
by definition).

As
(A,B)
and
(C,D)
are prime ideals of
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
by Lemma
11.8
, Lemma
9.6
gives
\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k})=(A,B)
and
\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k})=(C,D)
.

Now as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
is a domain,

\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k})=(A,B)\cong C(A,B)=(AC,BC)=(-BD,BC)=B(C,D)\cong(C,D)=\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime})

as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
-modules, and so
[\mathcal{D}_{k}]=[\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k}]
in
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
.

Proposition 11.10
.

\widetilde{\omega}_{k}\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k})\cong\mathcal{O}(-2\mathcal{D}_{k})
and
\widetilde{\omega}\cong\mathcal{O}(-2\mathcal{D})
. If
\mathcal{M}_{k}
is a self-dual
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
-module of generic rank
1
, then
\mathcal{M}_{k}\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k})
. If
\mathcal{M}
is a self-dual
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
-module of generic rank
1
, then
\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D})

Proof.

Since the isomorphism
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1})\to\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E})
maps
[\mathcal{D}]
to
[\mathcal{D}_{E}]
,
[\mathcal{D}^{\prime}]
to
[\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{E}]
and
\omega
to
\omega_{E}
, the claims for
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
follow immediately from those for
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E}
, so it is enough to prove the claims for
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
with
k=\mathbb{F},E
.

The last claim will follow from the computation of
\omega_{k}
, as
\mathcal{M}_{k}
is self-dual if and only if
2\mathcal{M}_{k}=\widetilde{\omega}_{k}
in
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
, and there can only be one solution to this equation, as
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is
2
-torsion free.

To determine
\widetilde{\omega}_{k}
, recall the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})\cong\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}[t]
from above, and let
\omega_{k}
be the dualizing module of
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}
. Then we have
\omega_{k}/t\omega_{k}\cong\widetilde{\omega}_{k}
, so it will suffice to determine
\omega_{k}
.

Now recall that the irreducible components of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}
are in bijection with the set

\mathscr{I}^{1,2,1}=\{(0,0;0),(1,0;0),(0,1;0),(1,1;0),(1,1;0)\}.

For each
P\in\mathscr{I}^{1,2,1}
, let
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}(P)
be the irreducible component of
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}
corresponding to
P
. Then
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(1,1;0)=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})
. Now by Corollary
4.7
,
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}
is a reduced complete intersection, and in particular is Gorenstein, hence we can compute
\omega_{k}
using Lemma
9.11
.

For
P\in\mathscr{I}^{1,2,1}\smallsetminus\{(1,1;0)\}
, let
\mathcal{Y}_{P}=\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}(P)\cap\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}(1,1;0)\subseteq\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})
. To use Lemma
9.11
, we must first determine
\dim\mathcal{Y}_{P}
for each
P
. Recall that
\dim\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}=1^{2}+2^{2}+1^{2}=6

First, on
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}(0,0;0)
we have
A=B=C=D=0
, and so
\mathcal{Y}_{(0,0;0)}
is a subscheme of

\Spec\frac{\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}{(A,B,C,D)}=\Spec\frac{k[R,S,W,X,Y,Z]}{(R+S-X-W,RS-XW+YZ)}=\Spec\frac{k[R,W,X,Y,Z]}{(R(X+W-R)-XW+YZ)}

and so
\dim\mathcal{Y}_{(0,0;0)}\leq 4<5
.

Next, on
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}_{k}(0,1;0)
we have
A=B=0
, and so
\mathcal{Y}_{(0,1;0)}
is a closed subscheme of

\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})/(A,B)\cong\Spec\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}[t]/(A,B)\cong\mathcal{D}_{k}\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}.

Moreover, for any
x=(R,N_{1},X_{2},N_{2},S)\in(\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})/(A,B))({\overline{k}})
we have
N_{1}=0
by definition, so

\Pi^{1,2,1}(x)=(N_{1},N_{2})=(0,N_{2})\in\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}_{k}(0,1;0)\sqcup\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}_{k}(0,0;0).

Hence

\displaystyle\Spec\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2})}{(A,B)}\right)({\overline{k}})

\displaystyle\subseteq(\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}(\st_{2,2}))({\overline{k}})\cap\left((\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}\left(\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}_{k}(0,1;0)({\overline{k}})\sqcup\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}_{k}(0,0;0)({\overline{k}})\right)\right)

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathcal{Y}_{(0,1;0)}({\overline{k}})\cup\mathcal{Y}_{(0,0;0)}({\overline{k}})

and so
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})/(A,B)
is a subscheme of
\mathcal{Y}_{(0,1;0)}\cup\mathcal{Y}_{(0,0;0)}
. As
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})/(A,B)=\mathcal{D}_{k}\times_{k}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
is reduced and irreducible of dimension
5
, while
\dim\mathcal{Y}_{(0,0;0)}\leq 4
, this implies that
\dim\mathcal{Y}_{(0,1;0)}=5
and
\mathcal{Y}_{(0,1;0)}=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})/(A,B)=\mathcal{D}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
(where we have identified
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}[t]
).

A similar argument gives
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,0;0)}=\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})/(C,D)=\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
and
\dim\mathcal{Y}_{(1,0;0)}=5
.

Lastly consider
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}
. Take any
(R,N_{1},X_{2},N_{2},S)\in(\Pi^{1,2,1})^{-1}(\mathcal{W}^{1,2,1}_{k}(1,1;1))
. Then by definition we have
R(N_{1}N_{2})=N_{1}X_{2}N_{2}=N_{1}N_{2}S=S(N_{1}N_{2})
and
\rank(N_{1}N_{2})=1
, giving
N_{1}N_{2}\in k^{\times}
, so
R=S
. It follows that
R=S
on
\mathcal{N}^{1,2,1}(1,1;1)
, and so
R=S
on
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}
. Thus
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}
is a closed subscheme of
\Spec\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})/(R-S)
. But now the isomorphism
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})\xrightarrow{\sim}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}[t]
identifies
\mathcal{R}^{1,2,1}_{k}(\st_{2,2})/(R-S)
with
(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}/(R))[t]
(recall that
\operatorname{char}k\neq 2
) and so we get
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}\subseteq\mathcal{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
. Lemma
11.8
now implies that either
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}=\mathcal{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
or
\dim\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}<5
.
4
4

4

In fact, one can show that
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}=\mathcal{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
. We will omit this argument, as it is not needed for any of our results.

If
\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}=\mathcal{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k}
, Lemma
9.11
gives

\omega_{k}\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{Y}_{(0,1;0)}-\mathcal{Y}_{(1,0;0)}-\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)})=\mathcal{O}\left(-(\mathcal{D}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})-(\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})-(\mathcal{Z}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})\right)

and so the isomorphism
\widetilde{\omega}_{k}\cong\omega_{k}/t\omega_{k}
gives
\widetilde{\omega}_{k}\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k}-\mathcal{Z}_{k})
.

Similarly if
\dim\mathcal{Y}_{(1,1;1)}<5
then we get
\omega_{k}\cong\mathcal{O}\left(-(\mathcal{D}_{k}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})-(\mathcal{D}_{k}^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})\right)
and so
\widetilde{\omega}_{k}\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k})
.

But now as
\mathcal{Z}_{k}\sim 0
,
\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k}-\mathcal{Z}_{k})\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k})
and so in either case we get
\widetilde{\omega}_{k}\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k})
. The rest of the claims follow immediately from this, together with the fact that
\mathcal{D}_{k}\sim\mathcal{D}^{\prime}_{k}
and
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})\cong\mathbb{Z}
is generated by
[\mathcal{D}_{k}]
.
∎

This completes the proof of
4.22
(
5
). Also as
[\widetilde{\omega}_{k}]\neq 0
in
\Cl(\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1})
,
\widetilde{\omega}_{k}
is indeed not free as
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
-module, and so
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
is not Gorenstein.

Thus,
\mathcal{M}:=\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D})=(A,B)
is the unique self-dual
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
-module of generic rank
1
, and for
k=\mathbb{F},E
,
\mathcal{M}_{k}:=\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}_{k})=(A,B)=\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}}k
is the unique self-dual
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}
-module of generic rank
1
. This proves Theorem
4.22
(
6
).

As
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
is a graded
\mathcal{O}
-algebra and
\mathcal{M}=(A,B)
is a homogeneous ideal it is now clear that
\mathcal{M}/\mathcal{I}^{+}\mathcal{M}\cong\mathcal{O}A\oplus\mathcal{O}B\cong\mathcal{O}^{2}
, proving Theorem
4.22
(
7
).

We now turn our attention to Theorem
4.22
(
8
). First, Lemma
9.6
gives

\displaystyle\widetilde{\omega}

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=(A,B)\cap(C,D).

To simplify this description we will show the following:

Lemma 11.11
.

(A,B)\cap(C,D)=(A,B)(C,D)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
.

Proof.

By Proposition
9.5
it suffices to show that
(A,B)\cap(C,D)=(A,B)(C,D)
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{\mathbb{F}}
and in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{E}
(since
(A,B)
and
(C,D)
are distinct height
1
primes in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
for each
k=\mathbb{F},E
). As usual let
k
denote either
\mathbb{F}
or
E
.

Consider the polynomial ring
\mathcal{P}_{k}=k[A,B,C,D,R,X,Y,Z]
and again let
\succ
be the reverse lexicographical ordering with variable ordering
(X,Y,Z,A,C,B,D,R)
. Then
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}_{k}^{1,2,1}=\mathcal{P}_{k}/(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6})
where

\displaystyle g_{1}

\displaystyle=\underline{XA}+ZB-AR,

\displaystyle g_{2}

\displaystyle=\underline{YA}-XB-BR,

\displaystyle g_{3}

\displaystyle=\underline{XC}+YD+CR,

\displaystyle g_{4}

\displaystyle=\underline{ZC}-XD+DR,

\displaystyle g_{5}

\displaystyle=\underline{X^{2}}+YZ-R^{2},

\displaystyle g_{6}

\displaystyle=\underline{AC}+BD

as in Proposition
11.3
. Let

\displaystyle I_{k}

\displaystyle=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6},A,B)=(g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},A,B)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{k}

\displaystyle J_{k}

\displaystyle=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},g_{6},C,D)=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{5},C,D)\subseteq\mathcal{P}_{k}

so that
I_{k}/(g_{1},\ldots,g_{6})=(A,B)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
and
J_{k}/(g_{1},\ldots,g_{6})=(C,D)\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
. It will suffice to compute
I_{k}\cap J_{k}
in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, which we will do via Gröbner bases, using Proposition
9.14
.

Let
\succ_{t}
be the monomial ordering on
\mathcal{P}_{k}[t]=k[A,B,C,D,R,X,Y,Z,t]
defined in Section
9.3
and let

\mathcal{K}_{k}=\left(ta+(1-t)b\middle|a\in I_{k},b\in J_{k}\right).

Using the above generating sets, and the fact that
I_{k}\cap J_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{k}
we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}_{k}

\displaystyle=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},\underline{tA},\underline{tB},\underline{tC}-C,\underline{tD}-D).

Now we claim the set

\mathscr{G}=\{g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},\underline{tA},\underline{tB},\underline{tC}-C,\underline{tD}-D,\underline{AC},\underline{AD},\underline{CB},\underline{BD}\}

is a Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{K}_{k}
with respect to
\succ_{t}
. Clearly
\underline{AC},\underline{AD},\underline{CB},\underline{BD}\subseteq I_{k}J_{k}\subseteq I_{k}\cap J_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{K}_{k}
, so this is a generating set for
\mathcal{K}_{k}
. It remains to check (
B
) for each unordered pair of distinct elements
f,g\in\mathscr{G}
.

The proof of Proposition
11.3
showed that each pair
(g_{i},g_{j})
with
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}
satisfies (
B
) (since none of these polynomials contain the variable
t
, these computations are unaffected by the change in monomial ordering).

Next we consider the
28
pairs of distinct elements
f,g\in\{\underline{tA},\underline{tB},\underline{tC}-C,\underline{tD}-D,\underline{AC},\underline{AD},\underline{CB},\underline{BD}\}
. It is easy to check that
\mathsf{S}(f,g)=0
for all
f,g\in\{\underline{tA},\underline{tB},\underline{AC},\underline{AD},\underline{CB},\underline{BD}\}
and that
\mathsf{S}(\underline{tC}-C,\underline{tD}-D)=0
, which accounts for
\binom{6}{2}+1=16
of these pairs. Of the remaining
12
pairs, if

(f,g)=(\underline{tC}-C,\underline{AD}),(\underline{tC}-C,\underline{BD}),(\underline{tD}-D,\underline{AC}),(\underline{tD}-D,\underline{CB})

then
\gcd(\LT(f),\LT(g))=1
. The remaining
8
pairs are in the form
(tx,ty-y)
or
(xy,ty-y)
for
x\in\{A,B\}
and
y\in\{C,D\}
, which all satisfy (
B
) as

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(tx,ty-y)=\mathsf{S}(xy,ty-y)

\displaystyle=txy-x(ty-y)=xy\in\{\underline{AC},\underline{AD},\underline{CB},\underline{BD}\}\subseteq\mathscr{G}.

It remains to show that
(f,g_{i})
satisfies (
B
) for each
f\in\{\underline{tA},\underline{tB},\underline{tC}-C,\underline{tD}-D,\underline{AC},\underline{AD},\underline{CB},\underline{BD}\}
and each
i=1,2,3,4,5
. If
i=5
then
\LT(g_{5})=X^{2}
, which is relatively prime to
\LT(f)
. If
i=1,2
then
\LT(g_{i})
is relatively prime to
\LT(f)
unless
f=sA
for
s\in\{t,C,D\}
. For any such
s
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{1},sA)

\displaystyle=s(\underline{XA}+ZB-AR)-X(sA)=sZB-sAR=Z(sB)-R(sA)

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{2},sA)

\displaystyle=s(\underline{YA}-XB-BR)-Y(sA)=sXB-sBR=(\underline{X}-R)(sB)

which all satisfy (
B
) as
sA,sB\in\mathscr{G}
. Lastly if
i=3,4
then
\LT(g_{i})
is relatively prime to
\LT(f)
unless
f=\underline{tC}-C,\underline{AC}
or
\underline{CB}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{3},\underline{tC}-C)

\displaystyle=t(\underline{XC}+YD+CR)-X(\underline{tC}-C)=\underline{tYD}+tCR+XC=\underline{R}(\underline{tC}-C)+\underline{Y}(\underline{tD}-D)+g_{3}

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{4},\underline{tC}-C)

\displaystyle=t(\underline{ZC}-XD+DR)-Z(\underline{tC}-C)=-\underline{tXD}+tDR+ZC=(-\underline{X}+R)(\underline{tD}-D)+g_{4}

and for
s\in\{A,B\}
we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{3},sC)

\displaystyle=s(\underline{XC}+YD+CR)-X(sC)=sYD+sCR=Y(sD)-R(sC)

\displaystyle\mathsf{S}(g_{4},sC)

\displaystyle=s(\underline{ZC}-XD+DR)-Z(sC)=-sXD+sDR=(\underline{X}-R)(sD)

which all satisfy (
B
) (as
sC,sD\in\mathscr{G}
).

Thus by Proposition
9.12
,
\mathscr{G}
is indeed a Gröbner basis for
\mathcal{K}_{k}
.

Hence by Proposition
9.14
,
I_{k}\cap J_{k}=(g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4},g_{5},AC,AD,BC,BD)
in
\mathcal{P}_{k}
, and so in
\mathcal{R}_{k}/(g_{1},\ldots,g_{6})=\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}_{k}
we have

(A,B)\cap(C,D)=(AC,AD,BC,BD)=(A,B)(C,D),

as desired.
∎

So now

\widetilde{\omega}\cong(A,B)\cap(C,D)=(A,B)(C,D)=(AC,AD,BC,BD)=(AC,AD,BC)

(where the last equality is from
BD=-AC
in
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
).

By the above we have
\mathcal{M}=(A,B)=\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D})\cong\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}^{\prime})=(C,D)
and
(A,B)(C,D)=\mathcal{O}(-\mathcal{D}-\mathcal{D}^{\prime})\cong\widetilde{\omega}
. It follows that there exists an
\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}
-module surjection
\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}}\mathcal{M}\twoheadrightarrow\widetilde{\omega}
. By
[
Man21
, Lemma 3.4]
, this implies that
\tau_{\mathcal{M}}:\mathcal{M}\otimes_{\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{1,2,1}}\mathcal{M}\to\widetilde{\omega}
is surjective.

This completes the proof of Theorem
4.22
.
